# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE A

1. The project addresses climate change adaptation and community resilience by improving water security in Vanuatu, as a climate adaptation investment, aiming to enhance climate resilience and increase water security of rural communities through the development of district-level Water Security Workshops (WSWs) and district-level water management planning systems across Vanuatu. The approach will utilize and improve government- driven District Water Security Workshops (DWSWPs), which are a key component of the Climate Change Water Planning (CCWP) process, mandated through national policy frameworks such as the National Implementation Plan for Safe and Secure Water (NIPSSW) and the Capital Acquisition Program (CAP).

2. This project is aligned with the Vanuatu National Adaptation Plan (VANAP), the Green Climate Fund Country Program, and is fully being co-developed with the Department of Water Resources (DWR) and other stakeholders for strong local ownership. The project will be implemented in 10 priority districts across Vanuatu, targeting communities vulnerable to climate change impacts (including community-based adaptation activities). The project is fully aligned with the National Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (NCCSAP), the Disaster Risk Reduction Policy 2010-2030, National Adaptation Programme of Action, National Disaster Management Act, and the National Sustainable Development Plan 2016-2030 and the Vanuatu National Water Policy 2017-2030.

3. Observed changes in the climate of Vanuatu show there has been a gradual change in climatic conditions over the last 70 years. The temperature has increased at approximately 0.1°C per decade since 1970, and the rainfall pattern has become more erratic with an increase in extreme weather events. A significant increase is observed or projected; extreme rainfall and tropical cyclone events are predicted to become more intense. These changes compound common water sanitation services and resource management challenges.

4. More intense extreme events will increase the incidence of flooding and induce greater damage to water infrastructure, including dams, reservoirs, and pipelines. Flooding can cause structural failure in these assets; damages can result in redundant infrastructure, cutting communities off from water supplies. Flooding events impact the quality of drinking water by contaminating it with sediments, organic matter, and pathogens, leading to illness and wellbeing of the Vanuatu population at risk. On the other hand, increased temperatures and increased intensity of droughts will lead to reduced rainfall, increasing the likelihood of prolonged periods of drought. Consequently, climate impacts directly threaten water security and sanitation across the country.

5. Compounding the negative impacts of climate change is restricted technical, institutional and financial capacity barriers related to water governance: Limited technical capacity, human resources, and financing at area council and provincial level to develop climate-resilient water systems. Limited technical knowledge, human resources, and financing at provincial and national levels to establish and maintain climate-resilient water systems. Insufficient awareness, knowledge, and skills on climate change issues and climate-resilient water management planning.

6. In 2020, the DWR, in association with UNICEF, conducted an inventory of water systems, as part of a Water and Sanitation Sector Strengthening Programme. Only 75% of systems were identified as functional and only 37% of these are considered climate-resilient.

7. The project will address these barriers by implementing Three distinct but mutually reinforcing Outcomes:

* **Outcome 1:** Communities are empowered to plan and manage climate-resilient water resources.
    * Activities will boost technical capacities across national extension structures to support climate-resilient water management planning, including the development of district-level Water Security Workshops (DWSWPs) that are induced facilitators.
* **Outcome 2:** Communities have enhanced climate-resilient rural water infrastructure.
    * The project will provide financial assistance for small-scale infrastructure investments and increase financial flows to enhanced DWSWPSs that require financial assistance to construct necessary resilient water infrastructure systems.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 4

## Outcomes:
### A Provincial and national institutions are strengthened to address climate risks associated with water security.
Activities will focus on capacity building across national and provincial levels to boost DWRH and WaSH sector partners' technical capacity concerning climate-resilient water management. Further, the Outcome will enhance evidence-based knowledge transfer in water resources and sanitation/water management processes. This will enhance evidence-based knowledge transfer and raise awareness of climate-resilient water management.

### B Ultimately, the combined impact of these Outcomes will induce a paradigm shift in Vanuatu's water management systems. It will catalyse a shift towards safe, climate-resilient, and sustainable water utilization and improved water security at community levels.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

9. The climate context presented below is the presented in the following sub-sections: adaptation context, vulnerability, climate data analysis (observed historical change and projected climate change), risks and impacts of climate change.

10. Adaptation context: Vanuatu is Small Island Developing State comprising 83 islands, 65 of which are inhabited, extending over 1,000 km in the Western Pacific between the equator and the tropic of Capricorn. The country lies to the east of Papua New Guinea, to the south-east of Fiji, to the north-east of New Caledonia, and to the west of Fiji. Port Vila is the island of Efate, is the capital of the country. Vanuatu has a federal system of government with three levels of government: national (responsible for promoting regional autonomy). There are three municipal councils for the state of Efate, one for the state of Tafea and one for the state of Shefa. The 65 inhabited islands have been divided into Area Council administrative units, with Area Administrators, who have devolved mandates from the District.

11. The Vanuatu National Statistics Office (VNSO) estimated the 2021 population to be at 314,600. The country is highly homogeneous – 99% of its population are the indigenous, Melanesian ni-Vanuatu people. Port Vila has a population of approximately 85,000 and is the largest city in Vanuatu and capital region - account for about21% of the total population. There are over 100 languages and dialects, which makes it one of the most linguistically diverse countries in the world. There are three official languages: Bislama, English and French.

12. Vanuatu's economy is characteristic of small Pacific Island nations: small in scale, remote from major markets, heavily dependent on external trade and aid, with a limited domestic market for goods and services; public administration, lower than the return on market activities and narrow the feasible set of economic opportunities. The country has a high level of dependence on imports to meet its basic needs. This dependence makes it vulnerable to external shocks, especially climate change-induced disasters and natural resource depletion. Vanuatu is highly dependent on agriculture for food security, as well as timber, food energy and water security - the latter of which this project sets out to address directly.

13. Water resources in Vanuatu: The islands in the archipelago are heterogenous in geographic, topographic and climatic characteristics. They range from low-lying coastal areas with good groundwater and surface water availability to high mountainous terrain where either surface or water is very limited and subject to seasonal enrichment. However, bed catchments and other sources of water are available throughout the islands and are affected by these climatic and geographic factors. Water resources in the country, therefore, vary and are influenced by these climatic and geographic factors.

14. At the same time, the island is prone to multivariable water-related climate risks coupled with a high level of vulnerability. The most significant of which include: droughts; floods; cyclones along the coasts, the balance of freshwater and saltwater ecosystems also plays a vital role in the sustainability of the islands' food security. Vanuatu's population is highly vulnerable to these impacts due to its small scale, lack of storage and limited freshwater resources - are increasingly exposed to climate change-induced disasters, particularly devastating natural resource-depleting livelihoods of rural communities (75% of the population), who continue to rely on subsistence farming (particularly water access) in the different islands.


---
**Page 10**
---

15\. Vulnerability analysis: Vanuatu’s climate profile is closely linked with, and is one of the important determinants of its performance of socioeconomic and political indicators in the country.

16\. More than 10% of Vanuatu's population lives in extreme poverty, compared to the regional average of 39%. The majority of these people live on less than $2 per day. These communities are disproportionately collectively host over 90% of people in southern Pacific Annex 2 – the Feasibility Study and Analysis for Vulnerability Assessment and Career Action Plan expose hardship, poverty and other indicators in greater detail.

17\. Despite producing insignificant greenhouse gas emissions that cause climate change, SIDS populations (particularly women) are disproportionately affected by the impacts of climate change. Vanuatu is one of the most climate-sensitive districts. Child mortality, for example has fallen in Vanuatu, but remains high compared to other Pacific Islands. The country’s vulnerability to climate change is exacerbated by some of the leading contributors to enteric and other infectious disease burdens, which remains high in Vanuatu.

18\. Vulnerabilities, in terms of limited climate resilience and water insecurity also encompass gendered inequalities and social inequities. These include:

• Different gender groups, particularly women and girls, have gendered needs, which remain under-recognised by policy makers and are often not adequately addressed during natural disasters or emergencies.

• Women’s voices may be undermined in water security and climate adaptation-related decision-making bodies, despite registering better performance when represented;

• Inequalities between men and women are exacerbated by climate risks; increasing time poverty among certain gender groups (women) and children primarily collect water for domestic use;

• Since water is central to well-being, water insecurity indirectly and directly contributes to exacerbate gender inequalities in other sectors such as limited land rights and limit adoption of adaptation and resilience strategies.

19\. This socioeconomic context informs the primary challenges for water safety and security in Vanuatu, which are:

• The lack of access to safe drinking water (60% of households have no access to a safe drinking water source and 85% access on the premise)14; the safety and reliability of the water consumed by the population is also a concern. In addition, there has been a decline in the quality of the indigenous ni-Vanuatu: unsafe drinking water can lead to diarrhoea and other water-borne illnesses which are common among children under five years old. These diseases are often chronic and contribute to chronic undernutrition. Climate change is a threat multiplier in this context.

20\. In terms of sanitation, the household living conditions analysis of the 2020 Census reveals that outside urban areas, only about 15% of households have access to improved sanitation facilities (i.e., flush toilets or latrines and no latrine at all). This is particularly true in Penama where between 83–100% of Ni-Vanuatu households do not have access to any form of improved sanitation. The lack of access to sanitation, both for household use and sanitation, and water shortages are common during the dry season which lasts from May through September. In addition, there is a lack of piped water supply systems that are not available or have inadequate yards during the dry season. Meanwhile, rainfall patterns have been affected by climate change with more frequent periods of extreme rainfall leading to floods that further exacerbate the WASH baseline context.

The following subsections detail the observed and projected climate change in Vanuatu, and how these will impact water safety and security.


21\. Observed historical trends and climate change: Vanuatu’s climate varies from wet tropical in the north sub-tropical in the south. From May through September, eastern-south easterly winds supply few sunny days but cold temperatures, heavy rain and occasional cyclones. The wettest months are from January through March.

### Precipitation – Rainfall is also affected by latitude and altitude in the islands.

The northern higher islands in the Banks and Torres groups receive an annual average of 4,000 mm rainfall, while the southern and lower islands receive less than half that amount (1,500 mm). Data for the southern islands are limited and not always available. The World Bank Climate Change Knowledge Portal states that a study has pointed to significant changes in precipitation patterns over time (the specific period of time is not specified within the document). Observing records over 400 years, it shows abrupt changes of +1,800 mm can occur between wet and dry periods. These changes are considered outside the range of normal inter-annual variation and have been documented and linked to human-induced climate changes.

### Extreme winds and Tropical Cyclones – November to April is also the tropical cyclone season, which impacts the water sector extensively. The geographical location of the archipelago in the southwest Pacific Ocean makes it vulnerable to extreme weather events such as tropical cyclones with wind speeds over 62 km/h or more. According to the Vanuatu Meteorological and Geo-Hazard Department statistics, the area of Vanuatu is affected by at least one tropical cyclone per year on average. The most intense occurrence of these events is in January and February. On average, Vanuatu, and its marine economic zone experience 10-20 tropical cyclones annually. These extreme weather events can affect any island of Vanuatu, with several impacts: extreme winds heavy rainfall, flash flooding, flooding, landslides, storm surges, and tsunamis. The effects are felt on the land and in the coastal seas. These events regularly cause damage to life, infrastructure and public goods, as well as property in the country. Since 2015 two category 5 (>300km/hour) Cyclones have directly passed over Vanuatu causing extensive damage.

- **Cyclone Harold**, 2020: 160 000 people, more than half of the total population, required assistance. On April 4th, Cyclone Harold made landfall in the northern part of the country, affecting the Province of Sanma and the Province of Tafea.
- **Cyclone Pam**, 2015: displacing about 60 000 people and resulting in USD 600 million in economic losses.

### Drought – In Vanuatu, drought is frequently associated with El Niño Phases of the ENSO cycle, that causes a warm phase during which there are increased temperatures and decreased rainfall. This leads to reduced crop yields, increasing runoff levels and inducing meteorological drought. Whilst there is uncertainty on the occurrence of El Niño phases in the future, it has been observed that Vanuatu experienced four extreme events since 1901. Four out of five extreme events since 1901 have occurred since 1970. This was most recently exemplified in the 2015/16 El Nino event that caused severe drought in Vanuatu which

**comparatively negative impacts of the aftermath of Cyclone Pam that devastated infrastructures across the country**. The drought caused severe water security impacts resulting in increased soil aridity and crop loss, forcing many farmers to abandon their crops (yams and taro) failed, forcing 90,000 people (about 30% of the total population) to rely on food aid[1].

## Sea-level rise – According to the Pacific Climate Change Science Program[2], as ocean water warms, it expands and also rises due to thermal expansion. The rate of sea level rise in Vanuatu is higher than global average rates of 3-8 mm per year. It is projected that sea levels will continue to rise faster than the global average of 2.3–5.8 mm per year. This higher rate of rise may be partly related to natural fluctuations in ocean water levels year to year or decide caused by phenomena such as the El Niño Southern Oscillation.

### Projected climate change: Vanuatu was ranked as being at the highest risk level in the 2019 World Risk Report[3]. This makes it one of the most vulnerable countries to climate change and extreme weather events, ranking among the top five most vulnerable countries in the world. For project preparation, therefore, it was crucial to conduct a climate modeling study for Vanuatu to understand trends, and ensure that these were considered into the design of delivery of this project paradigm.

For this climate study (which is included in Annex 2 – Project Brief[4]), quality data were analyzed based on the following criteria: spatial coverage, temporal coverage, and quality. In particular, analysis was carried out for baselines and the year 2050, under RCP2.6 and RCP8.5 scenarios, applying CMIP5 models. The climate model outputs were then compared with other available climate data sources, with outputs for the median, 5th and 95th percentiles. Due to the timing of the project’s preparation, the climate modeling study was not able to include all the latest climate projections consistent with CMIP6 scenarios and the findings of IPCC AR6.

Ten populated locations in Vanuatu were selected as representative sites for the analysis: Port Vila, Luganville, Norup, Goro Bay, Isangel, Sola, Lakatoro, Melissi, Lamap, and Anghewahol. These are shown below:

| Figure 2: Selected sites for climate change scenario analysis in Vanuatu |

23. **Temperature** - The climate modeling study revealed that climate change has manifested in the historical environmental data collected by the meteorological stations with an increasing temperature trend. Projections indicate that mean annual temperature showed increasing trends in all the selected stations. The projections indicate an approximate increase in temperature by 1°C across the locations under RCP4.5 against the baseline.

Table B: Baseline and projected values of monthly mean temperature by location

**Page 16**
---

24. **Precipitation** – data show no significant predicted change in the trend of annual rainfall. Percentage of precipitation events that are terminated at 0 mm with a probability of 1% to 15% under 2005 projections, relative to a 1986-2005 baseline. Despite this, the variability of individual rainfall events continues to increase. The frequency and intensity of extreme precipitation events is expected to increase. Daily extreme precipitation was calculated based on the six meteorological stations in the Vautumu region. The probability that an event will be greater than 10, 25, 50, 100, 200, 500 years is as follows: 97%, 84%, 63%, 40%, 20%, and 10% respectively. The extreme rainfall events are expected to produce an average of 6.5% more precipitation by 2050 under RCP4.5, compared with the baseline period (1986-2005). This is equivalent to a 73% increase in the probability that daily precipitation will be as high as 77.5% higher than baseline observations, indicating individual events over a season or year could be more intense and frequent. The frequency of extreme rainfall events is also correlated with predicted increases in Tropical Cyclone (TC) intensity and rainfall (see paragraph 26). For more information see Annex A.

25. **Extreme wind** – wind speed in all the Vautumu locations is very high, and under climate change scenarios, the median change indicates an increase in intensity. Based on the GARP-1 data, the 100-year return period for extreme wind speeds (at a height of 10 m) will be reduced from approximately 240–260 km/h, which are in the range of category five (253 km/h) of the Saffir-Simpson Scale. Under climate change scenarios, this is expected to increase by up to 7% for the ensemble median and reach more than 10% in the 95th percentile. This is also associated with a greater predicted increase in TC intensity (see paragraph 26). Annex A delineates on the outputs regarding wind speed from the climate data analysis.

26. **Tropical Cyclones** – the occurrence of and prediction of TCs is very hard to predict under current climate conditions, but there are some indications that they will become more intense in the future. The El Niño Southern Oscillation phases. TC tracking is also unpredictable, and it is not possible to accurately predict (at present) where a TC will make landfall or how long it will remain over an area of interest. The travel regional analysis is applicable to predicting overall trends in TCs. Analyses at the regional level indicate:

- High confidence in the increase in Average TC Intensity (category 4). This correlates with the information presented in paragraph 25 that indicates that extreme wind speeds are predicted to increase.
- Medium to high confidence in an increase in TC rainfall rates. This correlates with the information presented in paragraph 25 and the fact that extreme precipitation is expected to increase.
- High confidence that TC frequency will decrease over the coming century but there is low confidence that Category 4 and 5 events will change. This correlates with Figure 3 that indicates that TC frequency is predicted to decrease, while category 4 and 5 events are not.
- High confidence that sea level rise will increase TC-related storm surge events.
- In summary, the climate data analysis presented in paragraph 25 has indicated that the global proportion of major (Category 5) TC occurrence has increased over the last four decades (IPCC AR6). A further increase is expected as a result of increasing precipitation causing greater risk of loss and damages and associated impacts as listed below[1][2].

### Risks and impacts: Water remains the main channel through which climate change will be felt in Vanuatu and remains the key sector for developing successful adaptation strategies. This is because changes to rainfall patterns, sea-level rise, and increased frequency of extreme weather events will impact the water cycle in the equatorial Pacific, which is already exposed to elevated occurrences of natural hazards such as cyclones, droughts, and floods.

The analysis shows that climate variables, which can impact water access, supply and security, will continue to increase uncertainty over time.

# Table 5: Climate and non-climate drivers impacting drinking water and WASH infrastructure in Vanuatu

| Phenomenon | Type of driver | Risks and impacts for drinking water and WASH infrastructure |
|---|---|---|
| **Increased air and water temperatures** | Climate change | T1. Reductions in catchment water sources due to increased evapotranspiration – which will impact water points and sources that are dependent on rainfall. <br> T2. Increase in water treatment costs as quality of inflow increases, due to lack of sediment removal and increase in biological activity. <br> T3. Increased occurrence of algal blooms and toxic algae blooms is more likely with increased temperatures – which will impact the quality as well as the quantity and chemistry of water - these will impact both quality as well as the quantity of water available. |
| **Increased intensity of extreme precipitation events** | Climate change | T1. Reductions in catchment water sources due to accelerated conditions that increase biomass and higher levels evapotranspiration – which, in turn will reduce the amount of rainfall reaching groundwaters and surface waters access; <br> T2. Increase in operations, maintenance and repair costs for distribution networks as saline increases - which will particularly affect vulnerable communities with limited access to water services, low household income and/or cannot access government/other funds to maintain their systems. |
| **Extreme wind (cyclone) tropical cyclones** | Natural driver (as is in the system) | E1. ## Climate change driver (increasing intensity of TCs)

E3. These storms often initiate or enhance damage to structures, which are then worsened by extreme precipitation – this can particularly affect coastal communities and infrastructure such as roads, bridges, and buildings. These events also cause increased sedimentation in waterways, which clogs pipes (gutters, downspouts, screens) that are usually exposed.

E4. These storms also cause extensive damage and, in coastal areas, cause coastal flooding and erosion of beaches and dunes. This can lead to increased salinity intrusion into groundwater aquifers, which can increase salinization and impact upon both water access and health burdens of communities.

E5. Even with climate-proofing, it is essential to note that some extreme weather events are likely to occur in the future. Therefore, it is important for adaptation planning and infrastructure design[12], particularly in the coastal areas, to consider these extreme weather events as well as other natural and climate-induced hazards.

## Drought and decreased precipitation volumes, seasonal patterns changes

D1. Reduced rainfall can lead to increased salinity intrusion into groundwater aquifers and pollution concentrations – which, in turn, impacts availability and quality of water supplies (access to freshwater source points will reduce, and the cost of water production for potable use will increase). This will have direct impacts on water security as well as health burdens of communities.

D2. Drought can also cause increased salinity intrusion into estuaries and aquifers during drought season, causing imbalance between freshwater and saltwater in coastal areas. This can lead to increased salinity intrusion into groundwater aquifers which can increase salinization and impact upon both water access and health burdens of communities as well as expose them to health hazards caused by contaminated water.

## Sea-level rise

Climate change driver

S1. Saline intrusions and increased salinity of groundwater aquifers and coastal soils will have direct impacts on the availability and quality of water supplies (access to freshwater source points will reduce, and the cost of water production for potable use will increase). This will have direct impacts on water security as well as health burdens of communities.

S2. Saline intrusions and increased salinity of groundwater will have direct impacts on the availability and quality of water supplies (access to freshwater source points will reduce, and the cost of water production for potable use will increase). This will have direct impacts on water security as well as health burdens of communities. The costs associated with maintaining or replacing existing infrastructure due to lower quality water – this will particularly affect coastal areas of the country, driving up the costs of water production for potable use.

S3. Increased salinity intrusion into estuaries and aquifers during drought season can lead to increased salinity intrusion into groundwater aquifers which can increase salinization and impact upon both water access and health burdens of communities as well as expose them to health hazards caused by contaminated water.

## Deforestation

Anthropogenic driver

DF1. Deforestation can reduce communities' access to clean drinking water, by decreasing water yield.

## Water pollution and other environmental pollution (Anthropogenic driver)

### WP1: Water pollution occurs when harmful substances—often chemicals or microorganisms—enter water bodies, such as rivers, lakes, and oceans,
and other bodies of water, degrading water quality and rendering it toxic to human health.

### WP2: Water pollution can happen through point source pollution –
such as industrial discharge from factories, sewage treatment plants, or agricultural runoff. It can also occur through non-point source pollution—
like the runoff of fertilizers and pesticides from farms or the disposal of solid waste in landfills.

### WP3: Another environmental problem that can also find its way into water outlets, streams and water bodies.
Plastic and other anthropogenic debris are often found in waterways due to poor practices. Waste can often find its way into our water sources,
thereby impacting human health and the environment.

This is because water is very vulnerable to pollution. Known as a "universal solvent," it dissolves many substances, including salt, sugar, and even oil on earth.

### B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max 1000 words, approximately 2 pages plus citations)

This section presents the barrier analysis and the project's proposed theory of change to address these barriers.

**Barrier analysis**

4. Barriers for this project have been identified through a review of primary and secondary sources, as well as through a DRRWV stakeholder engagement process. Primary sources include Vanuatu’s draft GCF Country Programme Document (CPD) 2019-2023, which is currently under consultation with stakeholders at the national and provincial level; and workshops conducted during the concept note stage, as well as during the full project proposal stage.

5. The barriers were identified through a review of collected data from different climate sector and development sector actors, focusing on climate change issues in the Pacific SIDS, which are also relevant to Vanuatu. This includes the 2018 Climate Change Vulnerability Assessment baseline. Additionally, post-disaster assessments on Cyclone Pam (2015) and Cyclone Harold (2020) were reviewed as well as lessons learned from the GCF-funded project in Solomon Islands. Data was also available located studies on Samoa, Tuvalu and Tonga. The main barriers to climate change adaptation at the sectoral level in Vanuatu include:

### Barrier 1 - Capacity for planning and capacity – this is limited at community levels due to a lack of backlogging and outreach from extension agents to adequately design climate resilient DRRWV projects. This is further exacerbated by the lack of institutional capacity to provide sustainable water and sanitation services that can extend to external shocks and processes of change, particularly in rural areas where there are no established institutions or infrastructure for water provision to the WASH sector, but it is the infrastructure and the capacity of communities to manage their systems which will determine the extent of climate impacts and increased water security in Vanuatu or prove to be a hurdle in achieving the project goals.

Capacity barriers for climate-resilient WASH in Vanuatu, at local and community levels, have two features:

* Full project proposal under development; concept approved in 2020 https://www.thegcf.org/projects-operations/project-33712
* Increase resilience vulnerable and marginalized communities.pdf

**Page 22**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 18

B

* Increased access to evidence-based planning among communities for climate-resilient water management; and,
* Limited capacity, knowledge, and skills on climate change issues and climate-resilient water resource management at the community level.

Stakeholder consultations highlighted these observations: Red Cross Vanuatu (RCV) identified limited capacity to provide basic services at remote locations. The lack of infrastructure, particularly in rural areas, is a key gap. World Vision (WV), similarly identifies that at the community level, there are no water supply and project intervention needed to support design capacities, as well as be deployed quickly and efficiently.

**Outcome 1 will address this barrier – section B.3 below provides relevant details.**

## Barrier 2 - Limited market signals and insufficient CR WASH Infrastructure - Water is generally perceived as a public good in rural and remote settings in rural Vanuatu, and existing WASH infrastructure is often underutilized or not maintained.

In rural settings, water is a public good – although climatic and environmental conditions as well as reliance on rainwater harvesting systems are challenging. The lack of market signals for water services, particularly in these rural (often remote) areas, the absence of market signals such as prices and permits to access water sources, and the limited availability of alternative water supplies, all contribute to overutilization through the limited water source points currently available. This informed the establishment of the Water Sector Development Strategy (WSDS), which was developed in 2015 by the Department of Water Supply and Sewerage (DWSS) and the Ministry of Health and Medical Services (MHMS). The WSDS is a key policy document that will guide the sector’s development over the next five years. It aims to address water supply challenges, including those related to climate change, resulting in increasing temperatures globally and in Vanuatu, changes in precipitation patterns, and increased frequency of extreme weather events such as flooding and droughts threatening water security, health, and the environment.

One of the primary barriers in Vanuatu is limited access to piped water supplies. The DWSS Cluster has identified that only 37% of households have a piped water supply (as part of the Water Sector Development Strategy Strengthening Programme – which found only 75% of systems functional). Shea Province, which hosts the capital city Port Vila and is the most populous province in Vanuatu, had 100% of its systems functioning. However, in the northernmost province in the north, had only 72% of systems functioning. It is also important to note that, even operating at a lower capacity than originally designed, these systems are still providing water services.

Data collected from the Water Sector Development Strategy Strengthening Programme indicates that only 37% of systems were being reported as providing water 24/7/day, every day of the year.

**Outcome 2 will address this barrier – section B.3 below provides relevant details.**

## Barrier 3 - Design standards - current designs do not account for full range of climate resilient approaches and technologies. The Water Sector Development Strategy (WSDS) documents and water supply designs were reviewed from an engineering perspective as part of the feasibility study, while considering climate change and other vulnerabilities to secure water infrastructure.


---
**Page 23**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 19

Design standards, procedures, and choices of water systems and their components will provide the greatest resilience to climate change and extreme weather events. Due to limited available data, finances and/or resources, and skills and experience, some climate-resilient technologies are not adopted or implemented in a timely manner. The proposed project will focus on implementing simple yet effective solutions such as gravity-fed systems and community reservoirs. These are cost-effective and can be scaled up for greater consideration of water resource security hierarchy and threats such as saltwater intrusion.

Although the designs are standard and typically optimised sufficiently, they do not extend beyond the minimum requirements to include additional features that would provide resilience against mechanisms which could maintain water reliability during flood, drought or natural disasters. It was noted in the 2016 DOWR assessment that there is a lack of capacity at the district level to avoid these additional costs.

The additional costs of a larger tank or pipe could greatly increase water security now and into the future.

Outcome 2 will address this barrier – section B.3 provides relevant details.

## Barrier 4 - Technical capacity - human resource capacity and knowledge of technical areas associated with water resources management at all levels, including provincial and national levels

The National Sustainable Development Plan (2016-2030)258 proposes for capacity and technical assistance to be provided by the Department of Water Resources (DOWR). The DOWR provides human resources, overseeing the provision of safe, secure and sustainable management of community water supplies. In 2016, the DOWR had a total of 34 staff members, with 5 staff in each district. Staff have been organised in three units: Monitoring and Evaluation, Technical Services, and Projects and Operations.

In 2016, the DOWR lacked human resource capacity, with a need to expand staff numbers, and only having two of three Section Heads in an acting capacity and with little management experience. Furthermore, many staff members were not trained or qualified for their roles, and had limited experience at the start of their employment. In 2016, only 5 of 16 planned recruitments were successfully completed.

The DOWR has a number of projects that are being implemented by either the Department itself or dedicated Project Management Units. This, combined with competing urban water resources tasks, has limited capacity to manage and deliver other projects. The majority of the technical staff members were generally only available for collaborative projects. Indeed, the DOWR does currently have some civil engineering capacity in its ranks.

## Barrier 4 - Technical capacity - human resource capacity and knowledge of technical areas associated with water resources management at all levels, including provincial and national levels

### Stakeholder engagement, conducted during the preparation of this Feasibility Study, demonstrated that DoWR staff often undertake duties at a technical level below their terms of reference requirements. Training is required to improve the skills of staff in areas such as project planning, budget absorption of skills, leading to ineffective outputs. The training survey (self-assessment) conducted in February 2016 revealed that staff members were lacking in the following areas: basic computer skills, technical and project management, data processing and computer skills. The survey returned few respondents who felt confident with their ability to manage projects or understand the relationship between water security and WASH infrastructure, generated likely from the well-established status quo of siloed approaches within the sector. The DOWR has a total budget for 2016/2017 amounting to the following features:

- Infrastructure development resources on climate-resilient water management at area council, provincial and national level; end;
- Issues related to water security and skills on climate change issues and climate-resilient water management at the provincial and national levels.

Outcome 3 will address this barrier – section B.3 provides relevant details.

## Barrier 5 - Institutional constraints - The Department of Water Resources and associated water governance architecture, institutional arrangements, resources availability, service capacity and effective implementation across all involved levels

The DOWR is currently an operational division primarily located within the Office of the Prime Minister.

**Page 24**

## B

Service Commission’s Framework for Institutional Capacity Assessment and Development (available as Annex C of the Feasibility Study), which was designed to clarify the capacity needs, and assessment and development priorities of the institution. The framework is structured in nine sections: I: Introduction; II: Strategic Framework, Purpose and Mandate; III: Strategic Plans and Budgets; IV: Organisational Function and Design; V: Human Resources Management; VI: Information Management Systems and Processes; VII: Performance; VIII: Workplace Culture and Leadership; IX: Infrastructure. Several indicators were identified to assess the capacity of the institution in each section, including: I: institutional function; operational procedures; and service delivery standards. Some indicators were highlighted as requiring further development, such as: II: organisational design; III: human resources management; IV: information management and improved project implementation efficiency; and data accessibility.

The gap in institution building and capacity development, hence, is an important barrier that the project will address. The barriers are:

* inadequate institutional capacities on climate-resilient water management corporate and operations services
* lack of robust and accessible data for integrated and climate-resilient water management.
Outcome 3 will address barrier – section B.3 provides relevant details.

### Barrier 6 – Financial constraints - lack of financing restricts the ability for communities and local public institutions to meet the demand for CR-WASH infrastructure installations at provincial and community levels.

The World Bank Systematic Country Diagnostic (SCD) for Mozambique highlights that while public expenditures tend to be high in relation to the size of the population, they are not always targeted towards the most vulnerable. This is due to a lack of capacity on the part of the government to plan and manage the financial and human resources needed to provide adequate public services at national scales. This is supported by the fact that Mozambique has been ranked as one of the least transparent countries in the world, according to the 2018 Corruption Perception Index (CPI). Linked to Barrier 3 this has induced downscaling of infrastructure works or lack of funding for most appropriate technologies and services at provincial levels. This is particularly evident in the aftermath of Tropical Cyclone Idai. Financial constraints for investment in water infrastructure therefore directly present a barrier to achieving the project’s outcomes.

Outcome 2 will address this barrier – section B.3 provides relevant details.

Based on this barrier analysis, a Theory of Change has been developed with outputs geared towards developing climate-resilient WASH and water governance with an overall focus on improving the climate-resilience of WASH infrastructure in Mozambique.

### Figure 4: Theory of Change diagram

#### Project Goal Statement
The project goal explains the causal linkages between the project outputs, outcomes, and the goal that the project will contribute towards (in this case, increased water security and climate resilience of rural communities). The theory of change is presented in Figure 4.

#### Outcomes
1. **Outcome 1:** Communities are empowered to plan and manage climate resilient water resources through the development of a community-based water management plan (CBWMP) and increasing the number of DWSSPs, while also introducing climate adaptation measures in these plans.
2. **Outcome 2:** The project will contribute towards improved water security for rural communities by enhancing access to safe drinking water and sanitation services.

#### Project Delivery
The project will be delivered through three Outcomes to address the barriers above:

1. **Outcome 3:** Communities have enhanced rural water infrastructure

This Outcome has been designed to create a paradigm shift towards implementing CR-WASH in a scalable and sustainable way at the community level, building on UNICEF research that this can only be achieved by addressing the underlying causes of poor water supply and sanitation (WSS) service delivery. The Total Access Framework Programme (TAF), for example, which is implemented through the DWSSP process, provides an opportunity to address these underlying causes.

#### Outcome 4: Communities have enhanced rural water infrastructure
This outcome represents the core investment envisioned for a paradigmatic shift towards CR-WASH planning and implementation at national, provincial, district, and local levels in prioritized rural communities (through the DWSSP process) and to address climate variability and change.

## 50. Outcome 3: Provincial and national institutions are strengthened to address climate risks associated with water resources

A key barrier identified in the process of adapting to climate-related water risks in Vanuatu stem from constraints on institutional capacity at provincial level, particularly in terms of human resource capacity. This is further exacerbated by limited institutional strengthening support for provincial governments, coupled with national oversight and coordination of the many government agencies involved in water resources management.

The Government of Vanuatu recognizes that to ensure the successful delivery of the National Implementation Plan (NIP), there must be strong provincial support and leadership, the latter being combined with national oversight. Additionally, it also recognizes that essential to efficient delivery of the National Implementation Plan is the need for a clear understanding of the role of each government agency in supporting provincial governments.

Outcome 3 works towards building institutional capacity at provincial and national level to ensure the successful delivery of the NIP. This will be achieved through strengthening existing management processes to manage the adverse effects of climate change. This Outcome will address barriers 2, 4 and 6 as described above.

## 51. Six hundred communities will be direct beneficiaries as part of Outcomes 1 (improved planning and community-based activities on climate-resilient water management), of which 200 are also benefit from improved water quality in their communities as part of Outcome 3 (institutional strengthening). The DWSSPs will be targeted under Outcome 2 during the first year of the project. Further to this, 100 DWSSPs will be targeted under Outcome 4 during the second and third years.

This makes a total of 750 communities directly benefiting from Outcomes 1 and 3 (600) plus 100 from Outcome 2 (100). This represents approximately 8% of the total population in Vanuatu. Indirect beneficiaries include the entire rural population in Vanuatu (218,950 individuals), which will benefit from improved water quality and capacity building for institutions and processes leading to climate-resilient water security for rural communities (Outcome 3).

## Objective of the project: Therefore, it is to contribute to the management of water-related impacts from climate change in WASH and protected drinking water sources in Viet Nam.

The project will address water-related impacts from climate change in Viet Nam, which are increases in (i) water-related disasters, (ii) water scarcity, and (iii) water quality deterioration.

### Project Overview

The project will increase the adaptive capacity of rural communities to better cope with the additional burden of climate change on water security and safety by improving the current design criteria for protected drinking water sources and WASH facilities. The project will also improve the resilience of these facilities through the implementation of climate-resilient design criteria, as described in Outcome 3 that will focus on capacity building of government institutions to enhance delivery of CR-WASH services and increase the adaptive capacity of rural communities to better cope with climate change activities.

For detailed breakdown of each Outcome and their sub-outcomes, please refer to Annex 2: Feasibility Study.

## Outcomes

### Outcome 1: Communities are empowered to plan and manage climate-resilient water resources

Rationale: See Section B.1 Outcome 1 for more detail on the Outcome's rationale.

Beneficiaries: 73,940 direct beneficiaries (including 35,970 women), which is a 25% increase in coverage compared to the current project.

Complementary with other Outcomes: The Outcome will establish strengthened technical capacities and institutional arrangements for climate-resilient water resource management at the community level directly into DWSSP designs with a focus on adaptation for resilience and long-term sustainability. The Outcome will also contribute to the achievement of GCF Core Indicators 5, 6, and 8 through knowledge transfer. Increased capacity in these areas enables greater consideration of climate considerations in water resource planning.

Outcome indicators: Directly contributes to GCF Core Indicators 5 and 6 – see text for further details.

This Outcome has two outputs, one focused on improving the DWSSP methodology, while the other focuses on improving the design, implementation and management capacities of communities and local government institutions.

### Output 1.1: New and existing DWSSPs incorporate incremental improvements to mainstream adaptation solutions

There is a need for overhaul of design and engineering standards experienced by the DWSSP—which combines with the above water-related hazards of climate variability. This proposal will address this gap by incorporating incremental improvements into the DWSSP methodology to enhance resilience by implementing simple changes to the design criteria, providing staff exposure and experience in enhanced design criteria, and ensuring that these are reflected in the DWSSP methodology and technology design enhancements suggested through project formulation. These shall be refined over pilot testing.

The DWSSP methodology has been updated recently. Through this project the methodology will be further updated with a specific focus on strengthening the mainstreaming of climate resilience. Annual incremental improvements to the DWSSP methodology will also be made, taking into account experience-based learning in the enhancement process.

In the new DWSSPs developed with communities, the updated methodology will be used to ensure climate-resilient design criteria are incorporated. The existing and new DWSSPs technology assistance provided to ensure these plans are refined to better reflect climate risks and adaptation solutions. The important role of community-managed water resources in disaster risk reduction (DRR) through cash-for-assistance (CDA) or CDA-driven investments in the WASH sector, these are often as much in nature and have no direct link with the project. Therefore, it is important to ensure that climate-resilient design criteria are incorporated. The updated existing DWSSPs and new DWSSPs will therefore mainstream climate resilience into their design criteria.

### 1.2.2: Organize knowledge sharing events

#### 1.2.2.1: Organize community level knowledge sharing events to ensure that the communities are aware of the benefits and importance of WASH (Water, Sanitation and Hygiene) services. This will be done through video-conferences and learning materials (MOEEL and Environmental and Social Safeguard (ESS)) as well as through radio and television campaigns.

The impacts of climate change and solutions for climate resilience will be shared during these trainings. These trainings will be organized by Area Councils, including easily comprehensible written documents and video materials.

#### 1.2.2.3: Organize community level knowledge sharing events to ensure that the communities are aware of the benefits and importance of WASH (Water, Sanitation and Hygiene) services. This will be done through video-conferences and learning materials (MOEEL and Environmental and Social Safeguard (ESS)) as well as through radio and television campaigns.

### 62. Output 1.3: Vulnerable communities are supported to develop and implement their DWSSWPs (500 DWSSWs by the end of 2020)

The majority of WASH systems in the Pacific are isolated from government service provision and are managed by community members. The proposed project will support the development of governance structures at grassroots level that are accountable, with participatory decision-making mandates and any necessary legal frameworks to ensure sustainability. This will be done through a process by which community-owned WASH infrastructure (new and/or improved) can be sustainably managed and maintained.

The project will provide technical assistance for the development of 500 DWSSWs in targeted DWSSWs, identified through the country-wide (DoWR-led) NIP process. For a full description of the methodology used to identify these communities, please refer to Annex B. The support received at the Area Council or Provincial Government. Support to communities to develop DWSSWPs is provided by the Area Councils and/or Provincial Government. This takes into consideration drought maps and local knowledge from Area Councils, National Disaster Management Office (NDMO), and other relevant stakeholders.

If a community is selected for development of a DWSSWP, the support is provided to conduct a DWSSWP with the Rural Water Committee and other relevant stakeholders. The project will provide technical assistance in areas such as water quantity and quality, and capacity building through the Provincial Water Resources Advisory Committee (PWAC). This will be done by providing training on WASH systems management and maintenance during the DWSSWP workshops they will be eligible for CAP funding. RWCs will be registered under the national register of rural water (provincial NWR) region to be established in this Output.

As presented in Annex 2 section 6.3, approximately 2000 DWSSWs may be developed by 2030. To reach this target, it is necessary that the capacity of the existing WASH systems does not exceed the DWSSW current capacity. This project will therefore provide resources to enhance the capacity of the existing WASH systems and ensure their sustainability for at least a period of five years in 1-3 of the project. This will enable the 600 developed DWSSWs to access the resources as described above, and thus be able to continue to operate and maintain them during the remaining implementation period for communities most in need under the context of climate vulnerability.

The technical capacities provided by the project in terms of technical knowledge and human resources will be complemented with a range of institutional improvements that will ensure the sustainability required operations at the scale needed. This in conjunction with institutional improvements also provided by the project, will ensure that the DWSSWPs are sustainable and have long-term impact as well as DoWR has to long-term strategy and capacity to meet the 2030 target.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 29

## Introduction

The proposal aims to increase the engagement of communities in management, oversight and accountability of drinking water service points and infrastructure (through, particularly, the RWAs).

In 2014-2015, the national Water Resources Inventory (WRI) under DWRM and UNICEF conducted a water governance assessment across 379 water service points across 44 islands of which:

* **15% were Indirect Gravity Feed Systems** (IGFS)
* **2% were Direct Gravity Feed Systems**
* **65% Community Rainwater Harvesting systems.**

Only *79%* of systems were identified as functional and only *33%* of these systems were reported as providing water daily, every day or most days.

The CAP facility under the GCF aims to:

1. **Upgrade or build new infrastructure** for all required infrastructure needs at present.
2. **Build institutional capacity**, including human resource development and technical capacity building.
3. **Develop a monitoring system** that will allow for continuous improvement of the CAP.

Please see Table 5 below and Annex 4's detailed budget notes for a detailed breakdown of planned investments, costs, and timelines.

The GCF is committed to providing a level of support feasible considering the human resource and technical capacities conducting CAP operations in the country. The GCF will provide financial assistance to the DWRM through its Project Management Unit (PMU). Noting the 270 additional to the current CAP capacity which completed 100 investments in 2021, expected to be completed by June 2023.

Outcome 3 it is envisioned that this project will also increase capacity of the DWRM to out grow the first 102 IGFS and 54 Direct Gravity Feed Systems targeting the most vulnerable communities to climate change through the improved assessment process.


| Table 6: Average cost of investments through the CAP to date |
| --- |
| **System Type** | **Average Cost (USD): materials, shipping and labour** |
| **Piped Water Supply System** | $250 |
| **Direct Gravity Feed System** | $96360 |
| **Community Rainwater Harvesting system** | $17480 |
| **Indirect Gravity Feed System** | $53436 |

The 270 communities targeted for this Output will be selected from those DWSSPs developed under Outcome 1 and will be financed by GCF resources through the DWRM managed CAP. DWSSPs will be selected based on the criteria outlined in Annex 22, Section 2.1.1 and 3.1 for pre-qualification conditions and list of eligible communities. The selection process is detailed in the Terms of Reference.

Once a community has been deemed to meet the pre-qualification and eligibility criteria a request for CAP funding will be developed by the RWC with assistance from the Provincial Governors, DWRM Provincial Officers, Area Councils and other relevant stakeholders. For further information on how to apply please see Annex 22 for full details. In summary the process:

* **Step 1:** Complete the application form (application form and an approved DWSP).
* **Step 2:** Submit overall risk scores (category and safety) and justification for each application (see Section 4. Annex 22).
* **Step 3:** Use the overall risk scores to categorise all new applications (see Section 4.2 Annex 22).
* **Step 4:** Make adjustment to the category for community commitment (see Section 4.3 Annex 22).
* **On a bi-annual basis, complete the application form** for the National Water Resources Advisory Committee (NWRC) to review and approve.
* **On an annual basis**, complete the application form for the National Water Resources Advisory Committee (NWRC) to review and approve.
* **The NWRC will, on an annual base, review and make decisions on which community improvements will proceed**. The NWRC is responsible through its delegated authority to each province. NWRCs are newly provincial government of these decisions.

## B

### Executive Summary: SPIC will also be an EE for project acting through the Geosciences, Energy and Meteorology (GEM) component of the Pacific Climate Change Resilience Programme (PCCRP), a key technical pillar of the donor and develops, implements and supports coordinated climate change adaptation efforts to respond to challenges faced by communities, as well as repeated disaster risks in the Pacific region.

The DCRP will be essential in enabling community resilience through CR-WASH in Vanuatu given the breadth of issues that need to be addressed. These include water supply, sanitation, hygiene, food security, water security and sanitation, hazard risk understanding, Ridge-to-Reef solutions and natural resource management, as well as disaster risk reduction and climate change adaptation for Community Resilient Development in the Pacific (CRDP) through the provision of coordinated technical support to our mission. As such SPIC through the DCRP is well placed to execute the project.

As an EE, SPIC will be responsible for ensuring that the project is implemented efficiently. More specifically, the EE will oversee tasks within the overall project management structure including:

- contracting PMU (Project Manager, National Project Engineer, Administrative Assistant and Environmental Officer)
- providing guidance to support the PMU in alignment with the direction of the National Project Steering Committee
- supporting the PMU to ensure that all project level reports/output are prepared in a timely manner and according to GCF guidelines
- concluding and manage contracts of suppliers and services providers;
- quality assurance and validation of project reports submitted by the PMIC;
- ensuring implementation of activities are aligned with the project work plan; and,
- coordinating and facilitating project level audit in alignment with GCF obligations.

In this regard, SPIC will enter into a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) with UNICEF, pursuant to which UNICEF will provide technical assistance for specific activities identified in the MoU. This agreement will be signed by the PMU and UNICEF and will cover all aspects related to the implementation of the project.

## B

### Executive Entity: The Government of Vanuatu (GoV) will be the Executing Entity for the Project acting through the Ministry of Finance (MoF), the Department of Water Resources (DoWR) and the National Water Resources Advisory Committee (NWRCAC)

The MoF, as the AE, will execute all activities through the Project Manager, who will be contracted as a project position, and will provide technical support to DoWR on activities 1.1.1., 1.2.1., 1.2.2., 1.3.1., 2.1.1., 2.1.2., 2.1.3.

The MoF will receive the GCF resources from the AE and allocate these into the CAP accounts to be administered by the DoWR.

The DoWR - housed within the Ministry of Lands and Natural Resources, is the focal government entity responsible for water resource management in Vanuatu. The DoWR's mandate includes the provision of safe, secure and sustainable management of community water supplies, protection of water resources, catchment area management, and flood control.

The DoWR coordinates rural water supply sector implementation plans, driven through the National Implementation Plan for Safe and Secure Community Drinking Water (NP). The NP process and integrated approach to project development ensures that a clear vision is established where efficient and effective delivery of improvement services is mobilized. Through this, DWSWSS are planned for over 100 communities in Vanuatu.

The DoWR further administers the CAP under the decision-making authority of the MoF, which includes the allocation of funds to the CAP accounts, the provision of financial support for infrastructure construction or upgrades.

The Department is also the lead coordinator of the Vanuatu WASH Cluster, with DoWR acting as secretariat. The cluster provides a platform where stakeholders can share information and lessons learned from the numerous experiences that the country experiences, and the DoWR is experienced in responding to water sector challenges.

Considering the above, the DoWR will have multiple roles in the implementation of this project: co-chairing the Project Steering Committee; administering the funds of the CAP; and housing the Project Manager. The DoWR will also be responsible for developing and implementing processes, strategies and regulation, whilst facilitating learning and ensuring strong national ownership.

The DoWR will:

- contracting PMU staff (Provincial Project Engineer, Provincial Coordinator, Finance Officer, Procurement Officer) to manage funds within the CAP accounts
- for GCF resources these will be under a separate budget line item in the CAP accounts
- on approval of GCF funding the DoWR will administer the funds of the CAP, contracting and managing provincial engineers to ensure that all works are carried out to standard and in alignment with government policies and regulations
- providing guidance to support the PMU in alignment with the direction of the National Project Steering Committee
- supporting the PMU so as to ensure that all project level reporting outputs are prepared in a timely manner, and that all activities are aligned with the project work plan
- ensuring implementation of activities are in alignment with the project work plan

The GoV, acting through the DoWR, will execute all activities through the provincial engineers, provincial coordinators and finance officers. The provincial engineers, provincial coordinators and finance officers will participate in the implementation of activities 1.1.1., 1.2.1., 1.2.2., 1.3.1., 2.1.1., 2.1.2., 2.1.3., 3.1.1., 3.2.1 and 3.3.1.

The AE will enter into a subsidiary grant agreement with the GoV pursuant to which the AE will on-grant GCF resources for the implementation of the activities by the GoV, acting through its DoWR, MoF and NWRCAC.

74. National Project Steering Committee (NPSC)

A National Project Steering Committee (NPSC) will be established immediately after the inception workshop for the project and will be co-chaired by the Director of the DoWR and the NDA (or their nominated representatives). The NPSC will include the DWR Director, the NDA Director, the Chairperson of the WASH Coordination Committee, a representative from the Ministry of Women's Affairs, and a representative of the Ministry of Local Authorities of the Government of Vanuatu.

In addition to the above members, the EE, the Project Manager, the New Zealand Ministry of Foreign Affairs and Trade (MFAT) Vanuatu WASH Coordinator™, and selected technical partners (identified at inception workshop) will be invited as observers. The NPSC will provide implementation guidance, strategic support and financial and procedural oversight to the project.

The NPSC will:

- Provide strategic guidance and implementation oversight of the Project through review of progress annually and quarterly.
- Provide guidance and direction on cross-cutting issues which require consensus from the various stakeholders involved in the project.
- Ensure that institutional strengthening through the activities is consistent with the Project's overall objectives and strategies.
- Facilitate full cooperation of various stakeholders under their jurisdictions to access and acquire land for the project.
- Represent the interests of civil society and communities derived through NDA and DoWR bilateral dialogues.
- Approve the project’s administrative, financial, accounting and operations manual.
- Approve the project's Annual Work Program and Budget (AWPB), and
- Ensure that the project is implemented in accordance with the PMA and PMI as needed on an ad hoc basis.

The NPSC will act in accordance with best practices and standards for governing bodies and ensure that the Project consistently results with best value for money, fairness, integrity, transparency and effectiveness. The NPSC will be responsible to the EE and the DoWR and NDA Directors for approving the PMA and PMI as needed on an ad hoc basis.

75. WASH Coordination Committee

The WASH Coordination Committee is an independent committee appointed by the Minister for Land and Natural Resource and is chaired by the DWR and acts in the behalf of the Gov’t. All relevant stakeholders are invited to participate in this committee, including the NDA, DoWR, and the NWRC. The NWRC will, on an annual basis, review and make decisions on which community improvement activities should be prioritized for funding. The NWRC will provide NWRC advice to the DoWR and provincial government of these decisions. On approval, the DWR will administer the GCF resourcesinvested through the PMA.

76. Lead UNICEF

UNICEF is a lead partner in the Government’s (G) Funded WASH project as part of the mandate of the WASH sector in Vanuatu (and other Pacific islands), working with governments to achieve universal and equitable access to safe drinking water, sanitation and hygiene services through strengthening national systems and capacity through a mix of community-based activities and collection of evidence. UNICEF will provide technical support for the project implementation, including providing technical partnership with the government and civil society organizations, works primarily on the rights children of survival and development by through improved drinking water, sanitation and hygiene behavior.

Notably, the NPSC’s Terms of Reference (ToR) of the WASH sector and WASH Coordination Committee have been used to inform the project design. Further, UNICEF has actively been engaged in the NIP processes and defined its role as a lead partner in the G Funded WASH project. The Government of Vanuatu will enter into an EE, enter into a Letter of Agreement with UNICEF, pursuant to which UNICEF, as Lead Technical Partner, will provide technical support for the project implementation including through its activities. Specifically, in relation to sub-activities 1.1.1.1, 1.2.1.1, 1.2.1.2, 1.2.1.3, 1.2.1.4, 1.2.2.1, 1.2.2.2, 1.2.2.3 and 1.2.2.4 of the Project Implementation Manual (PIM), UNICEF will provide technical support and recommendations to the PMI for implementation to ensure alignment (but not overlap) with ongoing

## Project Management Unit (PMU)

The PMU will be established under the Subsidiary Agreement between the Government of [Country] and the Green Climate Fund, in accordance with the terms described above. On implementation matters, the PMU will carry out AWPBS (in consultation with the LTF or other relevant stakeholders) to ensure that all project activities are carried out as per the approved documentation. Further, the PMU will be embedded within the DWHR to allow the Department to play a central role in the project’s implementation and to facilitate close collaboration between the DWHR processes, and local contexts. Project implementation will be strongly aligned with national water safety and sanitation policies and strategies, and will be guided by the overall strategic direction of the DWHR at activity level. This will ensure more effective implementation through enhanced knowledge transfer between the DWHR and the PMU.

The PMU will consist of various positions hired both by EEAs in an open competitive process. The selection and recruitment PMU staff will by SPC will follow SPC recruitment practices and be done in close consultation with the DWHR. All positions will be advertised at national level, and a panel of qualified representatives to participate in the interval panel. All positions will be advertised at local levels and preferences will be given to EEAs for all positions.

### Project Manager

- **Responsibility**: Be responsible for the day-to-day operations of the PMU within the parameters laid down by the NSFPC through the approved AWPBS and in alignment with their guidance and recommendations. The Project Manager will ensure that all project activities are carried out as per the log risk and will ensure regular communication with all selected service providers, CSD organisation and provincial staff. They will also be responsible for ensuring that the PMU is well coordinated with stakeholders and facilitates implementation as needed. With support from PMU members as required, the Project Manager will be responsible for:

  - i) Ensuring that all project activities are carried out in accordance with the approved AWPBS.
  - ii) All projects meet GCF obligations under the F&A. Overall, it is the Project Manager’s responsibility to ensure that all decisions related to the project. This role will also serve as a project position by SPC.

### Project Administrative Assistant

- **Responsibility**: Will support the PMU through carrying out all necessary administrative tasks required for day-to-day operations and provide secretarial and executive roles for organisation for NSFPC meetings and ad hoc communications as needed. This role will be recruited locally within the DWHR.

### National Project Engineer (NPE)

- **Responsibility**: Will review DWSVP and CAP proposals with a focus on the infrastructure design works to be conducted under the project. They will assess infrastructure designs recommended by SPC, EEAs, and other stakeholders in line with the approved AWPBS and any guidelines as necessary. Capacity building sessions will also be provided to support DWHR engineers and provincial staff in reviewing DWSVP and CAP proposals. The National Project Engineer will act as the focal point for provincial engineers, supporting them on high technical designs and serving as a resource person during review of DWSVP and CAP proposals. This role will be recruited locally within the SPC GEM in consultation with the LTP.

### Provincial Engineers

- **Responsibility**: Six provincial engineering management specialists will be locally hired and will focus on reviewing DWSVP and CAP proposals for technical support on DWSVP and CAP proposal designs. They will also work closely with provincial officers, selected service providers and relevant information systems. All project activities and data will be coordinated in alignment with the MEL officer and the projects M&E officer roles described above.

### Provincial Coordinator

- **Responsibility**: Will be responsible for liaising with the Provincial Engineers and the National Project Engineer to ensure that all project activities are carried out as per the approved AWPBS, an equitable division of technical assistance between them. He/she will support the MEL Officer in coordinating provincial staff and aggregating financial figures and reporting from the provincial level. Further, he/she will interact with the PWRAGs and facilitate the submission of provincial

# B

## WASH Partners
The purpose of Vanuatu WASH Sector Partners is to ensure the adequacy, coherence and alignment of overall Government submissions by multi-sectoral partners.

### The WASH Sector
- Led by the DoWR and co-led by UNICEF.
- Composed of 15 members.
- Meets on a regular basis to share information and set priority activities.
- Provides technical guidance and advice for projects in the WASH sector.
- Acts as an important group to provide technical guidance and enhance planning and coordination between the DoWR, UNICEF, and other partners.

## Funds: From WASH
On successful completion of conditions prior to disbursement listed in the Project EAP, the DoWR will transfer funds from the GCF to the AE for LTP as a pre-selected project.
- Further, the DoWR will transfer funds to UNICEF for LTP as a pre-selected project.

## Subsequent Allocations and Disbursements
All subsequent allocations and disbursements will be requested by the PMB on AWBP approvals by the NPCIC. The AE will make subsequent allocations and disbursements to the EGE in case this project is aligned with the GCF Strategic Plan.
- All previous instances of financial and technical alignment reported in Annual Progress Reports.

## Financial Barriers
The DoWR has limited resources available for WASH in general, and even more constrained by the need to invest in water supply and infrastructure. The latest GLASS report determined that the average national expenditure globally on WASH is $50 per capita or 1.27% of GDP.
- Over 90% of Vanuatu's WASH activities are funded by the DoWR, with only 3% from other sources.

#### B

**87. Funding from GCF and also ensure that government-owned and -led processes to deliver climate-investment projects are strengthened through the use of a project-level cost-benefit analysis methodology, which has been proven in the years of its implementation. For instance, communities that have adopted this approach have seen significant improvements in their ability to implement high-impact and low-cost improvements.** Grant financing from the GCF is justified for this purpose because of the externalities associated with climate change adaptation projects, as well as the lack of resources at all levels (local, provincial, national) to deal with projected climate change impacts.

#### 88. Summary of economic and financial analysis:

A financial analysis for each of the four measures was carried out. The IRR and NPV were determined for two scenarios: 1) finance provided through a loan from the GCF to the government (donor or developer) and 2) finance provided through a grant from the GCF and “equity” from an investor (donor or government). The analysis considered three different discount rates: 5%, 7.5%, and 10%. Average Cost of Capital (WACC), and b) either a tariff set at the maximum ability to pay (95% VGGH/BRIVAMAC) or a tariff set at 20% of the VGGH/BRIVAMAC would be sufficient to cover the OPEX for the technology in question. The results are summarized below:

| Measure | Baseline | Project alternative |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **M1 - Rainwater harvesters** | Tariff required to pay VGGH/BRIVAMAC | % monthly income needed to pay | Minimum tariff (+ equity coverage) | % ability to pay |
| M2 - (Dairy) dairy feed system | $8.95 | 52% | 1,367% | $0.00 | 4% | 100% |
| M3 - Water desalination systems | $214.00 | 123% | 3,874% | $15.00 | 9% | 257% |
| M4 - DOF systems | $104.00 | 61% | 1,794% | $12.00 | 7% | 208% |

In all cases the monthly household tariff that would be required for a commercial loan-based investment would be many orders of magnitude beyond what households can pay. In the project alternative, with GCF grant funding and equity from an investor (donor or government), the maximum ability to pay reported required to pay M2, M3, and M4. Since these investments are seen as a public good these subsidies could be directed by public funds.

An economic analysis was conducted in order to conform to the incremental adaptation benefits to climate change. This analysis combines all three measures, scaled up to the envisaged number of investment units that would potentially be financed by the project. The project-level analysis takes into account the costs and benefits of the project (i.e., non-investment Outcomes or i.e., non-investment Outcomes as well) and project management costs and co-finance.
