The conclusion is that the proposed programme is economically viable and can be justified on financial grounds. However, it should also be noted that the analysis does not include direct benefits being envisaged. It is also noteworthy that the analysis included conservative assumptions and not all of these are likely to occur. Furthermore, the project will not be able to fully monetize their monetary values, but these would benefit nonetheless once under the proposed interventions.**

## B.5 Exit strategy (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

91. The exit strategy for this project ensures that the barriers to climate resilience are overcome sustainably, and that the project is able to be scaled up in a manner that is consistent with long-term sustainability. Recognizing the importance of long-term sustainability, the project has been designed from the outset to allow for the transition of responsibility to the Government of Vanuatu (GoV) at the end of the project's conception and preparation phase – see Annex 7 for more details), strengthen and design delivery capacity in the GoV, and ensure that the project is able to leverage resources in support of project objectives of improved climate resilience and water security in Vanuatu.

92. From the outset, the long-term sustainability of this project approach is ensured through the fact that it is designed as a replicable model for other projects within the country, rather than being a one-off or off-project-specific product. This includes the NIP framework, CAP investment programme and the DRRVAC fund. The project will also ensure that the PMU is embedded in the GoV to establish parallel structures and processes that only last for the duration of the project, instead operating through existing structures and processes thereafter. The PMU will be established at the end of the project's conceptionualization and will continue to function after project conclusion, integrating lessons learned and best practices from the project. Moreover, the embedding of the PMU within the DRRVAC ensures that

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 41

## Project activities are carried out in alignment with government activities. The strong government ownership of the NIP, CAP and DSWF/ModS processes mean that the project will be continued beyond its lifespan.

### 93.
The project is envisaged to run for 5 years, and will reach all rural communities through improved institutional processes (Outcome 1), 600 communities with new, and 100 with updated, climate-resilient water infrastructure (Outcome 2) and 400 communities with new or upgraded water supply upgrades (Outcome 2). These communities will then have long-term, climate-resilient water security and improved access to safe drinking water. The project will also provide training for the communities to undertake maintenance of the climate-resilient infrastructure (as part of Outcome 2). This means that the communities will be able to continue using their new or upgraded WASH systems to operate and maintain their water security and safety infrastructure, and they will be able to access safe drinking water.

### 94.
The project will also provide training to community facilitators, water officers and other members of the water governance structures, who will be up-skilled and empowered to continue to work on the WASH sector or in other sectors. The project will also aim at building the capacity of the government to lead the sector; in particular, this will improve the outcomes of the already existing NIP, CAP and DSWF/ModS processes. This means that the project will be continued beyond the lifespan of the project and outside of project-specific communities. The project also creates a policy environment for the WASH sector by providing a framework for water governance structures by mobilizing existing government-owned processes. The project interventions are designed to ensure that the new or upgraded infrastructure is maintained, through building a direct impact to communities (who will be trained) to maintain system improvements to existing WASH systems and infrastructure. This is in line with the Government of Vanuatu’s Water Sector Action Plan (Plan Annex 21) has been designed as part of preparation phase, which has reviewed existing strategies and policies and identified gaps and opportunities for the sector.

### 95.
At the local level, the project has been designed through a participatory process involving all stakeholders, including water governance actors at the local level (municipal councils, departments, NGOs, community members, etc.), to ensure that the project is owned by its beneficiaries. This ensures that the actions proposed in the project are socially viable, having built stakeholder ownership and commitment from the outset of implementation. The project will also ensure effective local inclusion and participation in governance and decision-making during project implementation through a gender-sensitive approach.

### Annex 6 - Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan provides more details on the gender-specific approaches that have been taken to address gender equality issues within the project.

### By being fully integrated into existing national programs, and in line with national policies and strategies of Vanuatu, as indicated in the table below, the project’s outcomes and outputs will be sustainable beyond its lifespan. D. Impact Assessment (500 words, approximately 1 page)

97. Water safety and security is vital for community resilience in the long-term as well as in the immediate aftermath of climate-induced disasters. GCF funding will positively impact and contribute to the management of water-related disaster risks in Vanuatu. The project will address three water-related impacts from climate change in Vanuatu, which are increases in (i) water-related disasters; (ii) demand for water resources; and (iii) water quality deterioration.

98. The project will operate through and enhance the functioning of the government-owned DWSSP process to deliver climate-resilient water security benefits to rural communities in Vanuatu. The Water Sector Development Unit at DoWri has worked to consistently develop and standardize Vanuatu’s planning for safe water. The NIP and DWSSP processes are key components of this work, which have been developed with the participation of government and civil society actors. The NIP process standardizes the implementation of DWSSPs for communities, facilitating governance and accountability in the delivery of water services. The DWSSP process is a participatory, community-driven approach to planning and implementing safe water supply projects and fund deliver DWSSP improvements, through which the project will operate to mobilize, utilize and freeflow systems, and ensure their sustainability.

99. The project’s impact in reducing climate risks for water security is reinforced by recent revisions of the DWSSP processes. These significantly strengthen climate change and vulnerability considerations within DWSSPs, from selection of projects to implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and maintenance. The revised DWSSP also establishes a base for parallel revisions of programs such as the Micro-irrigation Program (MIP) and the Water Sector Development Unit (WSDU). These revisions have been generally well received and understood, with some resources committed to support rollout. By further supporting these processes through this project, the revised DWSSP will be more widely adopted. Through enhanced integration of climate change into planning – the project will increase the adaptive capacity of communities in Vanuatu by strengthening their ability to respond to climate-related water security risks and stresses. This will in turn contribute to achieving GCF’s fund-outcome A7.0 “Strengthened community resilience to climate change” (impact A2.3 “Increased resilience of health and well-being, and food and water security”) and indicator A2.3.1 (“Community resilience to climate change, including increased capacity to respond to shocks and stresses”). This will in turn contribute to achieving GCF’s fund-outcome A7.0 “Strengthened community resilience to climate change” (impact A2.3 “Increased resilience of health and well-being, and food and water security”) and indicator A2.3.1 (“Community resilience to climate change, including increased capacity to respond to shocks and stresses”). # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 47

The project will generate significant environmental, social and economic benefits that can contribute to positive change in the lives of people living in rural communities. For example, it will reduce the negative environmental impacts of poor wastewater management by improving water management behavior and practices (Outputs 1.2 and 3.1). It will also improve access to safe drinking water through improved protection of water sources and forested water catchment areas (Output 1.4), through no- and low-cost community-based interventions.

Furthermore, the project will contribute to equitable access to water as a basic human right in Vanuatu, targeting especially the most vulnerable groups in rural communities. Significant social benefits resulting from project activities include improved health outcomes for women and children (through better sanitation practices and diarrheal) and improved social standing for women through their visible and meaningful roles in water committees (see Outputs 1.2 and 3.1). In addition to these direct project outputs, the project will catalyse gender mainstreaming for water governance through community-based, climate-resilient interventions.

Similarly, the project will provide economic benefits through avoided health care costs (as improved water security and sanitation practices lead to improved health outcomes) and avoided damages in case of disasters (as climate-resilient infrastructure and training on maintenance reduce the loss of damage (affected)).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 48

## D

### D.4. Number of Recipients (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

This project will address vital needs relating to water security for rural communities in Vanuatu, where over 25,000 households lack access to safe drinking water and over 37% have no alternative source of drinking water.

The project addresses the fundamental human right of access to safe drinking water. Communities that have elevated levels of vulnerability, low access to water services and are exposed to climate risks will be targeted for intervention. The project will provide a direct and sustainable solution to the water security needs of communities most in need of climate-resilient water management interventions.

The project recognizes the primary challenge for water safety and security in Vanuatu, namely that while access to a proximate source of drinking water is high (94% access) to an improved drinking water source and 80% access to safe drinking water are low. The majority of rural communities have no alternative source of drinking water.

Rural communities in Vanuatu use a combination of groundwater, surface water and rainwater that are increasingly precarious due to limited climate proofing of infrastructure and processes. Of these sources, rainwater harvesting is the most common (90% access), followed by groundwater (75%), surface water (28%) and piped supply. Groundwater-based water sources are less common, comprising only 13% of surveyed systems. Limited access to improved drinking water services in rural communities is due to a combination of factors including lack of infrastructure, low levels of awareness about the importance of safe drinking water, limited financial resources for water service providers and households, and inadequate population density.

Water rationing, both for household use and sanitation, and water shortages are common during the dry season. The dry season can last up to 6 months in some areas, with rainfall decreasing by as much as 80% from wet to dry season. Conversely, rainfall often manifests as intense periods of extreme precipitation, leading to floods that further exacerbate water insecurity.

The project will provide a direct and sustainable solution to the water security needs of communities most in need of climate-resilient water management interventions.

117. The most vulnerable communities in Vanuatu are mapped in Annex 2 in terms of the variations and trends in drinking water sources as well as sanitation infrastructure**, including**:

* **The majority of households (80%) rely on rainwater, wells or surface water for drinking water at 11%.**
    * However, regional and intra-island variation is high. In West Santa (Santa Province), South Esméranço and North Malekula (Malekula Province) the proportion of households relying on rainwater, wells or surface water for drinking water is as low as 2%.
* **The majority of communities are vulnerable to climate-induced risks**, with household-level vulnerability and community vulnerabilities to climate-induced risks falling across a spectrum and must be addressed through localized interventions.
    * The national average of households primarily using household or shared tanks for drinking water is 34.3%. This varies significantly between provinces, from as low as 10% in West Santa (Santa Province) and South Esméranço (Esméranço Province), to as high as 75% in North Malekula (Malekula Province).
    * The national average of households primarily using rainwater, wells or surface water for drinking water is 19%. This varies significantly between provinces, from as low as 2% in West Santa (Santa Province) and South Esméranço (Esméranço Province), to as high as 75% in North Malekula (Malekula Province).
* **The national average of households primarily using household or shared tanks for sanitation is 40%.**
    * This varies significantly between provinces, from as low as 12% in West Santa (Santa Province) and South Esméranço (Esméranço Province), to as high as 75% in North Malekula (Malekula Province).
* **The national average of households primarily using rainwater, wells or surface water for sanitation is 46%.**
    * This varies significantly between provinces, from as low as 20% in West Santa (Santa Province) and South Esméranço (Esméranço Province), to as high as 75% in North Malekula (Malekula Province).

118. The project will address these needs through a combination of institutional strengthening and capacity building at national and devolved levels, as well as through direct improvements to drinking water and WASH systems.

The interventions to achieve these improvements are outlined in Section D.3.


D.3. Sector ownership (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

119. This project is aligned with the Government of Vanuatu’s National Water Strategy, Vanuatu’s GCF Country Programme, and has been fully co-developed with the DoWfR, along with other national stakeholders. By addressing climate change impacts on water resources management in a participatory manner through the development of a community-based adaptation activity, the project is fully aligned with the Government of Vanuatu’s climate change strategy (Vanuatu National Climate Change Strategy 2016–2030) and its associated action plan (e.g. priority group J), as well as the NAP and the NDC, which both reference water resource management as a key sector for adaptation to climate change in Vanuatu’s National Adaptation Plan of Action 2016-2030 (e.g. objective E/C2) and National Water Policy 2017–2025.


120. The project responds directly to the Government of Vanuatu’s goal for every rural community to have a DWISP. The DoWfR, as the lead agency in water resources management at national level, will be leading the implementation of the project following by the NIP and DWISP processes, through which the project will be implemented. The DoWfR has been working with the Vanuatu Water Cluster (VWC) since 2014 to support DoWfR, which also is lead coordinator of the Vanuatu WASH Cluster, acting as the secretariat and chair during the cluster meetings. The VWC has a strong track record in supporting water sector development through experiences, and the DoWfR is experienced in responding to water access and security issues during disaster and post-disaster recovery phases. The project will be implemented with the support of the Vanuatu Water Cluster and DoWfR, which this project will build on.


121. The Government of Vanuatu has also allocated USD 30 million for financing to support delivery of the project interventions through the CAP process in support of development of DWISP(s). Further indicating the ownership and commitment of DoWfR to the project activities and objectives.

D.4. Efficiency, effectiveness and sustainability (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

122. The overarching project goal is to enhance climate-resilient water security in vulnerable rural communities across Vanuatu. To do so, the interventions will address the social, gender, regulatory, financial, ecological, and technical dimensions of water resources management (see Section B.3) with a focus on enhancing water sector security management (see Section B.1). The project thus serves to shift the water sector development pathway for rural communities in Vanuatu towards a more sustainable direction. A comprehensive description and illustration of the theory of change is presented in Section B.2.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 50

## 123. The project will build climate resilience and adaptation capacities in Vanuatu's WASH sector through resilient and efficient water management technologies.

### 124. The focus of the adaptations investments is on resilient and reliable water infrastructure. It will include:

- **Community Rainwater harvesting (RWH) systems**: These small, modular systems consist of roof catchments, storage tanks, and distribution networks that can be installed in individual households or buildings. Storages can be linked and augmented as needed. Water can be extracted from a tap directly from the tank or through gravity-fed systems.
- **Gravity-fed systems**: Gravity-fed systems deliver water through pipe and storage networks to demand sites. They are simple, low-cost, and easy to maintain. The system's capacity is determined by its size in capacity and distance. Direct Gravity Fed (DGF) systems use only gravity to convey water from the source to demand site. Indirect Gravity Fed (IGF) systems use a pump to lift water from the source and then gravity conveys it to demand center(s). DGF systems can provide large volumes of water at high pressure without requiring electricity, making them suitable for areas with limited access to power or where there is no safe, sited, and topography.
- **Disinfection**: Chlorination and reverse osmosis desalination is increasingly being adopted around the world for its ability to reliably produce excellent quality water with low greenhouse gas emissions. Groundwater systems are also being used in some areas.

Based on the above, the project has the potential to generate a broad range of environmental, social, and economic benefits and co-benefits, ensuring that the project is holistic, efficient and effective:

- **Increased access to safe drinking water**: This will lead to improved health outcomes through adaptation/security measures and needs.
  - Avoided costs associated with low water supply during extreme climate events
  - Avoided losses associated with water import which is costly due to Vanuatu's topography and remoteness.
  - Avoided health-care-related costs associated with water-borne diseases (IBRD)
  - Avoided costs of importing water for domestic use
  - Avoided loss related to labor for gathering water from remote, unamortized, and non-adapted water sources.

### Annex 3 – Economic and Financial Analysis

The financial analysis shows that without grants, the costs would be many orders of magnitude beyond what households can afford. The project is expected to generate significant co-benefits through the implementation of the GCF grant funding; the financial analysis shows that for rainwater harvesters and direct gravity fed systems, an affordable cost per household is USD 105. For indirect gravity fed systems, a higher level of government funding will be required. Concerning the economic analysis, the project-level EMPIV is negative: USD -37,150,065 and the NPV is negative as well: USD -28,490,065. The project is not expected to generate any revenue on its grounds, even though non-investment budget costs (including co-finance) have no envisaged direct benefit. This is the reason why the project is not financially viable and therefore not recommended for funding.

### 125. The efficiency of the DWSSP process will also contribute to the overall effectiveness and sustainability of the projects. The DWSSP is a participatory approach through which the project will identify and prioritize communities in need of system improvements and the activities to bring about such improvements. Those communities that have already created their own water systems will be prioritized for support, as they are more likely to make long-term investments.

### 126. The project will benefit from the existing structures and policy frameworks to efficiently scale up investments by revitalizing and mobilizing the DWSSP process through the ODIHR.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 53

#### Replicability
Low

This project will aim to improve the ability of rural communities and water users associations (WUAs) in Vanuatu to adapt, respond to, and recover from the negative effects of climate change through three Outcomes.

With the investments earmarked for infrastructure rehabilitation and development, combined with established mechanisms of identifying, selecting, and disseminating projects, the project is replicable sub-nationally. Post-project replication will be possible more widely across the country and in other SIDS in the region globally.

Further, having relevant institutional systems and capacity building in place at national level will ensure that the project outputs are sustainable and that DoWR have capacity to continue to deliver these outputs across the country at scale.

#### Sustainability
Medium

The DoWR – and other WASH partners – have been proactive in increasing sustainability of investments through the country owned processes that this project will utilize (e.g. NAP, PAP, CAP and DAWSSP). Additionally, recent Tropical Cyclones (Pan and Harold) – Vanuatu’s Disaster Risk Policy identifies that sustaining infrastructure climate-proofing as an important step towards water security.

The project will utilise existing institutional processes (NAP, CAP and DAWSSP) to embed adaptation measures into the ongoing maintenance of investments. This will ensure communities in Vanuatu are more resilient through improved water management investments, as well as improved water management practices that help sustain these investments in the long term.

By being fully part of existing processes, it is hoped that national policies and institutional capacity will be transformative by ensuring that public funds used for water security projects are not wasted. The project outcomes will also co-benefit from additional government funding, including for climate change adaptation through the same mechanisms as outlined above. Outcome 3 will enhance the institutional processes and build

## Core Indicator: Decrease in GCF project contribution to deployment, dissemination and innovation

### Rural Vanuatu has a relatively high coverage (90%) of improved water sources that exist geographically.

- **Direct beneficiaries include those beneficiaries in communities targeted under Outcomes 1 and 3 who will benefit from the installation of new climate-resilient DWSS and associated infrastructure. Estimated direct beneficiaries are 25% of the total population in Vanuatu (207,890 individuals).**

National government and national institutions are strengthened to promote climate-resilient water security in the long-term these activities and investments will lead to technology deployment, dissemination and innovation transfer benefiting the whole rural population.**

### E.7 Monitoring, reporting, and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
In its role as Accredited Entity, SPC CCES will oversee and supervise the implementation of this project in accordance with the agreement signed between SPC CCES and DoWri. The project will be monitored and reported in compliance with approved SPC policies and GCF requirements under relevant agreements. Further, SPC coordination between its CFU, Strategy, Performance and Learning (SPL) Team and the DoWri will provide supervision and technical assistance as needed to support the PMU’s implementation tools and methods to monitor, evaluate and learn from project activities.


---
**Page 77**
---

13\. In 2014, along with the New Zealand Government Ministry of Foreign Affairs and Trade and BetterEvaluation, co-designed a participatory process to assess and understand the current MEL system and opportunities for capacity strengthening in projects in this region. The MEL system is informed by a draft MEL framework that includes performance indicators, learning objectives, and evaluation criteria. This framework will guide project choices of MEL methods and processes. The MEL for the project will derive from this framework announced in conjunction with Pacific leaders and communities.

13\. The logical framework contains performance indicators by Outcome and outputs, which will be monitored by the PMU and regular (semi-annual) updates provided to the NDA, NSPC and SPC CCES during program implementation. Additionally, the project will undertake rigorous KPI monitoring exercises through Output 2. These will include a combination of quantitative and qualitative data collection methods and Outputs 3. The project will also establish an evaluation and learning framework established for improved learning for climate-based water management. Activities under these Outputs will improve the knowledge base on climate change adaptation in water resources, including the development of tools to support decision-making by key stakeholders.

1\. A Monitoring and Evaluation and Learning Officer will be hired under the PMU to coordinate MEL across the project. This MEL officer will include establishing MEL systems, developing a monitoring plan, and reporting results framework. The MEL officer will work together with the Project Manager under the guidance of the NSPC and the EE to develop a set of MEL tools, objectives, and reporting arrangements for project activities. This will include annual performance reports, quarterly progress reports, and other relevant information as required by the GCF. The MEL officer will also support capacity development in MEL with a focus on how this can be used to maximize activity outcomes while building the evidence base for the results and impact of the activities.

1\. Monitoring will enable the PMU to make adjustments to respond to unexpected events during the implementation phase as well as to build trust and respond to stakeholders and affected communities. The scope, robustness, and frequency of project monitoring, and reporting will vary depending on the type of activity being implemented and the level of risk associated with the activity in the screening process. In addition, monitoring requirements are taken into consideration the circumstances in which the project takes place and is implemented.

1\. For the CR-WASH infrastructure to be installed through the DWSPP processes, Rural Water Committees (RWCs) set up as local water governance mechanisms will be responsible for ensuring that the infrastructure is properly maintained. The RWCs will also be responsible for engaging with the provincial water governance bodies (consisting of Provincial Water Supervisor and Community Water Development Officers) for documentation and policy adjustments to ensure that the needs of the DWSPP are met. The CR-WASH Monitoring and Evaluation Unit will provide guidelines for ensuring the newly registered existing DWSPPs are functional.

1\. Finally, the CRU or GEM team will be jointly responsible for conducting the independent interim and final evaluations for the GCF. The evaluations will be conducted in accordance with the GCF evaluation policy and will include assessments against the criteria of relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, impact, sustainability, and learning others. The evaluation criteria are further described in Appendix II of the GCF Evaluation Policy (2021). The Interim Evaluation will be instrumental in combination with the project’s MEL framework to assess progress towards achieving the project’s objectives and identify any necessary adjustments or management measures for the remaining period of the project, and identifying relevant lessons learned for stakeholders in Vanuatu as well as the broader

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F1: Risk factors and mitigation measures (max. 3 pages)

### Limited interest of engagement from local communities

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | High |

**Description**

Limited interest of engagement from local communities might lead to underdeveloped projects and reduced impacts given poor ongoing maintenance after installation of equipment.

**Mitigation Measures**

The project will build on the NIP and the DWPW which have been well received across the country and reinforced through consultations. These mechanisms are further supported through resources and training and are working more effectively in recent years. The project will also benefit from the experience gained by the communities carried out through area secretaries and Provincial Water Officers. By building on this process the project will be able to build on a strong foundation of community interest and support for the proposed project and have indicated considerable interest in the proposed activities. The project will target communities which are located close to existing NIP and DWPW projects, where there is already an established level of water infrastructure after installation. The project has been designed in such a way as to strengthen participatory governance structures and ensure that communities derive benefits through training support to communities, to build leadership and ownership of the communities.

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | High | High |

**Description**

Urban communities are located in remote islands and provinces. Transportation to, and communication with these remote communities is a significant challenge, that can result in project delays as resources are required for transportation of staff, equipment, materials, or travel of extension agents is limited; reduced interactions with communities and delaying training delivery.

**Mitigation Measures**

UNICEF will engage communities, and provincial authorities will be engaged through the DoHHR and building on the established NIP and DWPW processes to deliver project results – this dispersed approach, making use of existing district level structures. The SPC and DoHHR teams will work with communities in remote areas. This will include community project needs to provincial authorities, and communities based on the relative geographic location of the communities. The SPC and DoHHR teams will ensure that there is a clear understanding of procurement to adjust to needs, and the SPC and DoHHR teams will adjust to transportation and communication challenges.

### Interagency co-ordination

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Medium |

**Description**

Limited coordination between government ministries, SPC communities, NGOs/CBOs, private sector and other stakeholders reduces the efficiency and effectiveness of implementation of project interventions. Further, this could result in delays as decision-making processes are delayed by lack of interagency co-ordination.

**Mitigation Measures**

Strong institutional and implementation arrangements for the project’s programming frameworks ensure effective coordination between government ministries and partners to facilitate constant contact between project partners and stakeholders and ensure that the main stakeholders communicate regularly and coordinate actions. The SPC will work with communities, NGOs/CBOs, private sector entities and supporting organisations (outcome 3) covering data collection, communication, and knowledge


---
**Page 80**
---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 76

#### Insufficient capacity and resources to implement projects

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | High |

**Description**

Communities have insufficient technical capacity and financial/material resources to implement priority projects which may lead to underperforming projects and limited results for local communities.

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

Existing technical capacity within communities, area committees and provincial authorities is quite limited which is why the project will provide training on the development of change grant proposals, project management as well as provided sustained technical support, guidance, and oversight from project support officers. The project will also provide financial resources to communities for the implementation of priority projects.

The DoWR at the national level and the GEM division of the SPC, through the PMU will assist delivery, serve as the financial and technical advisor to communities and provide training on the development of change grant proposals and other stakeholders.

#### Localized data and information are insufficient to help support development of priority projects

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Localized data and information is insufficient to help support development of priority projects leading to underperforming projects, poor project design, strong implementation of sub-activities and ultimately limited climate adaptation outcomes.

The project works directly with national and provincial agencies to catalogue existing climate, vulnerability, and other data that will be used in the development of change grant proposals. This includes identifying key water resources (for example related to groundwater) and identify gaps that need to be addressed. This data will be stored in a a central database which access requires permission. The data use and learning and support local project development.

#### Unfavorable exploitation of groundwater resources

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | High |

**Description**

In the case new boreholes would be needed as part of Outcome 2 activities (to be confirmed during the project preparation phase) then the current water resource sustainability might not be sustainable. For other activities we aim improving the sustainable use of water resources.

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

For projects where new locations will be selected in addition and additional information (incl. water balance and sustainability of groundwater abstraction per aquifer via yield tests) will be provided when developing the funding proposal. The project will also provide training on the development of change grant proposals, project management standards on borehole preparation, and design and installation of solar pump systems. The project design document will include a section on the selection criteria for new locations and the monitoring plan will include thresholds for water users that should be adhered to both during and past project implementation. The monitoring protocols will have clear cut-off points for when extraction is no longer sustainable (e.g., 50% of pre-extraction groundwater thresholds). Extraction does not exceed provisioning rates and ensures that water resources are sustainably managed.

#### Data and sourcing of technical and financial inputs for project activities

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0: PAGE 77

## Description

Procurement for the project in Vanuatu is complicated given the diverse, dispersed geographies of Vanuatu as well as constrained supply chains arising from the COVID-19 pandemic.

### Mitigation measures

The project will support effective procurement by ensuring that all international standards of best practice are adhered to and costs are kept low. The project will also provide project support capacity beyond the time frame of this project which will project will be training on climate change project development and good management practices to ensure more robust procurement practices beyond the project timeline.

### COVID-19 constraints for engagement and operations

#### Category
**Technical and operational**
**Impact**
Medium

#### Description
Due to the timelines of the extended pandemic, stakeholder engagement could still be constrained by COVID-19 and the resulting shifts in staff and protocols. Further, there is a risk to execution officers and beneficiaries on the ground due to the virus through project activities.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The project has been adaptive and proactive in finding pathways to engage with needed stakeholders at the local level during this time of pandemic. The project will continue to use existing networks and local networks. This foundation will help the project to proactively plan for alternative pathways for stakeholder engagement. In addition, during the duration of the pandemic, all necessary safety measures such as social distancing, hand washing, and protective measures will be in place for in person meetings (personal protective equipment use, hand sanitization, social distancing, etc.). Meetings will continue virtually on platforms as appropriate.

### AM/CTF and Prohibited Practices

#### Category
**Impact**
High

#### Description
Potential risks related to Anti-Money Laundering and Combating the Financing of Terrorism (AM/CTF).

#### Mitigation measures

The SPC has adopted Financial Policies for anti-money laundering and counter-terrorism financing to provide the policy framework for managing AM/CTF risk. The Director-General will ensure that all staff are aware of these policies.

Although the risks of money laundering and terrorism financing are considered "low" for Vanuatu, SPC will take steps to mitigate this risk by ensuring that all procurement activities comply with the relevant policies. The Director-General will implement a continuous risk-based approach to identify, assess and understand SPC's money laundering and terrorist financing risk profile. This includes identifying potential risks associated with the entity and undertaking due diligence in engagement with the development partner, supplier, implementation partner or any other party that may be involved in the project. Due diligence undertaken by the Director-General will enable the SPC to assess and mitigate the risk of money laundering and terrorism financing.

The SPC has procedures to ensure their avoidance including a white-collar policy. Manuals and emails for reporting prohibited practices can be found at https://www.spv.vg/index.cfm/0567

### Financial sustainability

#### Category
**Impact**
Medium

#### Description
Lack of financial capacity to ensure maintenance of infrastructure beyond project lifecycle

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

#### 138.
In the development of this funding proposal and its accompanying feasibility study, an initial risk assessment for the intended adaptation activities has been conducted. All project activities are expected to be limited both in scale and scope as well as in their potential environmental or social impacts. In accordance with GCF’s requirements, an initial project screening was undertaken following SPC’s SER Policy, which complies with GCF’s standards. The purpose of this document is to provide a summary of the initial risk assessment for the project and presents a detailed environmental and social risk assessment for this project. Outcomes 1 and 2 are expected to have no significant adverse impacts on the environment or society.

#### 139.
For a full breakdown of assessed risks and mitigation approaches proposed for the project please refer to Annex 4. The following table provides an overview of potential environmental or social risks in the absence of mitigation measures implemented by the project are presented below:

- **Groundwater contamination:** Overexploitation of groundwater tables or surface water sources
- **Pollution of soil, air and water bodies:** Pollution from construction processes
- **Salination of the immediate environment from desalination waste**
- **Soil erosion:**
- **Biodiversity loss in immediate surroundings:**
- **Risk of accidents during construction activities from construction processes**
- **Accidental contamination or pollution of water bodies from waste generated by construction processes:**

#### 140.
To ensure the compliance of investments under Outcome 2 with best practices in environmental and social management, SPC will apply the following ESMF processes for DWSSPs applying for infrastructure development assistance. This includes an initial ESM screening on categorisation of any project activities as high or low risk. The ESMF process will be applied to all projects under Outcome 2 and develop an ESMF for consideration of the investment. Further, all project investments will comply with a set of environmental and social standards (ES Standards) that are aligned with GCF’s Categorization and Development Standards. The project ESM Officer will assess all proposals through the technical assistance note and ensure that they meet the ES Standards. SPC will also provide guidance to the project team on how to mitigate any potential risk and that no excluded criteria are included in proposals. They will also support the DWS to strengthen its internal procedures.

### Disclosure

#### 141.
In the case a situation is considered to meet ESG Category B criteria for environmental or social impact, the ESM Officer must make the report publicly available in line with GCF disclosure policy and were not resisted by any partners or service providers conditional requirements, the ESM Officer will act accordingly:

- **publish the report on SPC’s website**
- **ensure that copies of the report are accessible to potentially affected peoples at a local level in a local language**
- **provide the report to the partner or service providers in line with their requirements under any funding agreement**

### Grievance Redress

#### 142.
SPC is committed to receiving any concerns or grievances from any affected persons or community, related to the implementation of this project. SPC will ensure that all such concerns are addressed and resolved. Affected parties can lodge their concerns by lodging a complaint on SPC’s website or to the PMU and NSPC in accordance with the

### Stakeholder Engagement

143. The consultations on the project were held on July 27 - September 21, 2021. This included a wide range of stakeholders. Supporting this, further provincial level consultations were held on the 20-29th of September 2021.

### G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### Annex 8 - The Gender Assessment and Action Plan – presents the gender mainstreaming exercise undertaken under this project. It includes a summary of the key findings from the gender-mainstreaming exercise in Vanuatu that could limit the potential, inclusiveness and success of the project objective, stemming from:

* Poor understanding of the different roles, rights, needs and opportunities of women and men.
* Lack of awareness about the importance of gender equality in development.
* Gender stereotypes and/or effects vulnerabilities in adaptation design, resilience capacity building and mitigation services.

#### Annex 8.1 - presents an assessment of the different roles, rights, needs and opportunities of women and men and how these are reflected in the project’s objectives and outputs. It also highlights the barriers towards improved gender equality in Vanuatu. Using gender as a lens will also help identify the social relationships between women and men that may be affected by climate change.

#### Annex 8.2 - The project recognizes that access to WASH is fundamental to development, but it can be mobilized as critical climate adaptation measure. It highlights how to engage with broader communities, as well as marginalized groups within those communities, including women and girls.

### G.3 The project adheres to the GCF’s Gender Policy and Action Plan (adopted in 2014, and updated in 2018). SPC’s Gender Policy and Action Plan (GPA) was developed in 2015 and updated in 2019. This document is prepared by the Department of Women’s Affairs of the Ministry of Justice and Community Services. The NGP gender policy and action plan is aligned with the GCF’s GPA, which promotes women’s participation and fostering gender-responsive and community-driven solutions to climate and disaster resilience.

148. Gender barriers identified through stakeholder consultations with Department of Women’s Affairs as well as among communities:
- Different gender groups, particularly women and girls, have gendered needs which remain underrepresented by existing water committees and other decision-making bodies.
- Women continue to be underrepresented in water security and climate adaptation-related decision-making bodies.
- Insufficient, non-resilient WASH infrastructure continues to be compromised by climate risks, increasing time pressures on women.
- Water insecurity indirectly and directly combines to exacerbate gender equality in other sectors (such as limited access to education and health services).

There is political will in involving women in water governance and climate change contexts and in water governance committees. However, able to participate in the registration process on time due to unpaid care work within the household. |

151. This co-benefit will act on the finding that women are underrepresented in water user committees in Vanuatu and establish Rural Water Committees (RWCS) through the DSWSS project process with a minimum of 40% representation. Women’s underrepresentation is detrimental, as UNESCO research has demonstrated that more women in decision-making positions leads to better outcomes for communities. This co-benefit feeds into the legal pre-requirements for the registration of RWCS (40% female representation) in Vanuatu.

152. This will be measured as an independent indicator and will be delivered in tandem with (primary) Outcome 1, which will establish 600 new DSWSS in Vanuatu, alongside providing policy and climate training. Further, the impact of this co-benefit will be monitored through the project’s monitoring framework (e.g., capacity strengthening) provided through Outcome 1 and incorporate findings from the gender consultations.4 Participation by women in water committees is a key indicator for the achievement of the co-benefit and their needs differ. These were summarized by a breakout group (which reflected other stakeholder groups in the Samoas and Talanoa projects in Samoa province too).

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP191

## Proposal name: Enhancing Adaptation and Community Resilience by Improving Water Security in Vanuatu

### Accredited entity: The Pacific Community (SPC)

### Country/ies): Vanuatu

### Project/programme size: Small

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. **The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:**


| Strengths | Points of caution |
|-----------|-------------------|
| The proposal targets vulnerable communities impacted by climate change through stronger flood and drought management systems. | It is important that locally self-sufficient operation and maintenance structures are established, as well as water distribution networks. |
| It proposes an integrated approach to enhance water security by establishing new water management plans and developing essential infrastructure in small islands. | To establish and develop water supply and management systems including infrastructures in small islands, the cost per household is relatively expensive compared to the average. |

2. **The Board will wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the term sheet and in addendum XIII, titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendations,” respectively.**

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background

3. Vanuatu is a small island developing State comprising 83 islands located in the Western Pacific. The country's geographical location, underdeveloped economic structure, and high import dependency make it vulnerable to climate change impacts, including natural disasters and natural hazards. Climate risks and shocks greatly impact hard-earned development gains and threaten food, energy, and water security. This project will focus on access to water resources in communities.

4. The objective of this project is to create safe, climate-resilient, and sustainable water utilization and improved water security at community levels in Vanuatu. This will be done by improving the capacity of local authorities and communities through a participatory approach, namely the Drinking Water Safety and Security Planning (DWSSP) process and the Capital Assistance for Infrastructure Development (CAID). The project will also implement a National Implementation Plan for Safe and Secure Water (NIP). The project will directly benefit 85,650 people (including


---
**Page 93**
---

### Component-by-component analysis

#### Component 1: Communities are empowered to plan and manage climate-resilient water resources (total cost USD 62.9 million; GCF cost USD 54.4 million, or 87% cost)

- **Objective**: To improve water security for rural communities in Vanuatu by enhancing the existing DWSSP process and promoting community-based adaptation solutions in these plans.
Since 2006, the Government of Vanuatu, through the Department of Water Resources (DWR), has been implementing a series of projects under the National Integrated Water Supply Project (NIWSP) process under the NIP. This is one of the official processes by which the government ensures that all planned water supply systems are integrated into the national planning framework.
The DWSSP process is also a community engagement process through which communities identify and discuss threats to safe and reliable access to water, including climate change impacts.

- **DWSSP**: The DWSSP guide day-to-day water supply operation and maintenance (O&M) as well as improvements to community water systems. It includes:
  - *Resilient water management*: This is an internationally accepted approach to manage water resources.
    - This was included in the 3rd edition of the World Health Organization Guidelines on Drinking-water Quality, which was adopted by the WHO and published at the World Health Organization Workshop on Drinking Water Quality Standards and Monitoring in Pacific Island Countries (PACIFIC) in 2015. The guidelines are based on the "5S" (secure, safe, sufficient, sustainable, secure) framework.
    - DWSSP provides for a framework through which community-based WASH systems (new and/or existing) can be planned, designed, constructed, operated, maintained, and scaled up with multiple steps including the formation and training of Rural Water Committees (RWCS), DWSSP facilitators, and other stakeholders. The process is participatory and involves communities in the planning.
    - The DWSSP process improves not only the water planning processes but also community-based WASH systems.

  - *The DWSSP methodology will be updated through this component with a specific focus on strengthening the mainstreaming of climate resilience*. It will address the shortfalls in the existing methodology, including: improving the quality and reliability of data collection protocols; making changes in the design criteria; and providing the relevant staff with exposure and experience to climate change. The updated DWSSP process will be used as a tool to mainstream climate resilience in the plans and will be further refined incrementally on an annual basis over the next 5 years, with finalization of the methodology at the end of this period.
    - The upgrades will enhance the hazard risk assessments and develop improvement and preparedness plans as well as climate change risk and improvement plans. The updated methodology will also include a monitoring framework for implementation. Training will be provided to all the DWSSP facilitators at the national level covering all the provinces.


---
**Page 94**
---

9. Moreover, there are currently 301 completed DWSSPs and, as a result of this proposed project, 600 additional communities will benefit by developing their DWSSPs. These communities will be identified based on prioritization of vulnerability to climate impacts. The 600 additional communities will also receive technical assistance for the development of resilience in planting while the existing DWSSPs will also be retrofitted to better reflect climate risks and vulnerabilities. In addition, all 100 DWSSPs that have been completed up until now will be updated to follow the new climate-resilient methodology. In total, this component will benefit 760 communities or 79,480 direct beneficiaries (including 35,970 women), which is 2.5 per cent of the total population in Vanuatu.

10. Component knowledge and learning sharing activities will be delivered to communities and area administrators within the framework of the existing CAP process.

Component 2: Communities have enhanced rural water infrastructure (total cost USD 10,200 million; GCF cost USD 16.4 million or per cent)

11. Communities in Vanuatu are currently underfunded environment envisioned under this funding proposal in order to strengthen climate-resilient WASH facilities in rural communities of Vanuatu, accounting for about 78 per cent of the total project cost. The aim of this component is two-fold: (i) increase access to safe drinking water and sanitation services through rehabilitation, renovation or construction of small-scale infrastructure projects identified through the country-owned CAP process; and (ii) improve the sustainability of existing WASH facilities by increasing oversight and accountability of drinking water service points and infrastructure (through, particularly, the CAP process).

12. This will be achieved through scaling up the existing CAP that the Government of Vanuatu has successfully deployed to strengthen water facilities in Vanuatu. DWSSPs and the CAP are complementary processes as they both aim at identifying communities with a need for an application for CAP funding. Moreover, communities can apply for CAP funding only after they make an application through the DWSSP process (which includes technical assistance and capacity enhancement). An approved DWSSP ensures that the requested financial assistance addresses priority needs of the community and is aligned to the existing CAP improvement plan or improvement option. Additionally, when communities go through the DWSSP process, it also means that they already have an EWC; whenever a planner applies for ongoing financing, operational funding, or maintenance funding from the Government of Vanuatu, they must ensure that the facility improvements through the CAP funding do not end up being a one-off investment without sustainability. This is because the CAP process is designed to complement component 1 in a sense that the technical capacities enhanced through activities under component 1 will enable the communities to better manage and maintain their existing water supply and sanitation facilities and ensure sustainability of the interventions.

13. Out of the 301 completed DWSSPs, 47 have had their water facilities upgraded through the CAP process (see Table 2). The number of communities that will receive CAP funding will increase to 117 (279 additional DWSSPs) will receive CAP funding, of which 220 are already targeted by component 1 and 58 are new. This means that a total of 346 communities or 35,833 beneficiaries from 1,524 (component 1 and 5,710 additional beneficiaries).

14. This component will also assess the CAP criteria for funding and upgrade the screening process to ensure that only those facilities with high priority are selected for funding. In addition, the Project ESS Officer will support the Provincial Engineers and DWSSP Provincial Officers in providing technical assistance to communities applying through the CAP process for WASH interventions (estimated at less than 5 per cent of applications), based on the trend of CAP applications received so far. The project will also provide capacity building training to communities, which will also contribute to the wider rural communities of Vanuatu when the communities that would not be covered under this project will have their upgrades through other sources of funding.


---
**Page 95**
---

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential

Scale: N/A

Six hundred communities will be direct beneficiaries as part of component 1 (improved planning and community-based actions on climate-resilient water management), of which 220 have existing WSSPs, while the remaining 380 are new communities that will benefit from this project. An additional 50 communities with existing WSSPs will be targeted under component 2 during the first year of implementation (2019–2020). The number of communities to be supported in each phase will be updated to follow the new climate-resilient methodology in component 1. This makes a total of 650 communities that are expected to benefit from this project, which will directly benefit 85,650 beneficiaries (including 42,025 women), which is around 27 per cent of the total population of Vanuatu. Indirect beneficiaries include the rest of the rural population of Vanuatu and 198,080 people living in urban areas. The project will also contribute to enhanced institutional capacities and processes leading to climate-resilient water security for communities.

This project will address three water-related impacts of climate change in Vanuatu, which are increases in: (i) water-related disasters; (ii) areas suffering from water stress; and (iii) loss of livelihoods. These impacts are among the key adaptation interventions identified in Vanuatu’s GCF country programme (pipeline project A61). Water security is vital for community resilience in the long term as well as in the immediate aftermath


---
**Page 96**
---

## 3.2 Paradigm shift potential

The project is a climate adaptation investment aiming to enhance climate resilience and increase water security of rural communities. The project will ground-WASH infrastructure investments in the context of climate change vulnerability considerations within DWSSPs, from training of trainers, community training and facilitation to data collection, improvement planning, and O&M.

### Scale: N/A

By scaling up the number of climate-resilient DWSSPs (registered component 1) and then delivering a more resilient infrastructure and system approach (component 2), the project will strengthen a whole-of-government approach to achieving climate resilience. This will be achieved by strengthening existing governance, planning, and decision-making processes that are decentralized, transparent, participatory, community-focused, gender-mainstreamed, and inclusive. The project will also reduce the vulnerability of water systems, interruption to water services during and after disasters will be reduced or avoided. Therefore, the project will reduce the costs associated with continual repairing and rebuilding after disasters. This will lead to a more sustainable approach to delivering new WASH infrastructure and sustainable exploration of water sources.

## 3.3 Sustainable development potential

This project will generate significant environmental, social and economic benefits that will contribute to positive sustainable development outcomes for Vanuatu in the long term. It will reduce vulnerability by strengthening the capacity of communities to manage climate change and vulnerability considerations within DWSSPs, from training of trainers, community training and facilitation to data collection, improvement planning, and O&M.

### Scale: N/A

Moreover, the project will enhance the equitable access to water in Vanuatu, which is one of the basic human rights. It will target the most vulnerable groups in rural communities, which is expected to generate significant social benefits including positive health outcomes and improved livelihoods.


---
**Page 97**
---

## 3.4 Needs of the recipient

### Scale: N/A

The project addresses the fundamental human right of access to safe drinking water. Communities that have elevated levels of vulnerability, low access to WASH services and are exposed to climate change risks will be prioritized for targeted interventions. The project is climate resilient water management interventions that will be directly targeted, the project will ultimately benefit all rural communities by strengthening the enabling environment for resilient interventions. The project will address these challenges by introducing safe managed and climate-resilient and gender-equitable water safety and security in Vanuatu.

### 3.5 Country ownership

The project is fully aligned with the Government of Vanuatu's climate change strategies and policies, including its Climate Change and Disaster Risk Reduction Policy 2016–2030 as well as the national adaptation programme of action and the NDC, both of which reference water resource management in rural communities. In addition to this, the project aligns with Vanuatu’s National Sustainable Development Plan 2016-2030 and National Water Policy 2017–2030.

The project directly responds to the Government of Vanuatu's call for every rural community to have a DWSPP by 2030, as outlined in the Water Resource Management Act and WASH Sector Strategy. The project will be implemented through the development and implementing the NIP and DWSSPC processes, through which the project will scale up systems improvements and WASH services in rural communities. The project will also work with local government along with other national stakeholders to address climate change risks and impacts on water


---
**Page 98**
---

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

**Scale:** N/A

The project will build climate-resilience and adaptation-capacities in Vanuatu’s WASH sector through the implementation of proven and efficient water management techniques. This will follow the nationally owned DWSSP processes that support the identification of locally led interventions, and, as doing so, lay the foundations for further scaling up beyond the project lifetime.

The project-level economic net present value is positive at USD 103,150,065 and the programme-level NPV is positive at USD 248,790,000. This means that all interventions are economically viable and can be justified on economic grounds, even though non-investment budget costs (including co-financed) have no envisaged direct benefits.

The project will also contribute to the overall efficiency and effectiveness of the WASH sector in Vanuatu through which the project will identify and prioritize communities in need of system improvements and the activities to bring about such improvements. The project has already identified 10,524 households that cumulatively spent almost USD 1 million of their own money on low-cost interventions.

The project is expected to be highly effective because it will provide a replicable model for efficiently scaling up investments by fine-tuning and mobilizing the DWSSP process through GEF funds.


### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

Project background: The project aims to improve water security for rural communities by targeting households in need of system improvements, which will be implemented throughout Vanuatu with rehabilitation, upgrading or expansion of water infrastructure such as concrete protection on spring sources, well head aprons, and rainwater harvesting. In addition, small-scale power-renewable technologies (e.g., solar-powered pumps) will be installed where appropriate.

The project will focus on capacity-building of provincial and national WASH authorities as well as communities to plan for and implement system improvements in a way that reduces GHG emissions and minimizes GHI emissions owing to installation of solar power. Social co-benefits are expected from reduced waterborne diseases, improved health outcomes, increased food security through timely planting and harvesting, and time-saving from collecting water from remote and unsanitary sources.

Environmental and social risk category: The confirmed and assigned environmental and social risk category is B (medium). The project is in the accredited entity’s (AE) accredited level II. It is within the accredited entity’s (AE) accredited level II. This is rationalized by rehabilitation or construction of small-scale water infrastructure, which has a low potential for adverse impacts on people due to risks and impacts that are generally reversible and site-specific, which can also be readily managed.

Safeguard instruments: The AE provided an environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) and management plan with the funding proposal. The document provides an overview of the Vanuatu context for environmental and social risk assessment, potential

### Compliance with GCF’s ESS Standards

The paragraphs below identify the key risks and/or impacts as regards the GCF ESS standards and how they are addressed in the proposal:

#### ES1: Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts

Compliance with GCF’s ESS standards is being addressed through the AE's screening tool mentioned in the document. Initial screening will be carried out by DWSP consultants, and if risks are identified as moderate environmental and social risk level, they require site-specific ESAs and environmental management plans (EMP). The EMPs will address all significant environmental and social impacts presented in the safeguards document based on interventions proposed during consultation with communities. These measures will ensure that project activities do not adversely impact community health and safety, and biodiversity. The description of baseline environmental and social conditions provided in the document is very minimal and will need to be elaborated at the subproject level.

#### ES2: Labour and Working Conditions

Construction-related activities under complete supervision by qualified personnel with adequate training and experience will be remediated by providing workers with personal protective equipment to wear during construction, safety training, and emergency plans in case of accidents. Materials to be used in the project are expected to be sourced from local suppliers and contractors who have a track record of adhering to health and safety practices. The AE is recommended to establish a process for assessing risks related to labour force recruitment and retention.

#### ES3: Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention

Installation of new and rehabilitation of existing wells and boreholes will be supported by the project where a water balance analysis has been conducted, and it was determined that groundwater recharge or long-term monitoring will be undertaken. Water pumps that will be installed by the project will be powered by solar panels to avoid pollution from diesel fuel. The AE is recommended to phase out diesel-fuelled pumps to eliminate spillage of oil into community water sources. Measures are also proposed for the installation of latrine facilities and waste management systems that will be installed by the project to avoid pollution from latrines including preventing vectors, pests, and livestock access to human excreta. The AE is recommended to ensure that no hazardous materials are generated or released as a result of construction activities. Additionally, the operational phase of the project will also generate waste from items such as plastic water tanks and solar components, including panels, after their lifespan has expired. The AE is recommended to provide guidance on the disposal of solar panels and batteries and handle any inconsistencies with best practices that are identified in the environmental management plan.

#### ES4: Community Health, Safety, and Security

Construction, retrofitting, and installation activities may create temporary noise and air pollution for local communities. Noise will be limited by using appropriate construction techniques and equipment. Air pollution measures will be outlined at the subproject level. Construction may also pose a safety risk to community members due to the use of heavy machinery and other hazards associated with construction injuries. Water storage facilities will be avoided to exclude breeding vectors that may cause diseases such as mosquito-borne illnesses, which are prevalent in the region. The AE is recommended to address potential or tribal disputes particularly where shared water resources are involved. Mitigation measures include community consultations, extensive community consultations, and obtaining community no-objection to selected sites.

#### ES5: Land Acquisition and Involuntary Resettlement

Construction-related activities will be widely consulted with communities and a memorandum of understanding will


---
**Page 100**
---

be signed between hWfK community leaders and landowners to secure land that may be required for water supply systems and to allow access for O&M purposes. The project excludes activities that will result in physical or economic displacement. However, there is a possibility that project activities could lead to the loss of livelihoods due to competition with other resources or place constraints on some specific land uses temporarily or permanently such as cattle grazing. In such cases, SPC will ensure that all affected parties are informed and given due diligence where this risk is identified at the subproject level and apply necessary mitigation measures.

### e5: Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources.
There is a potential negative risk on biodiversity as a result of brine disposal from desalination plants. The project will ensure that brine discharge minimizes an increase in salt concentration where brine is discharged. Abstraction of surface water may also potentially impact marine life negatively.

### e6: Community Engagement and Consultation with Indigenous Peoples.
The project is not expected to negatively impact indigenous peoples and takes place within a national context where around 95 per cent of the country is indigenous, mainly mVamani, with other groups that include the Kikuyu, Meru, and Embu. The project will ensure that all community consultations are conducted with the representation of all indigenous groups within a community. The SE notes that Indigenous Peoples’ rights are respected in the project through engagement with communities. In addition, community engagement will ensure that construction does not occur on land where there is no free, prior, and informed consent.

### e7: Cultural Heritage.
The AEV due diligence indicates that project activities are unlikely to directly impact any areas of cultural heritage value. Nonetheless, a chance finds procedure has been outlined in the document in case tangible cultural heritage is found at the project site.

### e8: Sexual exploitation, sexual abuse, and sexual harassment.
The revised GCF Environmental and Social Policy adopted by decision B2M-201/21 requires safeguarding from sexual exploitation and abuse (SEA) and sexual harassment (SH). The project’s ESMP has integrated various forms of SEA as one of the social risks/impacts and identified safeguards to address them. These include training for all project staff, contractors, consultants, facilitators, service providers and communities directly targeted are trained on prevention of SEA prior to their engagement in any project activities. The project will ensure that these trainings lead the development of SEAH protocols into the DWSS processes and provide training to the DWSS facilitators and trainers. All these activities will contribute towards the prevention side of SEA and SH. In addition, the project has also embedded SEAH responses in its grievance mechanism. This will allow for survivors to report incidents of sexual exploitation and abuse specific SEAH protocol to ensure a survivor-centred approach is in place. This will allow for survivors to be referred to appropriate services and support as well as ensuring that they enhance confidence in the survivor to come forward and log a formal complaint as well as be assured of confidentiality, privacy, and anonymity. The project has also embedded specific GMS processes. This will apply to all grievance mechanisms that will be controlled by SPC or the project management team. This ensures that the rights and responsibilities of the survivors are prioritized. This also ensures that their rights are upheld; privacy and confidentiality are maintained; non-discrimination measures are put in place; and translation opportunities are provided where necessary. The information related to GMS and/or SEAH cases will be kept confidential and identities protected. Moreover, SPC will ensure that all complaints are recorded and followed up on through the complaint processes. Should a case of SEAH or GBV be submitted, SPC will carry out duty of care to the survivors in line with its policies including, among others and where relevant, support for

### Gender policy

#### 4.2 Gender policy

The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan, and therefore completely complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy and action plan.

The gender assessment described describes the gender issues at the country level and indicates how they are being addressed in the project. The project will practice to promote gender equality and women’s empowerment. Vanuatu ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women in 1995 and has a National Gender Equality Policy that aims to engage in solutions to climate and disaster resilience that


---
**Page 102**
---

gender-responsive and community-driven. This project is expected to contribute towards that goal.

The assessment indicated that the climate crisis affects women in a unique and disproportionate manner. The analysis of the vulnerability assessment revealed the needs of women and other vulnerable groups in the WASH process. The findings indicate lack of access to water, sanitation, and hygiene services for women and girls who are the primary collectors of water for the family as in many countries. The assessment also pointed out that women and girls face a range of challenges related to their roles as men and, other vulnerable groups. Some of the specific needs of women that are not met are for example, lack of privacy and dignity in the use of WASH facilities such as management of menstrual hygiene, child-related water use, and separate showers with dignity. These needs are not fully considered in the design of the final infrastructure constructed. In addition to these issues, women face challenges related to access to safe drinking and non-potable water due to physiological requirements. Other issues presented related to the safety of infrastructure design and implementation such as lack of privacy and dignity for women. Some improvements to increase safety for women. This is in terms of lighting, improved locks and doors at entry points, and other measures that will be considered during the construction.

The analysis also indicated that women are more vulnerable than men to increased incidents of GBV. In addition to safety and infrastructure design issues, women's representation in the same household as men is a challenge for water service providers when it comes to adaptation-related decision-making bodies and in water user committees despite registering better performance on other aspects such as age, education level, and composition of factors raised earlier; effects women's ability to access safe water. Furthermore, they spend a lot of time in search of water. The limited year-round services at most points water, inadequate availability of water for domestic use, and lack of privacy and dignity for women in communities, and women in particular as it is their job to collect water (five hours per day). The water fetches are also long distances from the household, especially for people with disabilities as well as collecting firewood, washing clothes and gardening. Gender mainstreaming has been a challenge during infrastructure design and implementation.

Having noted the gaps, the gender action plan was developed with relevant activities, indicators, and targets. A gender gap analysis indicated that women were represented in the highest decision-making bodies registered better outcomes in terms of water provisions and services. In view of this, it is recommended to ensure that women are adequately represented in all levels of representation of women in water committees at least with 40 per cent participation, while ensuring that women's voices are heard during infrastructure design and implementation. The infrastructure design and implementation. The improved security and privacy features—such as adequate lighting, locks, doors, and other measures—that will be considered during the construction will address some of the challenges faced by women. The action plan includes activities towards increasing access to water for women and girls in communities through community-based initiatives while still assuring further gender-based needs. Also included are activities towards effective knowledge management, collection of data, and documentation of lessons learned and best practices. The implementation of this action plan will be monitored on a regular basis and will be facilitated by various groups with different roles including the Gender Officer and the


---
**Page 103**

Department of Women’s Affairs. Meanwhile, technical assistance on gender responsive actions will be provided by UNICEF in addition to the Gender Protection Cluster, which will provide sensitization and advocacy support during trainings to ensure meaningful participation of assets.

54. The AE is recommended to clearly articulate and demonstrate activities that speak to gender transformative and gender responsive actions.
