### 4.3 Risks

#### 4.3.1 Overall project assessment (medium risk)

55. GCF is requested to provide a grant of USD 23 million to enhance climate resilience and increase water security of rural communities by investing in WASH infrastructure and water management systems. The AE will be providing co-financing equivalent to USD 6.4 million on financing-by way of grant and in-kind contribution. UNICEF and the AE will be providing third-party contributions equivalent to USD 8.7 million and USD 10.9 million, respectively.

#### 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current project (medium risk)

56. PC, as well as the AE, will serve as both All and one of the EEs for this project. Headquartered in New Caledonia, it has been operating in the Pacific for over 70 years. Its Melanesian Regional Office in Vanuatu creates close linkages with the AE and the PMU in the country.

57. The Government of Vanuatu, through the Ministry of Finance (MoF) and the DoWR, will be a co-financing partner to this project. The MoF is responsible for providing funding that identifies gaps and provides recommendations. However, the assessment acknowledges that the country lacks capacity in water supply engineering and in climate change and vulnerability assessments, which are critical components of the project.

#### 4.3.3 Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)

58. Project delays in remote locations: The AE identified implementation delays in remote islands and provinces as a main challenge and high probable risk. This is also often associated with limited access to water infrastructure and lack of trained personnel. All projects being implemented in the country will be coordinated through relevant line ministries, including the MoF for logistics and procurement.

#### 58. O&M risk: The AE identified difficulties to maintain the new and upgraded equipment due to limited financing and/or expertise. A financing strategy for O&M is part of the O&M plan. It is recommended that a clear strategy on operating and maintaining the water system will be provided. Although direct revenue will not be generated from this program, it is expected that the AE will provide an annual budget which will depend on the financial mechanism agreed by the community through the DFWEES processes. However, as O&M responsibilities largely lie with the community, the project identified that there may be a risk of non-compliance and/or lack of maintenance in remote areas. If unexpected costs arise and/or migration of the trained community member happens, there will be constraints to ensure O&M beyond the project implementation period.

## Technical risks: The installation of desalination systems or boreholes would require upgrades, repairs of existing distribution pipelines or new distribution systems. This will require well-designed construction plans and technical precision to be in line with the technical standards of the DoWRA. Monitoring groundwater levels and ensuring that there is no apparent overextraction in the aquifer and water quality monitoring will be critical. The project will rely upon the technical capacity of the DoWRA who will be responsible for supervision and mentoring.

### Project viability and concessionality: Vanuatu is a small island developing State and funds required to implement the project are expected to be beyond what domestic resources can provide, even with concessional loans and/or United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) grants support. The cost of the investment will be beyond what households can afford (ranging from 657 per cent to 1034 per cent of ability to pay). Therefore, full grant financing is required for this project. The Government of Vanuatu has requested a grant of up to 98 per cent and the AE assessed that the adaptation project is economically viable and efficient.

## Legal assessment

The accreditation master agreement was signed with the AF on 8 November 2019 and it became effective on 10 April 2020.

The AF has provided a legal opinion signed by George Ramsay, Principal Legal Officer of the Aid Authority, confirming that it has the necessary approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.

The proposed project will be implemented in Vanuatu. GCF has signed a bilateral agreement with the Government of Vanuatu; however, such agreement is still pending its entry into force. This means that the privileges and immunities are only effectively conferred on GCF when it enters into force. Therefore, for the time being, among other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be managed.

The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism and the Independent Integrity Unit both have expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or investigate any alleged breaches of the rules and regulations due to GCF’s privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by GCF are made


---
**Page 108**
---

only after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP191

## Proposal name: Enhancing Adaptation and Community Resilience by Improving Water Security in Vanuatu

### Accredited entity: The Pacific Community (SPC)

#### Country/ies: Vanuatu
#### Project/programme size: Small

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential

This funding proposal for a small project in environmental and social safeguards category B is submitted by Vanuatu, a small island developing state in the South Pacific, with 65 inhabited islands spread over an area of approximately 20,879 km². This adaptation project, with The Pacific Community (SPC) as the accredited entity (AE) and also one of two executing entities (EEs), is being implemented by the Government of Vanuatu through the Ministry of Environment and the Department of Water Resources (DWR). SPC is a regional direct access entry to the GCF, and it is expected that this project will be replicated in other Pacific Island countries and territories members, when it provides scientific and technical advice and support. The total cost of the funding proposal is USD 28.29 million, as detailed in paragraph 3B below.

The project aims to enhance resilience against water-related impacts from observed and projected climate change effects in Vanuatu, which are increases in: (1) water scarcity; (2) extreme rainfall events; and (3) sea-level rise, leading to increased cyclone-induced fatalities. The project aims to enhance climate resilience and increase water security of 506 vulnerable rural communities across all six provinces of the country. Project activities will be undertaken through a multi-sectoral approach involving government agencies, civil society, and private sector stakeholders. These include: (1) enhancing water management planning systems; (2) enhancing community participation in the governance of water resources; and (3) improving access to safe drinking water for rural communities. The project is being implemented through national policy frameworks such as the National Implementation Plan for Safe and Secure Water Supply, the National Climate Change Strategy, and the National Adaptation Plan. It will also build up the CAP programme to improve drinking water supply through investment in new rainwater harvesting systems and other water storage facilities.

Observed changes in the climate of Vanuatu show that there has been a gradual change in climatic conditions over the last 70 years. The temperature has increased at approximately 0.5°C per decade, with an increase from 24 to 26°C during this period. Temperature increase will cause higher evapotranspiration which, when combined with increased frequency and intensity of extreme rainfall events, will lead to more frequent and agricultural droughts. Despite no general upward trend in overall precipitation being observed or projected, extreme rainfall and tropical cyclone events are predicted to become more intense and frequent due to climate change. This is consistent with the El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) phases where El Niño causes significant drought and La Niña significant heavy precipitation. The project will address these challenges by implementing water harvesting systems in rural communities, increasing access to safe drinking water through rainwater harvesting, and improving water security.


---
**Page 110**
---

more severe drought and category 4 and 5 cyclones that can damage water infrastructure, often occurred with Cyclone Pam (2015) and Cyclone Harold (2020). Table 1 in the funding proposal titled "Climate and non-climate drivers impacting drinking water and WASH infrastructure in Vanuatu" shows how climate change will increase extreme weather events such as increased intensity of extreme precipitation events, extreme wind speed in tropical cyclones, increased frequency of drought spells, and sea level rise on the structure and functioning of water and sanitation infrastructure.

These changes are compounding existing development challenges related to water and sanitation infrastructure. For example, an increase in temperature will increase the incidence of flooding and induce greater damage to water utilities, pipes, and community-based water systems. Similarly, increased frequency of droughts and associated damages by extreme winds can result in redundant infrastructure, cutting communities off from water supplies. Soil erosion caused by heavy rainstorms with human-caused deforestation or poor land management can clog drains and latrines, resulting in stagnant, contaminated water that puts the health of the community at risk.

The combination of climate change and other development challenges such as population growth, urbanization, and increased demand for water will have a direct impact on drinking water and WASH infrastructure. The frequency and intensity of extreme weather events associated with major El Niño events are predicted to cause increased incidence of meteorological drought. Consequently, climate impacts directly threaten water security and sanitation across the country.

Despite ongoing investments by government and its financial and technical partners in infrastructure development, the maintenance and management of drinking water systems across many of the country's 65 islands is a major challenge, intensified by climate change. A number of factors contribute to this challenge including lack of capacity for maintenance. Awareness and capacity are limited at community levels due to a lack of backstopping and outreach from government agents to adequately design climate-resilient water infrastructure.

In remote communities, there is insufficient access to piped water, an insufficiently maintained or lack of WASH infrastructure (a development challenge), and current design standards do not account for the impacts of climate change. In addition, many communities are located in areas with various financial constraints; limited technical capacity at area council, provincial and national government levels; and a lack of institutional arrangements to address the lack of robust and accessible data for integrated and climate-resilient water management.

The project theory of change is that if local planning, decision-making and water management practices are improved, then greater resilience in terms of both quantity and quality of water resources will be provided for the improvement of water resources. THEN climate resilience and water security of vulnerable communities can be enhanced. The project aims to achieve this through a CARE OF Enhanced community awareness and capacities, upgraded infrastructure to address climate risks and vulnerabilities, and strengthened national water governance capacities. Three components of the project aim at the following outcomes:

(a) Outcome 1: Communities are empowered to plan and manage climate-resilient water resources;

(b) Outcome 2: Communities have enhanced climate-resilient rural water infrastructure; and

(c) Outcome 3: Provincial and national institutions are strengthened to address climate risks associated with water security.

Adaptation impact: The project is expected to have significant adaptation impact by enhancing water safety and security and community resilience in the long term, in the face of


---
**Page 111**

### 12.1 Paradigm shift potential

As the per funding proposal, the project will transition the current precarious water security situation in Vanuatu to a more reliable and predictable supply of safe water. This will occur through climate-resilient, community-led planning and management practices (outcome 1), the delivery of climate-resilient infrastructure and system improvements (outcome 2), and the strengthening of institutional capacity for delivering climate-resilient water security benefits (outcome 3). By building and operating climate-resilient water infrastructure, interruption to services will be reduced. This will also reduce the costs associated with continuing repair and rebuilding after disasters, allowing the limited resources available for investments in new WASH infrastructure and sustainable exploration of water sources.

### Climate mainstreaming is still nascent in Vanuatu, while climate considerations are increasingly being integrated into DWSWP projects. The DWSWP has identified significant needs for improvements to deliver climate-resilient water security benefits for rural communities. In the context of this project, the funding proposal argues that the project will strengthen a whole-of-government approach to achieving climate-resilient water safety and security through the Delphi with

1.3 Sustainable development potential

The project contributes to three Sustainable Development Goals: SDG 1 (climate action), SDG 5 (clean water and sanitation), and SDG 2 (gender equality) through its objective of building climate-resilient and gender-equitable water safety and security in Vanuatu.

### Social benefits and social inclusion:
The project will contribute to equitable access to water and sanitation for all, including women and people with disabilities. Significant social benefits resulting from project activities include positive health outcomes, improved livelihoods, and enhanced community resilience. The design of infrastructure for drinking water provision will include posting layout plans at the community level for easy reference by all stakeholders. The project team has engaged with people with disabilities to object to the designs and plans if they feel their needs have not been adequately addressed.

### Gender equality benefits:
As outlined in the Gender Assessment and Action Plan, Vanuatu's limited WASH sector capacity is a significant barrier to achieving gender equality. Traditionally, gender roles typically involve women and girls putting in more labour and spending longer hours collecting water than men. This can lead to increased working times to reach source water during dry seasons or climate-induced emergencies can increase instances of violence against women and girls. The project aims to reduce these burdens and risks by enhancing sustained access to good quality drinking water (clean, safe, and reliable).

The project aims to achieve improved social standing for women through their visible and meaningful roles in RWCS, with 60 per cent female members mandatory for the committees' registration. The project team has identified that a minimum of two-thirds female representation by itself would be sufficient to ensure meaningful participation and leadership roles for women, given the current capacity constraints within the sector. The project will also provide training on women's time for unpaid care work within the household; the AE highlighted that UNICEF is currently implementing a similar initiative in the region.

### Environmental co-benefits:
The project will reduce the negative environmental impacts of poor wastewater management by improving water management behaviour and practices, including better waste disposal. The project will also contribute to improved water quality through better protection of water sources and forested water catchment areas through low- and no-cost community improvements, resulting in improved environmental quality.

---
**Page 114**
---

21. In response to a query from the independent TAP about the risk of overextraction of groundwater, the AE responded that "potential negative groundwater impacts need to be checked and confirmed during the individual investment preparation. ESS screening and impact assessment should be conducted in advance to identify potential risks and develop measures to mitigate the risk of overexploitation of groundwater resources. Monitoring of abstraction rates and water levels is also recommended as part of the monitoring plan, with training and equipment being provided to them during the water committee management training."

Furthermore, the AE noted that the Water Sector Strategy (WSS) for Vanuatu 2015-2030 will assist the RWC to develop appropriate rules for their water systems to mitigate the risk of over extraction of groundwater resources, including the provision of equipment and training to measure abstraction rates and water levels. The WSS also includes provisions on water restrictions and drops in groundwater levels.

23. Economic and social benefits: The direct impacts of limited water supply and water security issues such as drinking water and inadequate WASH infrastructure are increased morbidity (diarrhoea, stunting and other illnesses) and increased mortality, among both children and adults. These health problems lead to increased healthcare costs for the government (as improved water service and sanitation practices lead to improved health outcomes), and reduced productivity in the workforce due to illness and death. Improved access to safe and secure water supply especially in the aftermath of cyclones will lead to avoidance of morbidity and mortality, as well as increased productivity. The provision of resilient infrastructure and training on its maintenance will reduce the level of damage inflicted.

25. The independent TAP pointed out the importance of a well-planned programme to promote sustainable development and ensure that there is adequate financial and institutional accompaniment to water provision, in order to improve the health of the population. The AE acknowledged this point and noted that Vanuatu has been making progress on this front through a continuous push by Vanuatu's Ministry of Health and the WASH sector to promote handwashing, supported by the Water Sector Strategy (WSS) for Vanuatu 2015-2030. This includes the building of area level sanitariums (funded by Asian Development Bank). This has resulted in all business houses requiring handwashing facilities and has significantly improved awareness on the importance of handwashing among the population.

The AE also reported that UNICEF is scaling up a WASH in Schools programme to all provinces, and it is currently being implemented in 10 schools. The WSS for Vanuatu 2015-2030 includes demand and supply chain for sanitation and hygiene, which will lead to a costed implementation plan.

26. Traditional knowledge: In response to a query from the independent TAP about the lack of detail in the funding proposal about how island communities' traditional knowledge would be captured during the project design phase, the AE responded that traditional knowledge is essential to ensuring safety and security of the water supply, and that such knowledge will be captured as part of the ESS screening. The WSS for Vanuatu 2015-2030 includes a detailed methodology including finding out about the different traditional uses of different water sources and their reasoning for using them for these purposes; the identification of potential risks to safety, if the existing drinking source is contaminated or located around potential land disposals (previous or current) and information on if the water source is in a taboo area that means it cannot be developed.

28. The independent TAP assesses sustainable development potential as high.


### Needs of the recipient

29. Rural communities in Vanuatu use a combination of groundwater, surface water and rainwater for their drinking water supply. Groundwater is used by 70% of rural households either directly or through processes. Over 24,000 households across the country are reliant on either rainwater or surface


---
**Page 115**
---

water for their primary drinking water supply. Also, one-third of households have no alternative source of drinking water. The project addresses the primary challenge for water safety and security in Vanuatu that, while access to a proximate source of drinking water is high (94 per cent according to the 2018 World Bank Water Supply and Sanitation Assessment Report), the water quality is not always good. Moreover, water supply is increasingly unreliable on many islands, as climate change impacts are already being felt in Vanuatu’s freshwater infrastructure systems—for example, discharge of polluted wastewater into rivers or deforestation causing increased sedimentation, erosion, and reduction in rainfall.

Water scarcity, both for household use and sanitation, and water shortages are common during the dry season. The DWIR Water Resources Inventory indicates that many of the water resources in Vanuatu are highly vulnerable to climate change impacts. Furthermore, rainfall often manifests as intense periods of extreme precipitation, leading to floods that further compromise water supply systems. In addition, surface water supply is frequently compromised in the aftermath of cyclone events, which are expected to increase in frequency with climate change, posing serious public health risks. The project will address these needs through improved water infrastructure and sanitation services for urban areas, peri-urban, and developed levels, as well as through direct improvements to drinking water and WASH systems.

Vanuatu has the highest score in the world on the global disaster risk index (a score of 49.74), indicating a high level of exposure to natural hazards and a correspondingly high degree of loss if from natural hazards. This is intensified by the predicted impacts of climate change discussed above. The project will address these needs through improved water infrastructure, as well as to be addressed to build community resilience to these impacts. Financing this adaptation gap has posed significant challenges for Vanuatu, which has been unable to access concessional financing since its graduation in 2019 from least developed country to lower middle-income country status.

While steadily increasing economic and social indicators over recent decades are a positive trend, the vulnerability of Vanuatu’s population is still high. The most vulnerable people and communities will require adaptation assistance for the foreseeable future, given their exposure to climate change impacts as well as other natural hazards and other hazards. The economic impact of the COVID-19 pandemic—with the collapse of the tourism industry—has been significant in Vanuatu. In April 2020, Vanuatu was struck by Tropical Cyclone Harold which in April 2020—pushed the domestic budget deficit into debt in 2020/21 and may have set Vanuatu back to gross domestic product levels typical of least developed countries.

The World Bank Systematic Country Diagnostic for Vanuatu (which was conducted in 2014 with a focus on water, sanitation, and hygiene) identified the sector as being very high in relation to the size of the economies of these island nations, the absolute size of the public sector is still very small and often lacks the financial and human resources needed to provide adequate services. The project will address this by providing technical assistance and CAP requests for investment in DWIR’s water infrastructure. This has induced downscaling of infrastructure projects from national scale to district level, which has been successful. In turn, led to increased vulnerability as exemplified by the aftermath of Tropical Cyclone Harold. Financial constraints on the public sector have therefore directly presents a barrier to climate-resilient water security and sanitation.

Overall, based on the above, the needs of the recipients are rated as high.


### 1.5 Country ownership

The project is listed as the first priority project in the pipeline presented in Vanuatu’s GCF Country Programme and has been fully co-developed with DWIR, along with other national stakeholders. By addressing climate change risks and impacts on water management, and by


---
**Page 116**
---

working directly with vulnerable rural communities on community-based adaptation activities,
the project is fully aligned with the Government of Vanuatu’s climate change strategies and policies,
including the Climate Change and Disaster Risk Reduction Policy 2016–2030 as well as the National Water Resource Management Strategy 2015–2030, which both reference water resource management as a top priority. In addition, the project is fully in line with Vanuatu’s National Sustainable Development Plan 2016-2030, and National Water Policy 2017-2030.

As a further sign of country commitment, the Government of Vanuatu has committed USD 8 million to the project as part of its contribution to the Green Climate Fund (GCF). This
finance will flow mainly through the CAP, which is part of NIP, to support development of GEF projects. The GEF contribution will be used for two components: component one
will include operation and maintenance costs for the installed equipment after completion of infrastructure works; and component two entitles the country to use the equipment lifetime.
This financing is only post-grant GCF investment through the CAP and does not finance
the infrastructure works. RGVS leverages the infrastructure works to generate the required finance for long-term sustainability.

The project responds directly to the Government of Vanuatu’s goal for every rural community to have access to WASH by 2035, as outlined in the Water Resource Management Act and the Water Sector Action Plan (WSAP). The project will be implemented in phases. At the end
of the project, following the NIP and DWNR processes, through which the project will scale up system improvements, the project will be handed over to the Department of Water Resources and
Development (DWRD), which is also the lead coordinator of the Vanuatu WASH Cluster, acting as the secretariat and chair for the cluster. The DWRD has a strong track record in responding to water sector emergencies that the country experiences, and DWNR is experienced in responding to water access and sanitation needs during climate change events. There is thus already strong ownership
of such processes and activities by DWNR, which this project will build upon.

Country ownership of the project is thus seen to be high.


### Efficiency and effectiveness

The results of the economic analysis conducted for the project clearly show that the
project has a positive net present value (NPV) of USD 157,086/5 and the programme-level Economic Internal Rate of Return at 20 per cent. The conclusion is that the proposed
programme is financially viable on both technical and economic grounds, including the non-investment budget costs and co-finance for which no direct benefit was generated. According to the AI, the analysis included conservative assumptions and not all benefits were included in the economic analysis; however, it is expected that these will be captured through the co-benefits.

The total project cost is USD 102.29 million, 82.5 per cent of which is a USD 23.23 million GEF grant contribution. The remaining USD 4.94 billion, or 17.5 per cent of the total project
cost, is made up of grant and in-kind contributions from the Government of Vanuatu (USD 4.4 million) and other donors (USD 0.54 million). This means that 82.5 per cent of the total GEF
contributions to infrastructure in component two, through the ongoing CAP, cover less than 26 per cent of the project cost. The remaining 73.5 per cent is covered by Vanuatu’s own financial position.
The structure of the project as 100 per cent grant-financed represents an appropriate balance between the need to ensure that the project is financially viable and any revenues generated from sale of water to communities will be ploughed straight back into
operating expenses (which revenues will likely not even be able to cover fully).


---
**Page 117**
---

The independent TAP did, however, raise a query with the AE about why as much as 100 per cent of the cost of water infrastructure in component 2 was being covered by GCF funding, when section B6 of the funding proposal on efficiency and effectiveness states that “the intervention is not expected to address any of the social or institutional barriers that hinder communities from coping with the additional burden of climate change [the independent TAP]”. The independent TAP also noted that the Independent Evaluation Unit (IEU) acknowledges that “one of the primary barriers in Vanuatu is limited WASH infrastructure and water supply services”.

The IEU further states: “In 2015, only 38 per cent of households had access to piped water and less than 40 per cent of existing boreholes were functional. The islands have fragile water resources as a result of a combination of factors unrelated to climate change, such as the small scale, lack of storage and limited infrastructure for water treatment and distribution.” (IEU, 2016: 38). As such, the IEU states that “the key challenge here, i.e. lack of water security and safety, is primarily a development challenge rather than an adaptation challenge”. The IEU also notes that the project does not implement Adaptation resources, with GCF’s climate finance used only for the portion of the problem attributed to climate change.

In answer to this, SPC argued that “the fact that communities have fragile water systems is increasingly relevant climatically on its own accord, as it directly enhances their vulnerability to climate change impacts such as increased frequency and intensity of droughts, floods, cyclones and inundation events. As such, water security and safety are directly at risk from climate change in Vanuatu”. The IEU also notes that “the impact of climate change on water resources is likely to have more severe impacts on water systems than ENSO neutral years. The fact that the water systems in Vanuatu are already fragile and under pressure, coupled with the vulnerability of the freshwater reserve” is a direct issue that enhances community risk and exposure of climate change impacts, regardless of the historical cause. Limited freshwater supplies will be greatly depleted over time due to increased evaporation rates and reduced rainfall. The IEU also notes that water can easily contaminated in inundation events or through enhanced saltwater intrusion from king tides, sea level rise and storm surges. As such, communities need access to more reliable and more secure sources of water to enable them to adapt and enhance their resilience to predicted climate change impacts. This is particularly relevant for the most vulnerable and least responsive to climate change adaptation needs, justifying the need for climate finance resources.

Another area of cost-effectiveness into which the independent TAP expressed why desalination was included in the project, given its relatively high cost compared with the other technologies, is that it has a higher average yield per unit volume than the other technologies. The average yield per unit volume (litres per annum) and the upfront capital expenditure for each of the four technology types are shown below. The average yield per unit volume of the planned desalination plants was USD 0.51, in comparison with USD 0.62 for gravity fed systems and USD 0.03 for groundwater systems (with rainwater harvesting systems falling in between at USD 0.48). This is particularly relevant to communities located on low-lying reef islands, in particular those located on low-lying reef islands, do not have reliable access to groundwater. Rainwater harvesting can be a useful alternative water source but it has limited capacity, especially within El Niño phase. In these particular contexts, solar powered desalination is deemed as the most cost-effective and sustainable option for communities that rely on scarce water sources in the face of climate change. This is not a common need, and the Project is only providing this technology to 10 communities. The remaining 25 communities are likely to face significant water security challenges and have minimal alternative options for safe and sustainable sources of drinking water. This estimate is based on CAP requests to date. Despite the higher upfront capital cost, the IEU notes that desalination can be a viable option if it is justified in these rare cases, including considering the logistics and transport of fresh water to remote communities.

Desalination sites would also have required to have strong RWCs in place, given the more onerous O&M requirements. At the suggestion of the independent TAP, the AE also agreed that


---
**Page 118**
---

recent advances in small-scale solar powered desalination technology would be investigated, and the most affordable best practice technology would be utilized in project implementation.

The project's overall efficiency and effectiveness is rated as medium.


## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The independent TAP endorses the funding proposal for the project "Balancing Adaptation and Community Resilience by Improving Water Security in Namibia".

* The independent TAP highlighted emerging technologies that do not require battery storage or grid connection, but are still cost-effective. For more information on these technologies, see https://www.nam21.com/news/43/namibia/https://www.researchgate.net/publication/308659724_low_cost_solar_power_desalination_plant_could_possibly_be_the_future_of_water_security_in_namibian_deserts/ and https://solarfmagazine.com/no-batteries-needed-low-cost-solar-desalination-system-green-nambia-desert-coast/.

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP191)

## Proposal name:
Enhancing Adaptation and Community Resilience by Improving Water Security in Vanuatu

## Accredited entity:
The Pacific Community (SPC)

## Country/ies:
Vanuatu

## Project/programme size:
Small

### Impact potential
SPC is pleased to note that the independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) considers that the impact of this project to be 'high'. Given Vanuatu's high vulnerability to climate change, the project will provide significant adaptation impacts in terms of water security and community resilience in the face of extreme climate risks following government-owned water management systems.

### Paradigm shift potential
SPC notes that the ITAP assessment of the paradigm shift potential of the project as 'medium'. By strengthening governance processes through transparent, community-focused, gender-mainstreamed approaches, Vanuatu will move away from the "build-neglect-rebuild" cycles characterising many such investments globally and towards a paradigm of water security that is sustainable, participatory, equitable and climate-resilient.

### Sustainable development potential
SPC is pleased to note that the ITAP considers that the sustainable development potential of the project is 'high'. Access to safe and secure water is fundamental to Article 25 of the Universal Declaration of Human Rights. This project will contribute towards achieving Sustainable Development Goals 6 and 3, amongst others. SPC is proud to be supporting Vanuatu in their progress towards achievement of national sustainable development outcomes in a climate-resilient, inclusive and community-centred manner.

### Needs of the recipients
SPC is pleased to note that the ITAP considers that the needs of the recipients are 'high'. Indeed, Vanuatu is one of the most vulnerable countries in the Pacific; notwithstanding underlying social, economic and environmental challenges. This project will enhance the ability of ni-Vanuatu communities to achieve safe and secure wellbeing. This project will address many of these needs, providing opportunities for significantly improved living conditions for ni-Vanuatu communities.

### Country ownership


---
**Page 120**
---

### Efficiency and effectiveness

SPC notes that the ITAP assessment of the project’s efficiency and effectiveness as “medium”. While some interventions were assessed to have comparatively high financial cost, it is noteworthy that the costs to human lives, livelihoods and wellbeing of not taking such action or making such investments are even higher. The independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) notes that the costs associated with such investments in an island country such as Vanuatu are comparatively lower than those elsewhere in the Pacific region. The ITAP further notes that these costs are justified given the expected adaptation and sustainable development impacts vis-à-vis the needs of the people.

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

SPC is pleased to note that the ITAP has endorsed the funding proposal for the project “Enhancing Adaptation and Community Resilience by Improving Water Security in Vanuatu”.

# COVID-19 in Vanuatu

During the development of this report, the global community continues to grapple with the coronavirus pandemic, which is wreaking havoc on the lives of millions of people, both with regard to health and wellbeing, and socioeconomic indicators. COVID-19 is also having distinctive gendered impacts, as evidenced by the disproportionate burden placed upon women in many countries.

Vanuatu experienced a relatively mild outbreak of COVID-19 in 2020. Due to the authorities' decisive measures, Vanuatu primarily avoided domestic transmission of COVID-19, but the border closure, according to the Internal Affairs Minister, was key in preventing the virus from entering the country.

At the time of writing Annex E (Watch Against Violence), Vanuatu's health authorities announced community transmission of COVID-19, with restrictions on mobility and economy imposed on the main island of Efate (and its surrounding islands). A curfew running from 06:00 to 18:00 has severely constrained women's economic activity and incomes, notably female food sellers or voters.

The coronavirus pandemic has exposed entrenched inequalities and stark disparities in power, clearly illustrating how a global crisis like this impacts people differently depending on where they live, their gender, their age and their socioeconomic status. The consequences of this crisis for gender equality are profound, with women disproportionately affected by the economic fallout and stakeholder consultation processes for the project preparation phase. Worldwide, there has been increased attention to the disproportionate impact that COVID-19 is having on women's health and rights. This is particularly concerning in the case of Vanuatu—which, along with the broader Pacific region—is endemic for heightened gender-based violence and violence against women.

The project has attempted to cover these nuances throughout this assessment, paying particular attention to how gender, WASH and climate change interact and identifying actionable interventions such as increasing women's representation in community-based water management groups and ensuring that women are included in decision-making processes on COVID-19.

Through the project's paradigm shift pathway, vulnerable (often remote) communities in Vanuatu (and marginalized groups such as women and girls within them) are expected to be able to benefit from overall increase in climate resilience and improved water safety and security.

# Definitions

To ensure a baseline understanding of climate issues and the development of the analysis based on impact and exposure on women, men, girls and boys, the following definitions, adapted from the USAID Climate Change Adaptation Project Preparation Facility for Asia and the Pacific (Adapt Asia-Pacific), are provided as references for this Annex.

| Term | Definition |
| --- | --- |
| Gender Equity | The process of being fair to women and men, boys and girls. To ensure fairness, measures must be taken to address gender stereotypes and other social norms and values that prevent women and men, boys and girls from operating on a level playing field (DGAC/UNFPA). |
| Gender Equality | The state or condition that affords women and men equal enjoyment of human rights, socially valued goods, opportunities, and resources. Important equality means more than parity in numbers or laws on the book; it means expanded freedoms and improved overall quality of life for all positive impacts (DGAC/UNFPA). |
| Gender-blind | are project designs and activities that ignore gender factors including roles and relations and can lead to reinforcement of gender-based discrimination and existing inequities. (USAID) |
| Gender-balance | requires that men and women be equally represented - either in equal numbers or in proportion to their presence – in particular settings. (USAID) |
| Gender Mainstreaming | Process of incorporating a gender perspective into organizational policies, strategies, and operations at all levels. The process is intended to ensure that the needs and interests of both sexes are addressed at the organizational level ideally resulting in meaningful gender integration as outlined below. (adapted from IGWG training resources) |
| Gender and Socioeconomic Analysis | Is the process of collecting information about gender, age and other social differences and inequalities, including those of environmental circumstances (i.e., climate changes on specific groups of people). This type of analysis provides the basis for identifying key gender considerations and designing a “gender-sensitive approach” that responds to the unique circumstances and needs of all project beneficiaries. (USAID) |

# Gender-based Violence

In the broadest terms, "gender-based violence" is violence that is directed at individuals based on their biological sex, gender identity, or perceived adherence to culturally defined expectations of masculinity and femininity. GBV can take many forms including physical abuse; sexual abuse; psychological abuse; threats; coercion; arbitrary deprivation of liberty; and economic deprivation, whether occurring in public or private. GBV is rooted in economic, social, and political inequalities that are often perpetuated by patriarchal norms and practices from infancy through childhood and adolescence, the reproductive years and into old age (Koroma 2009). GBV can be experienced across a range of contexts including family, intimate relationships, communities, schools, workplaces, and public spaces. Specific types of GBV include (but are not limited to) female infanticide; early and forced marriage; sexual violence; domestic violence; economic exploitation; trafficking in persons; sexual coercion, harassment and abuse; neglect; domestic violence; economic deprivation; and elder abuse.

# Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Sexual Harassment

SEAH is the term used to refer to sexual exploitation, abuse and sexual harassment. Although sexual exploitation, abuse and sexual harassment can happen anywhere in society, when used by humanitarian organisations, it refers specifically to the risk of sexual exploitation, abuse or SEAH perpetrated by those working in, with, development and humanitarian organisations and within Peacekeeping Missions. (adapted from the Safeguarding Support Hub)

# Introduction to the GCF - Annex 8

## Objective of the study

This Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan (GA-GAP) is a supporting document for the design of the proposed GCF project: Enhancing adaptation and community resilience by improving water resources management in Vanuatu. The project, which is being proposed as a full-scale project by the Accredited Entity (AE) - The Pacific Community (SPC), will be implemented with the support of the Government of Vanuatu in close coordination with Vanuatu’s Department of Water Resources (DoWR) and WASH sector partners.

SPC is currently implementing the FPIM: Climate change adaptation solutions for Local Authorities in the Federated States of Micronesia® and has a track record in implementing gender-responsive adaptation solutions, within its broader portfolio of designing and implementing climate change adaptation projects. The organization recognizes that climate change, disaster risk management, food security and human rights are interlinked.

- Climate adaptation and mitigation pathways are not gender-neutral;
- Gendered needs and vulnerabilities of marginal groups, such as Indigenous Peoples, need to be mainstreamed into adaptation design, resilience capacity-building and mitigation services;
- Gender-transformative impact can be driven through robust financial means, gender budgets and dedicated resources towards mainstreaming gender action in climate change and development.

Without gender-sensitive socioeconomic analysis, and a thorough Environmental and Social Management Plan (Annex 5 of the Funding Proposal), the benefits of increased support and access to climate change adaptation services, such as disaster risk management capacity building, may accrue to better-off households or more mainstream groups that are able to capitalize on new opportunities and respond better to changes implemented through the project.

> More information on this project is available here: [https://www.greenclimate.fund/project/1938](https://www.greenclimate.fund/project/1938)

*Current literature on climate change, and its effects and emerging risks, are predominantly produced in scientific circles. True, there is increasing evidence that adopting social science methods, and situating resilience and adaptation research within the context of development and poverty reduction, can help to better understand how communities respond towards climate-related socioeconomic co-benefits. Particularly, this could better prepare communities to avoid resource strife and conflict, while also engaging rights into-resilience, removing existing risks into social conflicts in disasters. *For a missing gender analysis, or the lack of gender-responsive action, may lead to planners or personnel developing projects that are not inclusive of women’s needs and interests, which can have negative impacts for very vulnerable communities. Further, this also glosses over the existing burdens on women among such groups. See below: Gender equality and climate change adaptation: The need to mainstream gender in climate change adaptation and the need to mainstream gender in climate change adaptation | Gender and Development. A gender lens is both necessary and relevant for the project to maximise its outcomes for the CR-WASH sub-sector, and ensuring resilience of water infrastructure against natural and climate-induced hazards, disasters and weather variations that cannot be avoided. This gender responsive approach will help mobilize critical resources and build on existing political momentum and socioeconomic frameworks to mobilise medium- and long-term adaptation strategies in the context of climate change and its impacts on water security in the communities. Particularly, this project will aim to increase institutional representation and meaningful participation of women in community-based management of water systems and CR-WASH services, as well as improve access to WASH services that help deliver the adaptation pathway chosen by the project, as well as sustain results to ensure that communities are able to transition to a water secure and climate-resilient future.

The overall objective, therefore, of the gender assessment will be to provide a tool to promote gender mainstreaming throughout the project paradigm... particularly, by building on relevant entry points that the project can leverage in order to improve resilience and the climate-resilient water, sanitation, and hygiene (CR-WASH) sub-sector. Using gender as a lens will also help address the social relationships between women and men, and other marginalized groups in Vanuatu.

Access to WASH is fundamental to development but it can be mobilized as critical climate adaptation strategy for poor and vulnerable communities, as well as marginalized groups within those communities, including women and girls. The levels of global climate finance directed to the CR-WASH sub-sector are still very low, despite the fact that access to safe water and WASH is yet to be recognized as a key climate adaptation strategy, and for gender equality.

The focus on gender in this project will help identify the barriers to accessing WASH services, needs and opportunities of women men boys and girls in the project context, and mobilise project resources to tackle gender barriers, and contribute towards improved gender equality in Vanuatu. The gender assessment will be used as a tool to inform the project’s design through the project's results framework by formulating a Gender Action Plan (Section 8, based on the project’s Logical Framework (Section E of the Funding Proposal).

This will ensure that the proposed design will deliver improved water security and increased climate resilience in Vanuatu. The gender assessment will also identify institutional and social benefits in Vanuatu, with identified indicators timelines and means of verification. The logical framework of the project has three outcomes, two of which are focused on water infrastructure management and delivery in tandem with a Outcome dedicated to institutional strengthening.

Through these Outcomes and outputs, the proposed project will mainstream gender by:

- Analyzing existing gender norms and dynamics in Vanuatu through the National Implementation Plan for Safe and Secure Community Drinking Water (NIP), Capital Assistance Program (CAP) and more broadly, by equally targeting beneficiaries through the Drinking Water Sector and Securing Water.

- Establishing gender-transformative approaches for the design, delivery and maintenance plan of climate-resilient rural water infrastructure.

- Promoting gender parity to the extent possible, through the technical and maintenance capacity-building outputs, alongside the institutional development targeted for increased water sector participation by women.

- Mainstreaming gender analyses and praxis for GCF Investments in the CR-WASH subsector, both as a best practice in Vanuatu and for similar contexts in the Pacific Island Countries and Territories (PICTs).

## 1.2 Gender barriers in Vanuatu: a summary

A kaleidoscope of overlapping cultural, socioeconomic and political roles form gender norms, relations, uneven power dynamics and vulnerabilities in Vanuatu. For these reasons, the climate crisis affects women disproportionately and differently than men. The archipelago nation is spread over 80 islands and has repeatedly been featured as one of the most at-risk and exposed country to climate change, notably in the 2019 World Risk Index.


---
**Page 131**
---

Annex B - Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan

Vanuatu is also classified as a Small Island Developing States or SIDS[1], which are a distinct group of developing island countries facing specific social, economic and environmental vulnerabilities.

As with other nations with SIDS status, repeated exposure to climate events and natural disaster phenomena has led to the erosion of traditional knowledge and practices. This erosion is further exacerbated by shared prosperity in Vanuatu and undermines progress on gender equality indicators. This is compounded by a lack of access to information and communication technology (ICT) at both national scale, low income and assets, small domestic markets and heavy dependence on a few external markets and international support, high volatility of economic growth, fragile natural environment, and limited infrastructure.

Additionally, until December 2020, Vanuatu was a Least Developed Country (LDCs). During its graduation from the LDC list, United Nations Conference on Trade and Development recognized consistent strides made by the country to improve its social and economic development indicators but felt that exposure to environmental and economic vulnerabilities remain high, particularly in the face of climate change.

In terms of gender characteristics, Vanuatu can be identified as a traditionally male-dominated and largely patriarchal society, with skewed sex ratio (105 males: 100 females) in the Islands. Overarching gender barriers include:

- Limited participation of women in policy and decision-making spheres
- Women’s limited access to land and resources, which is further upheld by low representation in Parliament, as well as within devolved institutions of power.
- The national government's Beijing +25 review report, for example, highlights that no women were elected to parliament in the last two elections. In 2019, only one woman was elected to the National Council and there is a sole female councilor elected in the sixth. Socio-anthropological research in Kuruambarane and Purau villages in Tonga, for example, highlighted how women must navigate strict rules governing their movement and access to specific resources, including land, which excludes them from holding titles or wielding decision-making power even at localised levels of governance.[2]

[1] SIDS were recognized as having special needs both for their environment and development at the Earth Summit held in Rio de Janeiro in 1992. Updated list of SIDS can be found on: https://www.un.org/sustainabledevelopment/sids/

[4] See UNESCO’s LDC profile for Vanuatu: http://www.un.org/development/desa/lpdsd/wp-consultation-profile-of-LDC-Vanuatu.pdf

Inequality to economic lives as well as gendered capacity and input gaps among men and women

Vanuatu is an agricultural society in which 75% of the total population live in rural areas and depend heavily on subsistence agriculture and fishing for daily sustenance and livelihoods according to the Vanuatu National Statistics Office (2016). The 2016 mini-census found that 88% of household engage in some form of vegetable crop production, 57% in cash crop production, 69% in livestock production, and 43% in aquaculture. According to the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) found that women and men participate in almost equal numbers in the agricultural sectors, but inequality manifests in the crops that are grown (often: men may grow cash crops while women grow vegetables), access to inputs (men have better access to inputs, owing to cash income from more lucrative crops). Additionally, traditional and faith-based practices often operate and undermine gender equality in the essential inputs for productive and remunerative livelihoods (livestocks, such as not excluding women from matrimonial property, inheritance, and land rights).

Disproportionate share of unpaid work continues to be undertaken by women.

As in many other societies, the World Bank’s Gender and Investment Climate Reform Assessment for Vanuatu identified that women are often trapped into a double burden: combining responsibilities for home and family with their economic activities. Although parity in enrolment rates is achieved at primary school level, girls are less likely to complete secondary education than boys, and even less likely than men to have a completed secondary school education and go on to tertiary education. This hints towards a gender gap in educational achievements and skills, which translates into women being paid lower wages, low-paying jobs that are often found primarily in the informal sector.

In rural contexts, women face barriers in finding income-generating remunerative work, and may be constrained by their responsibilities as well as by various unpaid domestic tasks, exhibiting high levels of time poverty. Through stakeholder consultations for this project, it was revealed that women are primarily agents of domestic water provision, along with teenagers and young adults, which is corroborated by existing research by UNICEF and acedemica.

High incidence of gender-based violence in Vanuatu

Vanuatu has one of the highest prevalence rates of violence against women and girls globally. Research conducted by the World Bank (2014) established that 48% of women with an intimate partner had experienced physical violence, ~68% experienced emotional violence, and ~49% coercive behavioural control by men. According to the World Bank’s 2012


---
**Page 133**
---

# Annex B - Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan

World Development Report on Gender Equality and Development, between 60-70% of women in Vanuatu (alongside Kiribati and Solomon Islands) experience some form of domestic violence.

Male family members and boyfriends perpetrate most of the violence and it occurs in all provinces and islands, across age groups, education levels, socio-economic groups and religious beliefs. The majority of men and boys believe that beating their wives is acceptable and men reinforce the acceptability of violence towards women and girls and -60% of women agree with at least one "reason" for men to beat their wives. In Vanuatu, gender-based violence is often seen as a normal part of life and domestic violence against women is accepted and condoned as 'normal' behaviour in many communities. This impacts both women and children.

Stakeholder consultations conducted during the project highlighted the need for introducing security and safety outcomes for system improvements and new WASH infrastructure planned through the project - particularly demarcation of women and men's toilets, locks and doors, as well as lighting to ensure safe passage and accessibility. Therefore, working to mainstream gender equality into all aspects of the project (including water supply, sanitation and hygiene management) through planned project activities will overall increase community well-being, ensure social co-benefits and the success of the project adaptation pathway.

# Methodology note for Annex 8

## Primary data and information collected through key informant interviews and focused working groups

Due to pandemic-related obstacles, this gender assessment has been prepared using primary information and data collected by national stakeholder engagement experts between June - September 2021. The methodology was informed by the Government of Vanuatu’s guidelines for consultations and engagement processes.

Following the inception Workshop, one-to-one face to face consultations were held with key stakeholders actively working or involved in the national water program. These consultations focused on issues for water and utilities as well as the overall improvement of WASH service delivery in Vanuatu.

Using semi-structured interviewing methodology, ranging from one to two persons at a time and in accordance to COVID-19 guidelines laid down by the Government of Vanuatu, interviews were conducted with key informants including government officials, non-governmental organizations (NGOs), civil society organizations (CSOs); and UN entities active in Vanuatu, particularly UNICEF (an important WASH sector partner for this project). The stakeholders ranged from employees of the Davis Water Project to those involved in the national water program. These consultations focused on water security in Vanuatu. Participants and stakeholders were identified using a combination of purposive and snowball sampling during the field missions led by the DoWWR, and consulted according to their role and responsibilities within the WASH sector. The focus was on infrastructure, gender as well as socioeconomic issues to prioritise and vet information and data received.

The Department of Women’s Affairs (DoWA) was among the key stakeholders consulted as a part of this process. A key finding from this consultation was that gender has been increasingly incorporated in WASH processes over the years. However, mainstreaming remains a need across all WASH stakeholders that requires ongoing improvement. WASH design interventions and present practices are not always inclusive of women’s needs and interests such as those for children, the elderly and LGTBQ+. Given the reach of the proposed project down to community-level women, it was determined that the views of the provincial government and DoWA would inform the project prioritization stage accordingly. With consent from DoWWR, the provinces of Penama, Sanma and Torba® were selected. The consultations indicated ongoing relevant water

## Secondary data and information collected through literature review

To collect secondary information and literature, the gender expert conducted an in-depth review in tandem with the primary and formative research through national experts and key informants. The review was focused on gender mainstreaming, wider resilience and climate as a broader topic, drawing from key players in the water industry such as the Global Water Partnership (GWP), the Global Facility for Disaster Reduction and Recovery (GFDRR), United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), the International Finance Corporation (IFC) of the World Bank, as well as donor agencies active in the Pacific region, such as Australian Aid (AusAID), Japan International Cooperation Agency (JICA) and United States Agency for International Development (USAID).

Existing gender assessment and gender action plan prior to GCF investment in Vanuatu (PFO15), were also reviewed to understand the scope of gender climate resilience work through prior GCF investments. Lessons learned from these reviews were reviewed from recently closed and ongoing projects such as UNDP (V-ICAP project) and some of the Pacific Environment Programme (PEP) (VAN-KRIP project). These studies and reports were supplemented by other relevant literature, including those from the Australian National Institute on Environment and Development (IENID) and the World Resources Institute (WRI). Entries in peer-reviewed anthropology, geography, and climate-focused journals such as Anthropology Today, Pacific Geographies, and others (among others) were also reviewed to ensure that the assessment is able to capture latest studies and research in Vanuatu on the broader domains of climate resilience, WASH and gender.

# Concurrent triangulation of primary inputs and secondary data and information collection

The study employs a concurrent triangulation methodology, and deliberately splinters sequential research processes. In sequential designs, either the qualitative or quantitative data are collected first, followed by collecting the other type during a succeeding, second stage.21 In contrast, concurrent designs are characterized by the collection of both types of data during the same stage (which was the case for the development of this assessment).

Within these two categories, there can be three specific designs based on: (a) the level of emphasis given to the qualitative and quantitative data (equal or unequal), (b) the process used to analyze and integrate the data, and (c) whether the theoretical basis underlying the study methodology is tied to social change or advocacy.21

This approach has been deemed suited to this gender assessment, given that it will operationalize GCF and co-financing initiatives towards improved gender outcomes (such as: increased participation of women in rural water governance; enhanced capacity for climate-resilient infrastructure design and reinforced policy frameworks), through the paradigm of Increased resilience and water security in Vanuatu.

Additionally, the stakeholder consultations undertaken in the islands, was formative in nature and facilitated the development of this methodology to establish an inventory of information and a gender baseline that can be operationalized to cover the complex aspects of CR-WASH in Vanuatu. Therefore, the concurrent approach is suited because of: the flexibility to use secondary data to explore the phenomenon (mainly the stakeholder consultations), and the accuracy it can provide to samples of intermediate or small size (the stakeholders consulted during the fieldwork were not necessarily representative).

This also ensures that the inventory and action plan are able to propose measures that are relevant for the delivery of outputs identified in the results framework, particularly the identified gender-responsive co-benefit in Section 6 of the Funding Proposal: improved gender parity in water sector decision-making (from 20% to 50%) and increased women’s access to WASH. Additionally, the project will be targeting a 50-50 ratio of beneficiaries at the community level in the 600 new DWSSPs, as well as among the chosen DWSSPs (270) that receive infrastructure support. The project will also upgrade the requirements for DWSPs, modelling the inclusion of gender factors in the broader prioritization framework, as well as upgrading the methodology of the DWSSP.

# Policy environment for gender-transformative CR-WASH in Vanuatu

This section takes stock of the policy environment and legal frameworks available for gender-responsive climate change adaptation in the Vanuatu context, including international conventions such as the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women or CEDAW, national laws and policies, strategy documents on gender and climate change. It is important to note that the National Sustainable Development Plan (NSDP) 2016-2030 includes a Gender Equality Policy fostering gender-responsive and community-driven solutions to climate and disaster resilience, which this project will contribute to by delivering gender-responsive climate change adaptation interventions in rural and remote communities.

Identification of legal tools and enabling mechanisms, particularly at the baseline, is crucial in ensuring that gender inequality can be addressed through government-owned and formal procedures. These tangible processes can also help drive ownership of the project, and its social impacts. The NSDP 2016-2030 includes a Gender Equality Policy fostering gender-responsive climate change adaptation interventions in rural and remote communities.

The identification of legal tools and enabling mechanisms is crucial in ensuring that gender inequality can be addressed through government-owned and formal procedures. These tangible processes can also help drive ownership of the project, and its social impacts. The NSDP 2016-2030 includes a Gender Equality Policy fostering gender-responsive climate change adaptation interventions in rural and remote communities.

Awareness raising for women’s rights, particularly through civil society organizations, is also key. |

The NGEP 2020-2030, prepared by the Department of Women's Affairs of the Ministry of Justice and Community Services directs the government’s efforts towards achieving gender equality in Vanuatu through all governmental departments. The NGEP prioritizes five strategic areas: a) eliminating discrimination and violence against women; b) promoting economic empowerment for women and girls’ development; 3) advancing women's leadership and political participation; d) strengthening the foundation for gender mainstreaming in disaster risk reduction, climate change, and community-driven solutions to climate and disaster resilience.

## Gender Action Plans at provincial level

2020 - 2024

The purpose of this Gender Equality Action Plan is to provide a clear plan of action for government, civil society, private sector and development partners to work together in order to achieve gender equality and the well-being of women and girls in all 6 Provinces (in line with the National Gender Equality Policy (NGEP) 2020-2030.

### WASH policies

The Water Resources Management (Amendment) Act No. 18 of 2014 and the Drinking Water Safety and Security Planning (DWSSP), the Vanuatu National Drinking Water Quality Standards 2016, and the Vanuatu National Water Policy 2017-2030 all require community water systems to be registered with the Department of Environment and Forests (DEF). DWSSP is an internationally recognized approach for achieving safe drinking water in communities. The Water Resources Management Act No. 18 of 2014 and the draft Vanuatu National Water Strategy 2018-2030 (WNS) include strategic directions and targets for introducing DWSSP i.e., the process of community water systems registration, development of community water supply schemes (i.e., departments, private communities, schools, health facilities and households). The National Environmental Management Policy and Strategy 2016-2024 (NEMPS) also sets DWSSP targets.

Drinking Water Safety and Security Planning (DWSSP) is a process of community water systems registration. It involves the development of safe and secure drinking water, and making plans to manage their supply. The resulting community DWSSP guides day-to-day water supply management in communities.

Once the necessary community development training has been provided, the registration can be completed (Section 20F of the Water Resources Management Act No. 18 of 2014). Registration requires at least 40% of the members of a RWC must be women - which has been added as a co-benefit to the project's Logical Framework (Section E: Funding Proposals).

# Socioeconomic and gender baseline in Vanuatu

## Methodology for the baseline

The contents of this assessment are derived from a combination of primary research (through national- and local-level stakeholder consultations) and secondary research (literature review, policy analysis and collation of secondary data). Site-specific, location-based data has not been collected as part of this assessment. The baseline is based on the National Investment Project Implementation Plan (NIP) process and mobilize those funds through the Capital Assistance Programme (CAP) and the Vanuatu Social Protection Fund (VSPF).

Programme indicators are mapped against the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), including indices. These data points have been chosen to nuance the primary and secondary research on climate resilience and the WASH baseline in the country. Drawn from primarily VNSD, and different sources of information, these indicators are considered a “gender analysis toolkit” as crucial socioeconomic indicators that can impact upon the performance of the project and therefore, are being mapped and mainstreamed in the project design stage.

## National aggregate statistics with SDG indicators

The national-level data points are divided into four categories (poverty and hardship, education, labour and health), and presented alongside their corresponding Sustainable Development Goal (SDGs). These indicators cover: SDG 1; SDG 2; SDG 4 and SDG 5.

# Annex B - Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan

Vanuatu’s relatively high per capita incomes combine with reasonably widespread land access for subsistence agriculture and informal, community-based social safety nets to keep the incidence of extreme poverty low. However, these high per capita incomes overshadow the fact that Vanuatu (along with Fiji) has one of the highest rates of multidimensional poverty in the region. The rate of extreme poverty (the regional average for the Pacific is around 31%). Vanuatu (along with the FSM, Kiribati and Marshall Islands) has a higher proportion of people in poverty in the southern Pacific. This is generally viewed as hardship-critically in this context.

- National and International Poverty Line:20 National poverty lines are defined according to each country’s specific economic and social circumstances, and the International Poverty Line is pegged at US $1.00 or 210 VATU Since Vanuatu is a lower middle-income country, it is also below this line. The national poverty line for Vanuatu is US$3.50 (VATU) or US ($3.30 or 345 VATU). 39% of the population is below this line, according to the latest available statistics. With recent TCS Plan and Harold (strongest and second strongest to effect ever in Vanuatu), poverty rates have been reduced from around 62% earlier.

- Multidimensional poverty: Currently, due to a paucity of data, the MPI is not calculated for Vanuatu Like peer Melanesian economies, with large proportions of the population living semi-subsistence lifestyles in rural areas, consumption poverty is difficult to measure. The MPI is an important tool that measures this which are exacerbated by multivariable risks, including compounding climate hazards.24

- Gender-disaggregation of households: 25 In Vanuatu, due to the presence of multigenerational households, it is difficult to assess how household heads are designated and such data are not available from the National Population and Housing Census. The only data available from a monograph published by the WSD on the 2009 National Population and Housing Census reveals that HHIs are the norm (74% compared to 24% of FHs). The overall gender-disaggregated household composition is not available in the Vanuatu section of the WNSD monograph. However, analysing the data by the sex of the headhousehold lone HHIs have slightly better access to improved sanitation facilities compared to FHs. FHs also tend to have shorter dwellings, possibly reflecting a lower standard of housing for women in particular cases and areas.26

Despite producing insignificant greenhouse gas emissions that cause climate change, SIDS populations (including those in Vanuatu) are disproportionately affected by climate change impacts, including a high burden of climate-sensitive diseases. Furthermore, the government highlights the threats climate change poses to human health in its Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs). The NDCs also highlight the need for improved access to basic health services. The importance of SIDS is underwritten by the scope of this project: the vulnerability of SIDS to climate change and water scarcity of communities have direct and indirect impacts on health outcomes of the population, particularly in light of the ongoing COVID-19 pandemic.

* Maternal mortality ratio (MMR), infant mortality rate (IMR) and under-five mortality rate: 18 MMR, IMR and under-five mortality statistics are collected through the census to demonstrate the burden of disease on health systems.
* Child mortality, for example, has fallen in Vanuatu, but remains high compared to other Pacific countries. Poor water security and WASH infrastructure is one of the leading contributors to enteric and other infectious disease burdens, which remain high in Vanuatu.

Additionally, Vanuatu – like peer economies and SIDS – is vulnerable to tropical disease outbreaks. Climate change could affect the seasonality of such outbreaks, as well as the transmission dynamics of diseases that are endemic or introduced into a country.

The education system of Vanuatu is atypical in that it represents an amalgamation of two disparate systems, the British and the French, that co-existed within the country prior to independence. Additionally, there are significant variations between rural and urban areas.

Government expenditure on education (as a percentage of total government expenditure) is 12.6% and the sector is the largest government service deliverer and employer.
Overall, Vanuatu has made progress in improving access to education for both boys and girls, with performance of boys and girls, from available data. ### Vanuatu's Economy

Vanuatu’s economy is still primarily based on subsistence or small-scale agriculture, which provides a living for the majority of its population. The country has experienced significant fluctuations in commodity prices for copra and coffee, and donor funding have driven the economy. Major impediments to economic growth include limited economic base, constraints from poor transport infrastructure and a small domestic market.

- Although the gender differences are minor, it is telling that, according to World Bank statistics, about 70.63% of the active female labour force and 65.43% of the active male labour force are engaged in vulnerable employment. Roughly 20% of the workforce in Vanuatu is employed by the public sector, which provides a relatively stable income for some hundreds of jobs each year. Consequently, the social protection system in Vanuatu is limited to formal sector employees – a minority income of workers in the country.

- Given trends of low growth and limited employment opportunities, migrants, including temporary workers from other Pacific Island countries, have been an important source of remittances for many families. Remittances and migration have been a critical driver of increased living standards in Vanuatu (and other Pacific Island Countries). However, although out-migration has significantly increased household incomes, the impact on employment opportunities is mixed.

- In response to the COVID-19 pandemic, seasonal worker schemes have been affected in Australia and New Zealand, and the broader Pacific region, causing broad-based and significant reductions in employment and earnings. About 8.1% of the Vanuatu’s workforce rely on such programmes
