# Composite indices (HDI, GDI, GII, GGII)

Designing a climate-resilience-based WASH service delivery system requires situating the project within the framework of existing national and international development indicators. This study collates through various data sources. A collection of scores and rankings from comparable composite indices is used to provide an overview of Vanuatu’s performance on these indicators. The World Bank has included this assessment to reflect Vanuatu's overall performance on different indicators. These indices have differing methodologies, and are being employed as indicative (and not definitive) measures of current levels of development, gender equality, poverty, and labour force participation.

This baseline, at the outset, uses scores of three different UNDP composite indices: Human Development Index (HDI), Gender Inequality Index (GII) and Gender Development Index (GGI) as points of reference. The Human Development Index is a composite index that has been used to display the capacity of data characteristic of Pacific Island contexts. Secondly, the baseline collates scores from the World Economic Forum’s Global Gender Gap Index (GGGI), where Vanuatu was recently included.

| INDEX SCALE / ORGANIZATION | RANK / SCORE |
| --- | --- |
| Human Development Index, out of 189 countries (UNDP) | 141 |
| Gender Inequality Index, out of 162 countries (UNDP) | n/a |
| Gender Development Index clustered with group (UNDP) | n/a |
| Global Gender Gap Index out of 153 countries (WEF) | 126 |

Vanuatu’s HDI value is 0.609, which puts the country in the medium human development category globally. The GII score of Vanuatu is 0.487 and the GGI score is 0.503. These scores can partly be linked to the SID-specific challenges that the World Bank identifies for the Vanuatu economy.

As Boyer’s research shows, composite indices present both challenges and advantages. For example, numerous education studies have shown that composite indices are not only complex but also difficult to interpret quantitatively, and present empirical and aggregate measures of complex development phenomena, making values approximate rather than precise. The use of composite indices is often justified by the need for income-based development indicators, understanding relative degrees of development, simplifying complex interrelationships between variables, and providing a more comprehensive picture of development.

# Gender at the Green Climate Fund & the Pacific Community

The analytical prerogatives of this Gender Assessment and Action Plan are informed by both GCF’s and SPC's respective gender and protection as well as social and environmental responsibility policies.

The GCF adopted a revised version of its 2014 Gender Policy and Action Plan on June 2018 in Korea. The revised Policy addresses pertinent issues on gender and climate change: the expansion of gender mainstreaming beyond the preserve of women’s issues; and the identification of key areas for action, including the integration of gender into the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC's Gender Action Plan (GAP)), Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW). Overall, the Policy and Action Plan reinforce the responsiveness of GCF to the multiple, heterogeneous and intersecting dimensions of gender in climate change. They also highlight the links between gender issues and climate change - a perspective that has been mainstreamed in the development of this full funding proposal for CR-WASH in Vanuatu.

The SPC adopted a set of general policies towards social and environmental responsibility in August 2020. The key objectives of this policy are to promote and drive continuous improvement of SPC’s performance on gender equality, social inclusion and environmental sustainability across its programmes, projects and activities, as well as to align with international recognized best practices.

The policy provisions for considerations of gender equality and social inclusion in SPC's mandate by coordination between key divisions within SPC that are responsible for mainstreaming human rights, gender equality and social inclusion. In line with the objectives set out under this policy, the SPC will minimise social, cultural and environmental impact; contribute towards the economic empowerment of women, youth, persons with disabilities, and marginalised groups in all their diversity; and promote the participation of women, youth, persons with disabilities, and marginalised groups in all their diversity. The SPC will also contribute to the elimination of all forms of gender-based violence. These goals have informed the development of this full funding proposal for CR-WASH in Vanuatu.

The GCF and SPC are currently collaborating on sharing up-to-date transformative adaptation solutions from Pacific Island countries and territories, as well as lessons learned in the recently approved FP169*. SPC developed a robust gender assessment* and gender action plan** to inform the disbursement of sub-grants to local authorities in the Federated States of Micronesia.

# Gender mainstreaming for CR-WASH in Vanuatu

## Problem statement: WASH baseline in Vanuatu

Combined coverage of basic water and sanitation is lower in the Pacific than any other region of the world, with progress in the region described by the Joint Monitoring Programme (of UNICEF and WHO) as having stagnated between 2000 and 2015. In Vanuatu, it is estimated that 44% of people have access to improved drinking water sources and only 38% have access to an improved basic handwashing facility at home. Climate change impacts such as sea-level rise, changing rainfall patterns and increasingly frequent extreme weather events makes Vanuatu particularly vulnerable to inadequate potable water, and insufficient water and infrastructure for hygiene and sanitation.

Within Vanuatu, the report finds, the province of Sanma is reported to have the greatest burden of WASH-related diseases per 1,000 persons in the country. Surveys completed by World Vision Vanuatu (2015) indicate that women and girls are significantly more likely than men and boys to be 30% lower for access to clean water, and 21% lower for improved sanitation facilities. Torba is the most geographically remote province in the country, with the highest number of islands - and despite being one of the poorest provinces in Vanuatu, it has the lowest coverage rates. Torba has a significantly lower monthly income. In both of these provinces and indeed throughout the nation, people with disabilities and women face many inequities in accessing safely managed WASH facilities. This was confirmed by findings from focus group discussions and stakeholder consultations conducted by national consultants during the project preparation stage.

In Vanuatu, the report further finds, there still is a stigma or "taboo" around "skimun" or menstruation, which is believed to often lead to the isolation of menstruating women due to social taboos. The baseline survey undertaken by World Vision Vanuatu and Care International in 2018 suggests that women and girls in Vanuatu access information on menstruation from parents, teachers, grandmothers, aunts, school and clinics. However, the report noted that mothers and family members did not

# 6.2 Rationale: gender and climate-resilient WASH

The UN Sustainable Development Goals have three dedicated goals for Gender Equality (SDG 5), Clean Water and Sanitation (SDG 6), and Climate Action (SDG 13). Integrated water management and improved WASH praxis connects these goals—although issues relating to gender-responsive climate adaptation are not widely recognized. The lack of attention to this nexus is reflected in funding cycles of governments, donors and other stakeholders. Despite the centrality of WASH to climate resilience and adaptation, the links are not widely recognized—or extensively explored—and there remain significant knowledge gaps on how to mainstream climate change into water and sanitation sectors.

The water and sanitation sectors are systems of interconnected functions and continue to be seen as central to development aid (as opposed to being mainstreamed through climate aid). The proposed GCF project deviates from this gender-blind approach and will lay the groundwork for gender-responsive and climate-resilient WASH service delivery (with greater water security for both women and men, and improved access to sanitation services). Gender equality is fundamental to development but it can be mobilized as a critical climate adaptation strategy for poor households. The project will focus on improving access to safe drinking water and sanitation including women and girls in Vanuatu. Therefore, this project will deliver on gender mainstreaming targets through the design and delivery of climate-resilient WASH infrastructure (Outcome 2) alongside improved technical capacity and water governance (Outcomes 1 and 3).

In the preliminary research, the World Vision Vanuatu also suggested that women from urban areas used a variety of options to manage menstrual bleeding including sanitary pads, double cloth, tampons, washable cotton pads with or without disposable inserts, and reusable sanitary napkins. However, in rural areas, women use cloth and in some cases used natural materials like leaves because they are more affordable. The use of cloth is also common among women who cannot afford to buy sanitary pads due to lack of income, accessibility and availability. In some cases, there were reports that women would stay in bed for several days during menstruation as a result of the discomfort caused by using cloth. Some women were not allowed to handle food and use shared plates and cups during menstruation as it is considered "unlucky" and therefore they had to eat separately from their families. This practice was also reported among people with physical and disability status crimes.

# Barrier analysis: gender, water insecurity and climate-induced risks

Different gender groups, particularly women and girls, have gendered water needs which remain underserved by existing WASH infrastructure and are impacted by humanitarian emergencies:

This is a multi-structure barrier, which combines with limited technical capacity. The absence of safe and secure water supplies, and decent sanitation facilities has disproportionate negative effects on the lives of marginalized and vulnerable groups. For women and girls, apart from daily water collection (which can be time-consuming and hazardous), other gender-specific needs (such as child caring), specific care roles (such as care work for the elderly and children) as well as age and different abilities (disability) increase daily water and sanitation requirements.

Under-investment in WASH infrastructure is a major barrier to meeting these needs. Lack of safe drinking water supplies and infrastructure, discriminatory social norms as well as cultural taboos. Exact estimates are difficult to make due to the lack of data on gendered water use and the paucity of data, however, that gendered needs are not addressed, remains the status quo.

Investments in climate-resilient WASH infrastructure is currently limited.

Lack of hygienic conditions, a primary determinant of which is access to safe and potable water, also means that women and girls are more vulnerable to gender-based violence. This is because by increasing their exposure to water- and vector-borne diseases. Diarrheal disease - which is the second leading cause of death in children under five years old, is directly linked to unsafe water and sanitation. According to a study conducted in 2015, 83% of women and girls reported that they experienced sexual violence during their menstrual cycle.

Being underserved by WASH infrastructure, whether it is due to lack of access or /or because of poor design, can also increase vulnerability to gender-based violence. WaterAid has observed that women and girls are more vulnerable to gender-based violence when they use water services primarily during evening hours or at night. This increases their exposure risk to gender-based violence, particularly sexual assault and attacks, due to limited lighting and remoteness of these facilities.

In addition, the lack of access to safe drinking water and sanitation facilities can also lead to local norms around masculininity inhibiting their ability to avoid or report on experiences of violence.

**Page 150**

# Annex B - Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan

During humanitarian emergencies—which remains a cyclical and pervasive issue in Vanuatu due to repeated exposure to natural- and climate-induced hazards—accessibility is further reduced and exposure to WASH risks are exacerbated. As observed in the wake of Tropical Cyclone Pam which struck Vanuatu on 13 March 2015, the impact was felt across all sectors of society—including damage to water and sanitation assets. This had immediate effects by producing a significant increase in vulnerability for women and girls, who were disproportionately affected compared with men and boys, and across different population groups, and further to vulnerable groups within them. In Vanuatu, Women and girls are also exposed to post-disaster spikes in gender-based violence, while lacking access to essential services such as water, sanitation, hygiene (WASH), food security (often limited WASH services), and inadequate coordination on gender-specific issues by responders.

Women continue to be underrepresented in water security and climate adaptation-related decision-making bodies, despite registering better performance when represented. This is a political barrier, which combines with limited policy capacity. Women in Vanuatu are underrepresented in decision-making and policy-formulation bodies, as evidenced through figures of low (often zero) representation in Parliament, as well as at the provincial, municipal and area council levels. They also have limited access to land and resources, including water resources and managers. As stated previously, socio-anthropological research in Kuramuruhe and Purau villages in Tonga, highlighted that women are excluded from key decision-making structures (such as meetings and speeches in the nakamal (chief’s meeting place)) effectively excluding them from holding titles or wielding decision-making power even at localised levels of governance.

Consequently, women continue to be underrepresented in water user committees (WUCs), or community-based management bodies. This is despite women's leading role in household management and water collection, which are critical for water security and sanitation, which is importantly determined by access, affordability and reliability of water resources.

A national census of water points in Vanuatu was conducted between 2014 - 2016, which revealed around 8,000 water points in six provinces. Most of these were not governed by communities—and among the c. 175 community-owned water systems, the majority (99% – 81%) did not have a

committee in place, while the remaining (135 - 365) did. For these community-owned water systems with a committee, data was collected to understand community composition and the position occupied by women.

Of the WUCs that had at least one key post held by a woman, there were an average of six members per committee and a total of 2,327 members across the six provinces. Of these WUCs members, 16% (340) were women. More than half of the WUCs (51%) (186) reported having at least one key post held by a woman. Of those approximately half the posts were as secretary (53%), 40% as treasurer, and only 7% as chair.

Despite overall low representation of women in WUCs, trends of improved water management were noted when women were more represented: WUCs with women in key posts met more regularly than those without. The proportion of WUCs that had a regular meeting schedule took place more often when there is a female treasurer (73%), compared with a female chair (54%). Similarly, the proportion of WUCs where women held key positions and the system was in good condition: 35% in fair condition; and only 22% in poor condition or not working at all. Similarly, of the 179 with only men in key posts, 35% were in good condition, 29%. In fact, WUCs where women held key positions had a significantly higher proportion of WUCs with no women in key posts were also significantly more likely to function poorly or not at all compared to those that included women: 36% compared to 22%.

Insufficient, non-resilient WASH INFRASTRUCTURE continue to be compromised by climate risks, increasing time poverty among certain gender groups:

This is an infrastructure barrier, which is being compounded by climate impacts. WASH baseline in Vanuatu indicates that the majority of water points remain riddled with the lack of water quality (safety) and security (quantity), including: limited year-round service for most water points, inadequate output to cover daily water requirements. During the dry season and in drought years, water points are often not functional at all times, leading communities to declare "water emergencies". Communities are also experiencing growing tensions over water access, particularly regarding the need to address climate threats as well as institutional (and infrastructure) barriers.

In Vanuatu, women and girls (and often boys as well) usually bear the responsibility for collecting water, which is time-consuming and arduous. Lack of access to water supply systems has a significant impact on time spent transporting water, which can take up to five hours per day, according to the 2018 UNICEF/WHO Joint Monitoring Programme (JMP). The Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), which describes the key role played by its Vanuatu women in the rural subsistence economy.

*Women play a critical role… devoting most of their working lives balancing their time between meeting household and community needs. Their work includes an array of work from … caring for children, old people, people with disabilities and the infirm to domestic tasks such as fetching of water and firewood, cleaning the house, washing clothes, cooking food, preparing meals, looking after livestock and poultry, gardening including planting and production of handicrafts such as mats, baskets for the home as well as weaving of mats, baskets and grass skirts for sale as well as for cultural purposes.*


---
**Page 152**
---

This inequitable gender-based allocation of unpaid domestic and care work, representing a "double whammy" for women who enter the workforce (whether formally or informally), often leaves women with little if no discretionary time. This is known as time poverty21—a predicament in which individuals are unable to meet their basic needs because they lack sufficient income or wealth, or would be at risk of falling into poverty if the individual reduced their working hours below a certain time-poorly line.

Time poverty has important repercussions for women's economic opportunities and health, and can act as an obstacle to accessing and benefiting from project interventions, trainings and adaptation strategies for increased climate resilience and improved water security.


Water insecurity indirectly and directly comes to exacerbate gender inequality in other sectors (such as limited land rights) and limit adoption of adaptation and resilience strategies: This is an institutional barrier. Climate change represents the most complex challenge of our time—it is a threat multiplier22 that has and direct and indirect effects on existing sociocultural, economic, political, and environmental systems. These effects are felt differently by different groups, human rights, and how power, roles and responsibilities are organized among men, women, boys and girls within the household and in the economy.

Due to climate change, water stress is expected to increase globally face decline in land productivity, greater land degradation, limited access to water, increase in pests, deforestation, destruction of crops and so on. A major issues among this, related to the project paradigm is water availability. The projected increase in global temperatures will lead to a decrease in precipitation. Many countries will face increasingly severe water shortages due to reduced precipitation. This anticipated intensification of water shortage will likely lead to land use change, possibly requiring women to move from their traditional lands and to adopt more drought-resistant varieties and new propagation techniques23.


As a result of gendered norms and patriarchal structures, women may be disadvantaged and lack both the technical capacity as well as opportunity to adopt adaptation strategies. Women are often seen as passive recipients of development projects and thus face additional burdens of trouble accessing water, or using implementing energy-saving solutions, or accessing extension and/or financial services. In Vanuatu, land laws do not explicitly discriminate against women in terms of access to land. However, the social norms and cultural practices means that women have very limited rights to control and manage land. Rigid social norms in rural areas, however, reinforce the decision-making power of men in Vanuatu. Women are increasingly threatened because the rights they do have—to use the land—cannot be registered. Further, women's participation in decision making on land use is limited to informal discussions

The National Council of Chiefs, or Mbulamutsumi, must be consulted by the Parliament on any matters related to land. Since women cannot be high chiefs, they are not part of the Mbulamutsumi and do not have a say on decisions related to tenure of customary land.

These factors combine to directly and indirectly determine women’s vulnerability to climate change. Women also have limited decision-making readiness for and accessibility to climate resilience. Overall, land and water rights - in the context of mounting climatic and environmental change- function in tandem, and determine how vulnerable women are, as well as how different social sections within them, are able to access opportunities as well as adopt adaptation strategies.

## 6.5 Overall impact of the project: decreased time poverty for women and youth

Clean water is an essential resource for which there is no substitute. Each family needs a certain amount of water each day to survive, and the lack of access to improved water supply places a disproportionate burden on women and girls who tend to be the primary collectors of water for the family. The majority of these women are young (15-49 years) and are responsible for the care of improved water supply where there is no adequate safe drinking water close by, people—mostly women and children—have to travel, sometimes long distances, to fetch it.

This scenario has discussed that women, girls, and youth—as well as youth—are often responsible for domestic water provision. This has been corroborated by qualitative studies, as well as UNICEF experience. Further, during the stakeholder consultations held during the project preparation stage—breakout groups confirmed this phenomenon.


---
**Page 156**

# Annex B - Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan

Recent research by ADB1 has shown that: access to clean water supply closer to home could, therefore, have a significant impact on women's time poverty, especially for poor women and girls who otherwise tend to have the least access to clean water. Overwhelmingly, the evidence confirms that improved access to clean water can reduce time poverty and increase income savings can have several spillover effects: significant and important impacts on 'girls' attendance at school, for example, although overall data on participation on market work is limited.

The quote below comes from a qualitative study looking at the relationship between access to water, time poverty, and gender equality in rural communities in Vanuatu.2 The following is an excerpt of a woman's response: "Before there was so much difficulty to fetch water - I used to sit my baby in the sleeping house so I could take the clothes down to wash and carry back the water. It used to be hard for me to do this, but now it has become easier. Now that we have clean water is less walking and work to get the water. I have more time at home to care properly for my children. I'm now teaching good hygiene practices and I am a good mother. Before I used to go to the hospital with my children all the time, but now they are healthy..."

The Vanuatu study found that, in general, reduced labor in collecting water as a result of improved access to clean water has had a significant gender transformative impact by shifting project paradigm - to deliver climate-resilient WASH infrastructure to enhance community resilience and reduce women's time poverty with a gender-transformative impact. It has the potential to reduce the time poverty that women and girls (primarily) face, by increasing access to water sources and improving overall water security.

7. Grievance Redress Mechanism, in the case of SEAH and GbV

In 1993, the Declaration on the Elimination of Violence against Women (A/RES/48-10) defines violence against women as "any act of gender-based violence that results or is likely to result in, physical, sexual or psychological harm or suffering to women, including threats of such acts, coercion or arbitrary deprivation of liberty, whether occurring in public or in private life."

More recently, as both theoretical and practical work on GbV evolved and became enshrined in numerous conventions and declarations, article 3 of the Council of Europe's Convention on Preventing and Combating Violence against Women and Domestic Violence (2011) explicitly offered the following definition: "gender-based violence against women shall mean violence that is directed against a woman because she is a woman or that affects women disproportionately."

A critical component in this context is the concept of the Specific Emergency Assistance Hub (SEAH)—as part of the overall incidence of gender-based violence (GbV) -that is prevalent in Vanuatu.

Indeed, GbV statistics in Vanuatu are telling. Vanuatu has one of the highest prevalence rates of violence against women in the world. According to the National Women's Health and Family Centre (2011) established that ~60% of women with an intimate partner had experienced physical or sexual violence at some point in their lives, and 45% reported that they felt under control by men. According to the World Bank’s 2012 World Development Report on Gender Equality and Development, between 60 -70% of women in Vanuatu (alongside Kiribati and Solomon Islands) experience some form of domestic violence.*See Figure 2 below.

| Prevalence Data on Different Forms of Violence against Women: |
| --- |
| Physical and/or Sexual Intimate Partner Violence in the last 12 months |
| Child marriage - n=4,605 |
| Domestic violence - n=3,879 |

Therefore, SPC has designed a grievance redressal mechanism to ensure these issues are taken into consideration during project implementation. A grievance is or concern or complaint raised by beneficiaries of affected communities and stakeholders related to the perceived impact of projects on their lives. The objectives of setting up an appropriate grievance redressal mechanism (GRM) are to:

- provide stakeholders with a clear process for providing comment and raising grievances and concerns in an anonymous manner;
- streamline the process for receiving, recording comments, responses, and grievances in a timely manner; and
- ensure that comments, responses, and grievances are handled in a fair and transparent manner, in line with local and national policies.

The GRM can serve as an effective tool for early identification, assessment and resolution of grievances and therefore for strengthening accountability to beneficiaries. The GRM is an important feedback mechanism that can improve project impact and respond to concerns and grievances raised by stakeholders. It also provides a platform for the dissemination of information about the project(s) in a timely manner. With restrictions on movement, it is important that, where possible, staff managing grievances can access systems remotely to enable GCFM processes to be conducted efficiently.

The GRM will provide an opportunity for beneficiaries and other stakeholders to share their views about the project’s activities, to specifically address gender-based violence (GBV) and other cross-cutting issues.

All grievances will be clearly monitored by the Accredited Entity to assess the number and type of grievances and evaluate any trends over time. This will be conducted by the relevant responsible parties as highlighted under SPC's policies for accountability[2]. All monitoring and reporting will be carried out conforming to confidentiality and consent from aggrieved parties or survivors, where applicable. All information will be reported in line with GCF as imposed through the Accreditation Master Agreement and Funded Activity Agreement.

### 7.1 GCF Grievance Redress Mechanism

Paragraph 69 of the Governing Instrument of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) requires the Board to establish a grievance redress mechanism for all projects and programmes funded by it. The GCF established the IRM through the adoption of the Terms of Reference (ToR) of the IRM which sets out various matters, including the role and functions, governance and administrative arrangements of the IRM. The following are the responsibilities allocated to carry out the following functions:

- Requesting reconsideration for a project or programme that has been denied funding by the Board and, as appropriate, make recommendations to the Board.
- Address grievances or complaints by a person, group of persons or community who/which have been or may be adversely impacted by a GCF funded project or programme through problem solving and/or compliance review, as appropriate.

### 7.2 Grievance related to Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and/or harassment

In all situations involving complaints related to gender-based violence (GBV), sexual exploitation, abuse or harassment (SEAH), violence against children (VAC) and human trafficking (HT), the relevant grievance procedures (Annex B 1.3) shall be applied in accordance with this document. This will apply to all grievances addressed through controlled SP or the PAU. In line with this approach, the following principles will be systemically applied through all steps and actions:

- The safety of the survivor and wishes of the survivor (or victim) is the foremost priority of everyone involved in the project.
- The survivor has a right to:
  - o be treated with dignity and respect instead of being exposed to victim-blaming attitudes.
  - o choose the course of action in dealing with the violence instead of feeling powerless.
  - o privacy and confidentiality instead of exposure.
  - o non-discrimination instead of discrimination based on gender, age, race/ethnicity, ability, sexual orientation or status or any other characteristic.
  - o receive comprehensive information to help her or him make their own decision instead of being told what to do.

- The safety of the survivor shall always be ensured. Potential risks to the survivor will be identified and action taken to ensure the survivor's safety and to prevent further harm including ensuring that the alleged perpetrator does not have contact with the survivor.

If the survivor is an employee of the Project, reasonable adjustments may be made to the survivor's work schedule and work environment to ensure their safety.

* All actions should reflect the choices of the survivor.
* All information related to the case must be kept confidential and identities protected. Only those who have a role in the response to an allegation should receive case-level information. This includes the need for a clear chain of custody for all documents and reports. This applies to any documentation or reports related to the case. Identities will not be revealed unless explicit written consent is provided by the survivor.
* The survivor must provide informed consent to progress with each stage of the complaints process. Survivors may withdraw their consent at any time during the process.

In the event that a case of SEAR or GBV is submitted, SPC as the Accredited Entity will carry out the necessary actions in accordance with the guidelines and procedures where relevant, support for the provision of medical services (including psychosocial support), legal counsel, community driven protection measures, and reintegration of the survivor. This will be carried out in a timely manner to ensure maximum safety and support is provided to the survivor.

## 7.3 SPC's Grievance Redress Mechanism

SPC has a Grievance and Redress Mechanism (GRM) on place to ensure that complaints are being processed promptly, fairly, and transparently by the relevant division. This process aims to address complaints from affected stakeholders, including communities, about the social and/or environmental performance of the project, and to take measures to redress the situation where necessary. The GRM is designed to provide a simple and efficient mechanism for ensuring that SPC has such a mechanism established, and how they can access it to set their grievances.

The SPC GRM is operated through a web-hosted page on SPC site for the expression of concerns or complaints, which can be posted by email with the information in using the complainants' template. The complaints will be reviewed and assessed by the relevant division, which is primarily to the division in charge of the project or to relevant division. Grievances will be sorted out through a conflict resolution process. In case this process is not functional, other process will be used such as a compliance system, the overall objective being to address and redress project stakeholders' grievances in a simple and efficient manner.

## Project-level Grievance Redress Mechanism

Through a project-level GBA, SPC will receive concerns or grievances from an affected community during the implementation of the project plan at a period of time in the project. In that direction, communities and stakeholders will be sensitized about the existing grievance process and form. Both national level and provincial level government agencies will be responsible for supporting the project to ensure that the GBA is implemented as per the guidelines provided by SPC letter. The national level and provincial level government agencies are taking part into the grievance and redress mechanism through documenting grievances and coordinating with SPC, the project team, and other stakeholders in order to resolve issues or settle project-related grievances.

1. Bring up the complaint during the meetings of the PHRC or community awareness meetings. The complainant then will be directed to the project GCF focal point who will then forward to the SPC legal team.
2. Contact by email the Project Management Unit.
3. Contact by email the key project institution (DwWR), which will then forward to SPC.
4. Email SPC through the online process: https://www.spc.int/accountability.
5. Email SPC through the hotline: 1800 267 900.

The Project Management Unit will receive and register grievances and will contact SPC legal team. He/she will provide an initial response within two business days to the person who submitted a complaint. The initial response will be recorded in the SPC GBA, which is then logged onto the SPC GBA. As a first timeframe, a response will be provided to the complainant within a two-month period, with indication of appropriate process to address the grievance. This duration may vary depending on the complexity and nature of the complaint. The SPC legal team will screen for eligibility as well as assign organizational responsibility for proposing a response. This process will possibly involve engaging with other project stakeholders to resolve the issue.

SPC is responsible to inform the complainant that he/she has the right to pursue other options to resolve the concern if unsatisfied through the SPC GBA process, noting that the GBA may result in no action from the complainant, but does not preclude the complainant from escalating to the project-level GBA only if deemed appropriate. This will also ensure that, within the Indigenous communities being targeted, the project benefits from active, traditional mechanisms of conflict resolution and decision-making processes.

## Community-level Grievance Redress Mechanism

At the community level in Vanuatu, concerns or grievances can be addressed through the traditional governance structures and processes managed by the chiefly systems of individual Islands. In the absence of a formal grievance mechanism at the community level, especially among villagers, complaints from marginalized gender or vulnerable groups, issues related to water access points and gender-based violence. This level of the GBA will ensure that communities are sensitized about the existing grievance process and form. Complaints can escalate to the project-level GBA only if deemed appropriate. This will also ensure that, within the Indigenous communities being targeted, the project benefits from active, traditional mechanisms of conflict resolution and decision-making processes.

# Annex B - Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan

The nakamal or Village Council is made up of chiefs and community leaders of a particular village. This authority is convened by the paramount chief or a designated customary leader and it deliberates and resolves matters at the specific village level which could include family matters, disputes between individuals or groups within the village.

The Ward Council of Chiefs sits above the Nakamal or Village Council and comprises chiefs and customary leaders from several villages. It deals with matters that are not resolved within a designated Ward Council. The Ward Council deals mostly with land ownership disputes. Matters unresolved at the Ward Council are elevated to the Area Council of Chiefs or even higher to the national government level.

If an individual or group of individuals are aggrieved, their grievance can be raised for redress at the Nakamal or Village Council. If matters are not able to be resolved at this level, the paramount chief or a designated customary leader may:

1. elevate the grievance for redress at the Ward Council or with the Chief; or,
2. register the grievance directly with the representatives of the provincial authority for redress.

Matters raised with the representatives of the provincial authority are usually done through Area Administrators or Area Secretaries. These provincial officers then have the option to raise the issues further:

- table the grievance for redress at the Provincial Area Council level through the Area Technical Advisory Committee (Area-TAC);
- table the grievance for redress directly through the Provincial Technical Advisory Commission (PTAC); and,
- raise the grievance directly with the relevant national government representative present at the provincial level.

If and when the grievance is raised through the provincial institutional arrangements, the matter can then be elevated to the national government level for redress by the relevant government agency or ministry.

8. Key recommendations

Despite protracted gender issues, as identified in the gender assessment above, Vanuatu has made important gains in ensuring gender mainstreaming in climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction. This is registered in the Government's communications on the implementation of the Gender Action Plan (GAP) and the National Climate Change Strategy.

Additionally, the DoWA reviewed national policies (such as draft climate change/disaster risk reduction policies, the National Sustainable Development Plan 2016–2030) to find that programming for gender equality is now more integrated into these policy documents. This includes Minister's Paper (decision 94/2017) advocating for robust gender-responsive planning and budgeting in five key ministries (Agriculture, Climate Change, Education, Internal Affairs, Lands) in the next three years.

Micro-studies focused on community research has also registered a positive trend of increased climate change and gender awareness. Among the Nakanamanga-speaking peoples of Tonga Island Indigenous concerns about self-reliance and livelihoods, cultural continuity and climate impacts are increasingly being recognized as important for women's empowerment and political autonomy rather than continuation of patrilineal customary laws. This includes safeguarding and reviving of traditional knowledge, while allowing for greater collective governance by including marginalized groups in decision-making.

Gender equality, therefore, has gained progressive priority in the Government of Vanuatu’s. This has also been the case with CGI’s, SPC’s, and other Pacific-focused agencies’ portfolios where gender is mainstreamed in an effort towards implementing a holistic social development mandate. The CGI Gender Action Plan (GAP) is one such example that has been developed through international conversation on gender mainstreaming in climate change adaptation efforts. If implemented effectively, this project has the potential to become a best practice gender mainstreaming model for other Pacific Island countries including Vanuatu, in the Pacific Islands regionally as well as in SIDS and LDC ex-LDC contexts globally.

Given mounting evidence that dynamic, long-term planning for climate change impacts and external shocks yield better results when inclusive, gender-responsive and stakeholder-friendly, this CGI project is being developed through a participatory approach during its project preparation stage. Key recommendations of the Gender Assessment are as follows (the Gender Action Plan in Section 8 reffies these into the project’s Logical Framework - available as Section E in the Funding Proposal):

# Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan

## OUTCOME 2: Communities have enhanced climate-resilient rural water infrastructure

### IMPACT STATEMENT

Through Outcome 1., the current total of DWSPs delivered (277, according to latest DoWR data) will be scaled up by almost 100% (270 more - 120 new identified and 150 existing communities). This will result in an additional 390,000 people being provided with improved water supply. Communities will therefore receive climate-resilient system improvements along with training to maintain new WASH infrastructure.

### GENDER IMPACT STATEMENT

Improved climate-resilient WASH infrastructure delivered to 270 communities through the DWSP process is expected to have gender transformative effects, including increased access to safe drinking water and sanitation for women. Trainings imparted to community members will include 50-50 male and female participation to ensure both gender groups can operate and maintain new systems. The overall effect of improved water supply and WASH infrastructure will address water insecurity among communities and vulnerable sub-groups within them.

### OUTPUTS

* Output 2.: 270 vulnerable communities supported to construct, operate and maintain climate-resilient water infrastructure

# OUTCOME 3: Provincial and national institutions are strengthened to address climate risks associated with water security

## IMPACT STATEMENT
Through Outcome 3, Institutional capacities and processes toward climate-resilient water security for rural communities, particularly at the national and provincial level, will be improved. This will result in greater institutional capacity and sub-activities under this Outcome will lead to greater technology deployment, dissemination and innovation transfer benefiting the whole rural population.

### GENDER IMPACT STATEMENT
Institutional and regulatory barriers in Vanuatu will be addressed—particularly the limited financial and technical capacity that restricts the DoWt and DWSP from shifting their focus toward gender mainstreaming. This will result in greater institutional capacity for climate-resilient water management, and men and women benefit from improved water security and safety, but also that national and provincial staff are sensitized to gender barriers, while HEL and MEL data outputs important gender experience on the field for improving project delivery, and a suite of planning.

## OUTPUTS
* Output 3.1: National- and provincial-level staff and WASH sector partners trained on climate-resilient water management
* Output 3.2: Gender mainstreaming entry points identified
* Output 3.3: Monitoring, learning and evaluation framework established for improved learning for climate-resilient water management

# Annex A: Implementing roles and responsibilities for gender mainstreaming

## Gender Officer in the Project Management Unit:
An International Gender Officer will be hired on a part-time basis to increase the capacity of the PMU and provide technical assistance to deliver the planned activities of the Gender Action Plan. They will take the lead in supporting the DWGSP and CAP design processes (including ESS related training, gender analysis, and gender mainstreaming). The Gender Officer will also be responsible for implementing the Gender Action Plan (GAP) and its associated GAAP. They will support the development of gender and WASH training modules and protocols including ESS through Outcome 1 and 3, and ensure rapid gender assessments provide inputs to project design and implementation. The Gender Officer will work closely with the PMU and other relevant units in the project team. They will also support the MEL Officer in conducting regular monitoring and evaluation of project implementation against the EMIP and GAF. The Gender Officer will review and provide feedback on draft project documents, including ESS, to ensure that gender is mainstreamed into all aspects of the project design and implementation.

In case that any issues are identified, the Gender Officer will draft technical recommendations to address these issues in their implementation. These will be reviewed by the EE and where needed supported by technical assistance through supervision costs. If technical enhancements are required to address gender related issues through improvements in project design or implementation, they should be included in the project budget.

## Department of Water Resources:
The DWR will be the co-executing Entity of the project. The Department will oversee the registration of DWGSP and ensure that 40% representation of women is ensured in that process. Since the DoWR owns the DWGSP process, it will take the lead role in delivering the co-benefit identified for the project.

## Department of Women's Affairs:
The DoWA of the Vanuatu Ministry of Justice and Community Services will play a facilitating role as required, particularly in the awareness raising activities to ensure the active participation of women in community-based water management bodies as well as to ensure community well-being and acceptance of such planned activities of the project.

## Gender and Protection Cluster:
The formation of the Gender & Protection Cluster in Vanuatu on 13 March 2014 coincided with the Tropical Cyclone (TC) Lusi hitting Vanuatu. The response was the first time that gender and protection considered as part of post-disaster assessment and response phases. In the light of the experience gained during this period, the Gender & Protection Cluster has been established to ensure that gender and protection are mainstreamed into all aspects of disaster risk reduction (DRR) and NDMO's coordination structure. The Gender & Protection Cluster contributes to improving preparedness for responding to natural disasters in a gender-and-protection-sensitive manner by ensuring that women and girls' needs, rights, and capacities are considered at every stage.

The Gender & Protection Cluster comprises of representatives of National Ministries, UN Agencies, International and National NGOs, Women's Organization, the International Federation of the Red Cross, the Vanuatu Red Cross Society, and other Organizations with a focus on protection. The DoWA is the designated Lead Agency of the Vanuatu Gender & Protection Cluster.

# Annex B: Gender questionnaire for field missions

## Introduction and background
The Green Climate Fund (GCF) has enlisted E Co., under the project preparation framework agreement to support the Pacific Community (SPC) to assist with the development of the full Funding Proposal (FP) package for the project titled: Enhancing Adaptation and Community Resilience by Improving Water Security in Vanuatu. The project is expected to be delivered in 2019, and will focus on improving water security through improved access to safe drinking water infrastructure and built environment.

You are invited to complete this Gender Assessment and Action Plan part of the project, and insights into Vanuatu’s water sector and climate change adaptation actions. The delivery of the FP is expected by the end of this year, post which iterative interaction is expected from the GCF to finalise the project for the GCP approval process. The project will be implemented in 2019-2023.

### Outcome 1: Evidence-based planning and decision-making for climate-resilient water management at the community level
Outcome 1.1: Community-level awareness, capacity, and skills of communities & local councils on climate-resilient water management increased

Outcome 1.2: Vulnerable communities have implemented no low-cost activities identified in their DWSPPs

### Outcome 2: Climate change considerations incorporated into engineering standards and training
Outcome 2.1: Climate change considerations incorporated into engineering standards and training

### Outcome 3: Institutional strengthening at provincial and national levels to better address climate risks associated with water security
Outcome 3.1: Increased capacity of Provincial DWSPP staff, and WASH sector partners in integrated climate-resilient water management activities

### Outcome 4: Monitoring, Evaluation and Learning integrated into the National Implementation Plan for Safe and Secure Water to ensure continued climate resilience
Outcome 4.1: Climate change considerations incorporated into engineering standards and training

We need your valuable insight to gauge how the project can be designed to be of relevance at the community and national levels; how better improve the project’s effectiveness; and create pathway for potential sustainability. Therefore, suggestions for improvements or other comments will be sought and may be incorporated into future work undertaken by this project, as well. If you have any additional comments or questions, please contact Deborauna.cabral@apeccgroup.com.

(b) Has an institutional audit been done to identify gaps in lead ministry capacity and practice (DWR and other relevant institutions), in responding to gender issues?
